Foreword
On behalf of the Ministry of Health and Medical Services, it is my great
pleasure to introduce the Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual. This manual
is a milestone in our commitment to improving the quality and accessibility
of healthcare across Kiribati, ensuring that all I-Kiribati have access to safe,
effective, and culturally appropriate care.
The development of this manual has been a collaborative effort, drawing
on local expertise and the valuable knowledge and resources shared
through partnerships with international organizations. It will serve as an
essential tool for healthcare workers across our nation, empowering them
to deliver care that meets the highest standards of clinical practice.
The Kiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual provides comprehensive
guidance on managing medical conditions, responding to emergencies,
promoting health and well-being, and enhancing the patient experience.
It also emphasizes the importance of community-based care, with a focus
on health promotion and prevention.
As we continue to address the health challenges faced by our
communities, this manual will play a key role in strengthening our
healthcare system, improving health outcomes, and enhancing the lives
of all those we serve. The Ministry remains committed to supporting the
health workforce and ensuring that our services meet the diverse needs of
the I-Kiribati population.
I extend my gratitude to all those who have contributed to the creation
of this valuable resource and to all healthcare workers who will use it to
provide exceptional care. Together, we are building a healthier future for
Kiribati.
Mr. Michael Bootii Nauan
Minister for Health
Republic of Kiribati

Source attribution and adaptation notice
Except where specifically identified as Kiribati Original Protocols or from
the Kiribati Health Workers Manual (Green Book)  the clinical protocols in
this Manual are adapted from the Queensland Primary Clinical Care Manual
(PCCM) published by the State of Queensland (Queensland Health) and
the Royal Flying Doctor Service (Queensland Section). The adaptations
were undertaken by the Ministry of Health and Medical Services (MHMS),
Government of Kiribati, and reviewed by MHMS clinical experts, to ensure
relevance and compliance for Kiribati. Changes include (but are not limited
to) adjustments to:
ΓÇó	 reflect the unique cultural context and health-system setting of Kiribati
and its people;
ΓÇó	 align with MHMS policies and procedures and applicable national
regulations;
ΓÇó	 reflect local medicine supply chains, the Kiribati pharmacy formulary/
essential medicines list, and other operational constraints; and
ΓÇó	 incorporate local clinical guidelines, including the Kiribati Antibiotic
Guidelines (Second edition, 2022) and any subsequent updates.

Introduction

Introduction
This manual has been designed to serve as a comprehensive guide for
healthcare providers, including nurses, medical assistants, and allied
health professionals, working within KiribatiΓÇÖs Primary Health Care (PHC)
system. It offers essential clinical information, guidelines, protocols, and
best practices for managing common health conditions, diseases, and
emergency situations, ensuring that healthcare providers can deliver
quality care at the community level. The manual will support healthcare
workers in conducting high-level patient assessments, managing
medications, and following evidence-based protocols that align with
international and local healthcare standards.
The primary purpose of this manual is to empower healthcare providers
with the necessary knowledge and resources to deliver safe, effective,
and patient-centered care. It is intended to be a practical tool that can
be referred to on a daily basis, supporting clinical decision-making and
facilitating consistent, high-quality healthcare delivery across all health
centers and outreach services in Kiribati.
Structure of the Manual
The manual is organized into clear chapters based on the most common
health issues encountered in primary care settings in Kiribati. Each chapter
includes:
ΓÇó	 Overview of the health condition or issue ΓÇô A concise explanation of the
disease or condition, its prevalence, and its impact on public health in
Kiribati.
ΓÇó	 Diagnosis and clinical assessment ΓÇô Key diagnostic criteria, assessment
techniques, and patient history gathering to make an accurate
diagnosis.
ΓÇó	 Management and treatment protocols ΓÇô Evidence-based
recommendations for managing the condition, including
pharmacological and non-pharmacological treatment options.
ΓÇó	 Prevention and health promotion ΓÇô Guidance on how to prevent the
condition, including community-based prevention strategies and
education.
ΓÇó	 Referral guidelines ΓÇô When and how to refer patients for further
diagnostic testing, specialist care, or hospital-based treatment.
2ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Each chapter also highlights important considerations such as patient
safety, cultural factors, and common challenges in the Kiribati context.
Throughout the manual, clinical flow charts, decision-making algorithms,
and checklists are included to guide healthcare providers through the
diagnostic and treatment process, ensuring that no steps are overlooked.
Integration of the Manual Within the Model of Care
This manual is not an isolated resource; it is an integral part of the broader
healthcare model in Kiribati. The primary goal of the manual is to ensure
that all healthcare providers can confidently and competently deliver
essential services within the PHC framework. This manual complements
the Model of Care by guiding healthcare workers in providing services that
align with national health priorities and international standards.
The Model of Care in Kiribati is based on a comprehensive and patient-
centered approach to healthcare, which includes prevention, diagnosis,
treatment, and follow-up. The manual supports this approach by offering
evidence-based guidelines for managing health conditions at the
community level and ensuring that healthcare providers are well-equipped
to address the most common health issues. It ensures that patients receive
consistent and high-quality care, whether they are in urban or rural areas,
and helps maintain continuity of care by guiding healthcare providers on
follow-up visits and patient education.
Furthermore, the manual's integration with the regulatory and governance
frameworks, such as the Pharmaceuticals Legislation and Professional
Governance, ensures that healthcare providers adhere to the legal and
ethical standards governing medical practice in Kiribati. It also reinforces
the importance of maintaining scope of practice for nurses, medical
assistants, and doctors, ensuring that each provider works within their
professional boundaries, making appropriate referrals, and collaborating
effectively within the healthcare system.
Use of Antibiotics: All antimicrobial prescribing must align with the Kiribati
Antibiotic Guidelines (Second edition, 2022, Ministry of Health and Medical
Services, Government of Kiribati) or the most recent version. Clinicians
should use these Guidelines alongside this Manual to ensure compliance
with national policy and antimicrobial stewardship. Where there is
uncertainty or a deviation from the Guidelines is being considered, seek
advice from MHMS.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ3

The ManualΓÇÖs Role in Education and Training
The manual is also a critical tool for education and training, especially for
nurses, medical assistants, and other healthcare providers who may be
less familiar with advanced medical procedures. It serves as a practical
resource during formal and informal training, allowing trainers to use it
as a reference for case studies, skills-building exercises, and continuing
education. Nurses and medical assistants can learn best practices, clinical
guidelines, and patient management techniques that will improve their
clinical competencies, helping them to undertake high-level patient
assessments and interventions with greater confidence.
For those involved in capacity building and training, the manual should
be used as a reference point in teaching clinical skills and protocols,
ensuring that PHC workers have a standard and consistent approach to
care. This also allows for continuous improvement as health conditions
and treatment guidelines evolve, ensuring that healthcare workers stay
updated with the latest evidence and standards of care.
Feedback and Continuous Improvement
To ensure that the manual remains a living document that evolves with the
needs of the healthcare system, healthcare providers are encouraged to
provide feedback on the manualΓÇÖs usability, content, and clinical guidelines.
This feedback will help identify areas for improvement and updates in
future editions, ensuring that the manual remains relevant, practical,
and effective in meeting the healthcare needs of the Kiribati population.
pccmfeedback@mhms.gov.ki
By continuously updating the manual, healthcare providers can stay at the
forefront of care delivery, improving patient outcomes, and contributing to
the strength of the PHC system in Kiribati.

Kiribati PatientΓÇÖs Bill of Rights
1. Karinean inaomatan te aoraki
The right to be treated with Respect and Dignity
2. Te tararua ae nakoroai, rokoroi ao kororoai
The Right to receive Quality and Holistic care
3. Te tararua rinanon te katei
The Right to receive Culturally Safe and Sensitive care
4. Karinean ao Boutokaan inaomatan te aoraki
The Right to receive Informed Consent
5. Kamanoan rongorongon te aoraki
The Right to Confidentiality and Privacy
6. Te katiteboaki
The Right to be Treated Fairly
7. Butimwaen ao rinanoan ana tangtang te aoraki
The Right to Lay a Formal Complaint
8. Te tararua ae tabwannin raoi
The Right to Receive Care that is Central to the Individual, Family or
CommunityΓÇÖs needs
9. Ongorean bwanaan te aoraki
The Right to be Heard
10. Kabobongan ao Kakororaoan raoi te tararua
The Right to Receive Quality Care
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ5

Kiribati Nurses Code of Conduct
The Nursing Council, under the I-Kiribati Medical Services Act (1996), is the
statutory authority that oversees and governs the practices of nursing in
Kiribati. The fundamental purpose of the ΓÇÿActΓÇÖ is to protect the health and
safety of the general public.
The implementation of the inaugural ΓÇÿAnuan Te NeetiΓÇÖ (The Code) is a
vital feature of the ActΓÇÖs commitment to the protection of the public. The
code provides a set of professional standards that nurses must adhere
to in demonstrating their being ΓÇÿfit for practiceΓÇÖ and maintaining their
registration.
The Code evolved during the development of the I-Kiribati Nursing
Cultural Model of Care which was informed through extensive consultation
with the public and the profession. This included a rigorous review of
Kiribati nursing consultation documents and an international literature
review of other codes.
Incorporated within the Anuan te Neeti are I-Kiribati cultural values defined
by the Nursing Council as acceptable professional conduct. To ensure public
confidence and trust, nurses are required to uphold an exceptional standard
of behaviour both in their professional and personal lives.
Anuan te Neeti code outlines the professional behaviour expected of
nurses in all practice settings as set by the Nursing Council under sections
47 & 48 of the Kiribati Medical Services Act (1996). The code is written
with the complete understanding that nursing practice is not limited to the
provision of direct clinical care delivery. Nursing practice settings extend
to all areas of nursing. This includes working in a non-clinical capacity
involving patients or members of the public, or any other role where the
integrity of the profession is upheld.
Anuan te Neeti can be utilized by:
ΓÇó	 Patients or service users, families or public, employers, nurses,
professional organizations, and the statutory body (Nursing Council) as
a guide to evaluate the conduct of nurses.
6ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Failure to uphold these professional standards of behavior, may result in a
disciplinary inquiry. The term ΓÇÿpatientΓÇÖ has been incorporated throughout
this document to align accordingly with the Medical Services Act (1996).
Contextually, the term ΓÇÿpatientΓÇÖ refers to the individuals, families, groups
and communities who are the recipient of nursing care (1996 Medical
Services Act, I,p.44). While mandatory language such as ΓÇÿmustΓÇÖ, ΓÇÿshallΓÇÖ and
ΓÇÿwillΓÇÖ is not sought throughout this code, it is an expectation that nurses
understand the foundational principles of the code and how it impacts on
their professional standing.
Please note: The terms conduct and behaviour are one and the same,
hence have been used inter-changeably throughout this document.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ7

10 principles of nursing including standards are provided in the code.
These characteristics are fundamental to I-Kiribati cultural values and are
in harmony with each other.
The term ΓÇÿtrustΓÇÖ has been used deliberately as an entry-point to defining
the 10 principles. This is because the first step to achieving successful
partnerships of any kind, is in the patient trusting that the nurse has their
best interests at heart. This is the Kiribati way:
Principle 1: Kateimatoaon kamanoan kakawakii aoraki
Translation: Maintain patient trust by protecting their dignity.
Principle 2: Kateimatoaen te reitaki ae tamaroa rinanon te makuri;
Translation: Maintain patient trust by working in partnership
Principle 3: Kateimatoaan ao uarokoan makuri ni kuakua rinanon te katei
ao te aroaro.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by providing culturally responsive care
Principle 4: Kateimatoaan ao kamanoan rongorongoia ma tararuakiia
aoraki.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by ensuring privacy and confidentiality
Principle 5: Kateimatoaen ao kanakoroaon te kuakua ae oioi iaoiia ke
mairouii aoraki.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by the provision of patient-centred care
Principle 6: Kateimatoaan te tararua ae manoroai, nakoroai ao n rokoroi.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by providing safe and competent care
Principle 7: Kateimatoaan onimakinan te makuri ni kuakua ao n tararua
iroiiia Neeti.
Translation: Maintain patient trust in the nursing profession
Principle 8: Kateimatoaan onimakinan karinean inaomatan te aoraki.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by providing informed consent
Principle 9: Kateimatoaan karinean te reitaki ae tamaroa imarenaia Neeti
ao raoia ni beku.
Translation: Maintain patient trust through respectful relationships with
colleagues
Principle 10: Kateimatoaen te onimakinaki rinanon kakaraon te koaua, te
eti ao te riai.
Translation: Maintain patient trust by working with integrity and honesty
8ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

The Nursing Cultural Framework
(Abridged Version)
Four key objectives underpin the Cultural Framework:
1.	 Ara Katei / Culture
2.	 Aoraki ma aia utu / Patients as consumers of Health Services and their
family
3.	 Te bonnano / Unity
4.	 Karikirakean te rabakau n te mwakuri / Professional Development
Vision
A healthy and prosperous I-Kiribati through quality and responsive nursing
workforce founded on unique cultural needs and values.
Philosophy
The cultural framework will serve as the ΓÇÿroadmapΓÇÖ and also a ΓÇÿkey driverΓÇÖ
for I-Kiribati Nurses in ensuring optimal health and safety for patients
which is an inclusive term for (individuals, families and communities) as
well as the safety for the profession.
The Philosophy is grounded on I-Kiribati cultural needs and values; to
ensure public confidence and trust, nurses and/or midwives are required
to uphold a high standard of conduct or behaviour in their personal and
professional lives.
Boua / Five posts
Symbolises the holistic nursing care delivered, incorporating contextually
the five cornerstones of health:
FIVE CORNERSTONES OF HEALTH
I-KIRIBATI TRANSLATION
Spiritual Aspects
Te Tamnei
Psychological Aspects
Te Iango
Cultural Aspects
Te Katei
Physiological Aspects
Te Rabwata
Social/other
Te Reitaki

Maneaba: I-Kiribati Nursing Model of Care
Maneaba means accommodating and welcoming: A safe place to rest, to
meet or get together or take refuge.
Historically, the maneaba was considered a meeting place for villages/
island to congregate and make important decisions. Customarily, subject
to context e.g. church gatherings/tribal affiliations or individual status,
special seating allocations within the maneaba are adhered to accordingly.
Traditional beliefs suggests that the Maneaba had been ΓÇÿpurposefullyΓÇÖ built
with a lowly-positioned roof to serve as a constant reminder to I-Kiribati
that on entering the dwelling, they must always bow or kneel to show
respect for their Unimwane & Unaine / Elders. This was also symbolic of
acknowledging their ancestral gods, prior to the arrival of Christianity on
their shores.
In present-day, although many members of the Kiribati community have
adapted to Western ideology, traditional values and beliefs are still
maintained. Hence, the symbolic gesture in the use of the Maneaba as a
cultural framework for nursing is befitting.
This is likened to other Polynesian conceptual frameworks where
traditional thatched or flax-woven shelters have been utilised
metaphorically to depict the significance of culture in their care delivery.
10ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Kiribati Decision-Making Framework
(Abridged Version)
The Kiribati Decision-Making Framework (KDMF) provides a clear and
consistent process for nurses and medical assistants to determine whether
a clinical activity should be undertaken. This summary outlines the key
principles only. All clinicians must refer to the full KDMF for detailed
requirements, examples, and procedural steps.
Purpose
Clinical decision-making is a core professional responsibility. In Kiribati,
where many clinicians work with limited resources or in remote outer
island settings, decisions about treatment, medication administration,
delegation, and escalation must be safe, lawful, and accountable. The
KDMF supports clinicians by providing a structured approach to these
decisions, ensuring consistent and patient-centred care across all facilities.
The Framework is grounded in the ICN Definition of ΓÇÿNursingΓÇÖ and of ΓÇÿa
NurseΓÇÖ (2026).
Why the Framework Is Needed
KiribatiΓÇÖs health system requires a structured decision support process
because of:
ΓÇó	 Geographical isolation and limited access to higher-level services
ΓÇó	 Resource constraints that affect what can be done safely in each setting
ΓÇó	 Multiple cadres working together across facilities and islands
ΓÇó	 Legal requirements governing the use of medicines
ΓÇó	 Ongoing system development, including new PCCM interventions
The KDMF ensures that decisions are consistent, safe, and legally
defensible in all contexts.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ11

Foundations of Safe Decision-Making
The KDMF is based on three domains that must all be met before any
activity is undertaken:
1.	 Regulation and Legislation: activities must comply with the Legislation
(ie: Medicines Act 2018), MHMS policy, and professional regulation and
standards.
2.	 Professional Competence: clinicians must be ecducated / trained,
competent, and have recency of practice.
3.	 Context of Practice: decisions must reflect local resources, supervision,
staffing, and referral pathways.
Only when all three domains are satisfied is an activity considered within
safe scope of practice.
Guiding Principles
The Framework is underpinned by the following principles:
ΓÇó	 Patient safety comes first in all decisions.
ΓÇó	 Practice must be lawful and within authorised scope.
ΓÇó	 Accountability is individualΓÇöeach clinician is responsible for their own
decisions.
ΓÇó	 Competence and recency are essential for safe practice.
ΓÇó	 Context determines safety, particularly in remote or resource-limited
settings.
ΓÇó	 Collaboration with colleagues strengthens decision-making.
ΓÇó	 Alignment with professional identity, consistent with the ICN Definitions.

Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ13

Supervision and Delegation
Supervision and delegation are integral to scope of practice in Kiribati.
Registered nurses remain accountable for decisions to delegate and must
ensure the delegatee is authorised, competent, and supported. Supervision
may be direct, indirect, or remote, depending on the complexity of the
activity and the competence of the clinician involved. These processes
support patient safety and help build workforce capacity.
Expanding Scope of Practice
Scope of practice is dynamic. Expansion must occur through a structured
and governed process, including legal review, MHMS endorsement,
education and training, supervised practice, competence assessment, and
ongoing monitoring. Any new activity added to scope must remain lawful,
supported, and in the best interests of patients.
This summary is for quick reference only. All nurses and medical assistants
must refer to the full Kiribati Decision-Making Framework for complete
guidance, detailed explanations, and examples.
Statutory Authority to Use Medicines
Medicines Act 2018, Part 4
This manual must be read together with the Kiribati Medicines Act 2018
(Part 4, ss.17ΓÇô20). The manual does not create new authorities; it guides
practice within the authorities below and within each practitionerΓÇÖs scope,
competence and local governance.
14ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Who may prescribe:
ΓÇó	 Medical practitioners may prescribe any scheduled medicine for persons
under their care.
ΓÇó	 Dentists may prescribe Level D medicines for persons under their care.
ΓÇó	 No other cadre has statutory prescribing authority under the Act
(veterinary prescribers are limited to Level V for animals).
Who may supply / dispense / administer:
ΓÇó	 Pharmacists may obtain, compound, prepare, store, distribute, supply or
dispense any medicine on the Register of Medicines.
ΓÇó	 Medical practitioners may administer any medicine on the Register for
persons under their care.
ΓÇó	 Nurses may dispense and administer Level 1 and Level 2 medicines for
persons under their care.
ΓÇó	 Medical assistants, and nurses supervised by a medical assistant, may
dispense and administer Level 1, Level 2 and Level 2B medicines for
persons under their care.
Using PCCM protocols
ΓÇó	 Protocols list recommended medicines and doses. You may only carry out
the parts of a protocol that fall within your statutory authority and scope.
ΓÇó	 Where a protocol includes a medicine above your authorised level (e.g.,
Level 2B when you are a nurse not under medical-assistant supervision,
or any medicine requiring a prescription), a medical practitioner (or
dentist for Level D) must prescribe and, where applicable, administer.
ΓÇó	 All actions must be for particular persons under your care, documented,
and undertaken with required supervision, competence and local
approvals.
Medicine schedules noted in protocols
ΓÇó	 Schedules referenced in protocols (Level 1, Level 2, Level 2B, Level D,
Level V) must be applied as per ss.18ΓÇô19 and the Register of Medicines.
ΓÇó	 The Pharmacy team will provide the authoritative schedule status for
each medicine; the manual will reflect these once confirmed. Until then,
check the Register of Medicines or consult MHMS Pharmacy before
proceeding.

1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Acute pain
2.	 Immediate management

ΓÇô
contact MO urgently

ΓÇô
adult - insert IVC + give IV morphine or fentanyl
ΓÇó	 If IVC delay, consider alternative routes e.g. IM/subcut
ΓÇó	 If severe pain + looks sick - screen for Sepsis
ΓÇó	 If sudden onset severe headache/ΓÇÿworst headache of lifeΓÇÖ - go to Headache
ΓÇó	 If abdominal pain still give analgesia. Note: will not mask physical signs/hinder
diagnosis
3.	 Clinical assessment1,2
ΓÇó	 The most useful diagnostic approach to acute pain is to take a detailed and
systematic history

ΓÇô

ΓÇô

ΓÇô
Associated symptoms eg nausea, vomiting, sweating, fever

ΓÇô

	ΓÇ║
has anything changed the pain
	ΓÇ║
ever had this pain before, how often does it occur

ΓÇô
Exacerbating or relieving factors:

ΓÇô
eg rest, medicines, eating, position changes, ice/splinting

ΓÇô
Severity - at rest, on movement:
	ΓÇ║
mild, moderate, severe
	ΓÇ║
scale from 0ΓÇô10 (0 = no pain, 10 = worst pain imaginable)
	ΓÇ║
if child consider FLACC or FACES

renal, heart or liver failure, coagulation disorders
Management of associated emergency: Consult MO. See Anaphylaxis7

Nausea and vomiting - Adult/Child
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Always consider life-threatening causes eg bowel obstruction, mesenteric

1.

ALERT suspect Button battery, in all children if vomiting blood. A button battery
lodged in the oesophagus can burn a hole through to the aorta causing catastrophic
haemorrhage

Check for red flags - if any contact MO urgently 2,3

child

46ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Emergency

Basic life support (BLS) - Adult/Child
DRSABCD resuscitation, the collapsed patient
Recommend 1-4
ΓÇó	 Good quality CPR and reducing time to defibrillation are the highest priorities in
resuscitation from sudden cardiac arrest
ΓÇó	 Airway takes precedent over any injury, including possibility of spinal injury -
handle gently with no twisting or bending of spinal column or neck1
ΓÇó	 If pregnant, once CPR in progress put padding such as a towel under the
womanΓÇÖs right hip to tilt the hips about 15ΓÇô30Γü░, but leave her shoulders flat
ΓÇó	 Child/infant - central pulse palpation may be used to asess circulation. If a pulse
cannot be definitely identified within 10 seconds (or HR &lt; 60 with signs of poor
circulation) start CPR
Background
ΓÇó	 Agonal breaths (occasional irregular gasps) are common in early stages of

May present with 2

ΓÇó	 Unresponsive and absent or abnormal breathing ie gasping
2.	 Immediate management 1-6

ΓÇó	 If trauma related control catastrophic haemorrhage

	ΓÇ║

	ΓÇ║

ΓÇô
Look (movement of chest/abdomen), Listen (for breath sounds), Feel (air on

if unresponsive but breathing normally, see Unconscious patient

ΓÇô
100ΓÇô120 compressions per minute
ΓÇó	 Defibrillation - attach automated external defibrillator (AED) as soon as available
and follow prompts:

ΓÇô
adult and child > 8 use adult pads. Child 1ΓÇô8 use paediatric pads (or adult size
if not available)

ΓÇô

ΓÇô

ΓÇó	 Continue CPR until responsive, normal breathing returns, or MO/SNO /MA

3.	 Clinical assessment - See Immediate management
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Control bleeding, protect from weather + other first aid measures depending on

5.	 Follow up

ΓÇó	 Provide support for family members
ΓÇó	 If required, see Patient death in absence of MA,
6.	 Referral/consultation
Always contact Medical Officer

Intravenous (IV) cannulation
General considerations
Insertion of a cannula into a vein to administer fluids or medications.
ΓÇó	 Common in emergency and elective settings.
ΓÇó	 Requires good knowledge of anatomy, technique, and asepsis
ΓÇó	 It is recommended to choose a straight portion of a vein to minimize the chance
of hitting valves.
ΓÇó	 Use the patientΓÇÖs non┬¡ dominant arm (if possible)
ΓÇó	 For prolonged courses of therapy, it is recommended to start distally and move
proximally as distal catheters are replaced.
ΓÇó	 The superficial veins of the upper extremities are preferred to those of the
lower extremities for peripheral venous access as they interfere less with patient
mobility and pose a lower risk for phlebitis.
ΓÇó	 DO NOT INSERT A CANNULA IN AN ARTERY ΓÇô risk of limb ischemia.
ΓÇó	 In a diabetic patient, avoid lower limb cannulation
Venous access allows:
ΓÇó	 sampling of blood
ΓÇó	 administration of IV fluids and medications
Contraindications
ΓÇó	 No absolute contraindications to intravenous cannulation exist
ΓÇó	 Avoid peripheral venous access in an injured, infected, or burned extremity if
possible
Equipments
ΓÇó	 Non-sterile gloves,
ΓÇó	 Tourniquet,
ΓÇó	 Antiseptic wipes,
ΓÇó	 5-ml syringe,
ΓÇó	 Sterile gauze,
ΓÇó	 Cannula,
ΓÇó	 Saline- flush,
ΓÇó	 Adhesive Plaster
Veins Commonly Used for IV Access:
ΓÇó	 Cephalic vein (thumb side)
ΓÇó	 Basilic vein (little finger side)
ΓÇó	 Median cubital vein (elbow crease)
ΓÇó	 Dorsal veins of the hand

How to insert Arrow EZ-IO┬« device2

Diving accidents and decompression sickness
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Emphasise diver safety.
ΓÇó	 Advise all divers to attend certified safe diving training classes.
ΓÇó	 Visitors should only dive if qualified.
ΓÇó	 There is no hyperbaric chamber in the islands.
Background
Diving-related problems are increasingly common due to air travel and deeper
dives. As depth increases, pressure rises from air and water, leading to increased risk
of barotrauma and gas-related injury. Most underwater deaths are related to diver
inexperience, carelessness, or equipment failure. Key hazards include air embolism,
decompression sickness, and sinus-related injuries due to blocked passages in the
nose, ears, and sinuses.
1.
May present with
Air Embolism
ΓÇó	 Symptoms appear within 2 minutes of ascent
ΓÇó	 Personality changes
ΓÇó	 Seizures
ΓÇó	 Loss of consciousness
ΓÇó	 Neurological deficits (motor or sensory)
ΓÇó	 Pneumothorax
ΓÇó	 Shock
Decompression Sickness
ΓÇó	 Joint pain (ΓÇ£the bendsΓÇ¥) ΓÇô deep aching or throbbing
ΓÇó	 Skin bubbles, mottling, rashes, itching, crawling sensation
ΓÇó	 Neurological symptoms ΓÇô mood change, altered consciousness, seizures
ΓÇó	 Lung symptoms ΓÇô chest pain, cough, dyspnoea, cyanosis, shock
Ear, Nose and Sinus Problems
ΓÇó	 Severe ear or sinus pain
ΓÇó	 Hearing difficulty
ΓÇó	 Nosebleed or blood behind tympanic membrane
ΓÇó	 Vertigo
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ59

2.	 Immediate management
Air Embolism
ΓÇó	 Place patient on left side with head down (to keep bubbles in right side of heart)
ΓÇó	 Protect cervical spine if injury suspected (use collar or stabilisation)
ΓÇó	 Maintain airway
ΓÇó	 Give 100% oxygen at 7ΓÇô8 L/min (continue until exhausted; give air breaks only for
assessment or hydration)
ΓÇó	 Insert IV line and run normal saline rapidly if hypotensive
ΓÇó	 Give IV Valium 5ΓÇô10 mg slowly (diluted to 10 mL with sterile water or saline)
ΓÇó	 If not breathing, check central pulse ΓÇö if absent or doubtful, begin BLS
ΓÇó	 Apply direct pressure to stop any bleeding (do not use tourniquet)
ΓÇó	 Transfer to hyperbaric chamber as soon as possible (delayed therapy can still
help)
ΓÇó	 Use aircraft pressurised or fly at sea-level altitude
Decompression Sickness
ΓÇó	 Manage as for air embolism:
ΓÇó	 Left side, head down
ΓÇó	 100% oxygen at 8ΓÇô10 L/min
ΓÇó	 IV fluids rapidly if BP low
ΓÇó	 Valium IV slowly for seizures
ΓÇó	 Transfer to hyperbaric chamber as soon as possible
Ear, Nose and Sinus Problems
ΓÇó	 Beconase AQ nasal spray: 2 sprays per nostril, twice a day for 3ΓÇô5 days
ΓÇó	 Promethazine (Phenergan) 25 mg orally twice daily for 3ΓÇô5 days
ΓÇó	 If patient has had similar problems in the past:

ΓÇô
Begin above therapy 1 week before diving and continue through diving period
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Full head-to-toe check for signs of gas embolism, sinus trauma, or hypoxia
ΓÇó	 Neurological examination (motor, sensory, behaviour, speech)
ΓÇó	 Chest and lung assessment
ΓÇó	 Ear examination (including tympanic membrane)
ΓÇó	 BP, HR, RR, oxygen saturation

Critical emergencies
Unconscious/altered level of consciousness -
Adult/Child
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Unconsciousness is a medical emergency - early stability + diagnosis are vital1

1.
May present with2
Unconscious/altered level of consciousness (LOC)
2.	 Immediate management2

ΓÇó	 Do NOT leave sitting in a chair or put head between their knees

handle gently, avoid twisting or forward movement of head + spine

use head tilt/chin lift for adult + child > 1 years

ΓÇô
infant - keep head neutral

ΓÇô
GCS - if drops ΓëÑ 2 points urgently contact MO

Shock - Adult/Child
Background
ΓÇó	 Shock is a loss of effective circulation resulting in impaired tissue oxygen +
nutrient delivery and causes life-threatening organ failure. Any seriously ill or
seriously injured patient is at risk of developing shock1
ΓÇó	 Causes include - sepsis, anaphylaxis, major haemorrhage, large fluid loss + rarely
obstruction of cardiac outflow + cardiogenic failure
ΓÇó	 Assume a serious cause until proven otherwise1
1.
May present with1,2
ΓÇó	 Signs of shock:

ΓÇô
∩âó LOC

ΓÇô
∩âó perfusion - mottled or pale skin, cool extremities, capillary refill > 2
seconds ┬▒ clammy

ΓÇô
∩âí HR, ∩âí RR
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 DRSABCD
ΓÇó	 Call for help + urgently contact MO when able
ΓÇó	 If signs of shock + suspected anaphylaxis:

ΓÇô
give IM adrenaline (epinephrine) 1:1,000 (see Anaphylaxis, for doses)

ΓÇô
repeat every 5 minutes if needed
ΓÇó	 Lie the person down, if unconscious place on side1
ΓÇó	 Start resuscitation
ΓÇó	 Give O2 15 L/minute via non-rebreather mask 1,2
ΓÇó	 Insert IVC x 2 eg 18 G or Intraosseous,  if 2 failed attempts
ΓÇó	 Rapidly give bolus IV sodium chloride 0.9%:2-4

ΓÇô
adult 250ΓÇô500 mL, child 20 mL/kg

ΓÇô
reassess + repeat if no improvement in HR, LOC or perfusion
ΓÇó	 If persisting signs of shock after fluid bolus x 2 - urgently contact MO +

ΓÇô
consider Sepsis, if suspected or no other cause of shock identified, initiate
sepsis pathway + give broad spectrum antibiotics2,3

Sepsis/septic shock - Adult/Child
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Sepsis is a medical emergency - early recognition + rapid treatment is imperative
for survival. Consider in every patient with fever or acute illness 1,2
ΓÇó	 Initial presentation can be vague. Always have a high index of suspicion in
neonates, young infants, the elderly and immunocompromised patients
ΓÇó	 If suspected sepsis ΓÇô initiate treatment  + discuss with MA.
ΓÇó	 Sepsis = infection + organ dysfunction 1,2
ΓÇó	 Children can have sepsis with normal BP. Hypotension is a late sign of shock
ΓÇó	 Neonates + infants &lt; 1 year are at highest risk as immature immune systems are
unable to ward off severe infections.
Background
ΓÇó	 Septic shock = shock + infection. Sepsis = infection + organ dysfunction2
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Consider in all patients with acute illness or deterioration who may have an
infection5

dialysis, IV drug use, significant underlying conditions + known to be colonised

unless contraindicated

Meningitis - Adult/Child
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Meningitis is a medical emergency. Early recognition + treatment is imperative1,2
ΓÇó	 If suspected, but lumbar puncture cannot be done Γëñ 30 minutes, give IV
antibiotics1,2
ΓÇó	 Do NOT use hyponatraemic solutions eg glucose 4% with sodium chloride 0.18%
or sodium
ΓÇó	 chloride 0.45% - ∩âí risk of cerebral oedema
ΓÇó	 Suspect meningitis if sick child with no obvious source of infection
ΓÇó	 Parents/carers may notice early, subtle changes in childΓÇÖs LOC - do not ignore
their concerns
ΓÇó	 Meningococcal infection is a notifiable disease ) notify Public Health Unit within
6 hours
Background
ΓÇó	 Children often present with non-specific symptoms and not the classic triad of
fever, headache + neck stiffness2
ΓÇó	 Meningitis is an inflammation or infection of the membranes that surround the
brain + spinal cord. It can lead to serious long-term neurological complications +
death
ΓÇó	 Viral meningitis is more common than bacterial meningitis, but is usually less
severe
1.
May present with1
ΓÇó	 Typically presents with acute history of fever, neck stiffness + altered conscious
state ┬▒

ΓÇô
headache, photophobia + nausea or vomiting - often prominent1

ΓÇô
petechial rash - very late sign + indicative of meningococcal sepsis

ΓÇô
focal neurological deficit, seizures, shock
ΓÇó	 Older children may present with any combination of the above2┬▒ ΓÇô upper or
lower respiratory tract symptoms, myalgia + abdominal pain
ΓÇó	 &lt; 3 months - bulging fontanelle, high pitched cry, poor feeding, apnoea, seizures,
vomiting2

immunisations - Hib, meningococcal, pneumococcal + check vaccination

ΓÇô
prior use of oral antibiotics (may modify clinical features)
ΓÇó	 Check for risk factors:2

ΓÇô
recent contact with a case of bacterial meningitis (especially in family)

ΓÇô
recent contact with HSV ΓÇÿcold soresΓÇÖ or confirmed enterovirus infection

ΓÇô
recent overseas travel

ΓÇô
maternal Group B Strep (GBS) colonisation if &lt; 3 months

ΓÇô
immunocompromised

ΓÇô
recent neurosurgical procedure, penetrating head injury, ventriculoperitoneal
(VP) shunt,cochlear implant
ΓÇó	 Weight - bare weight if &lt; 2 years
ΓÇó	 Do physical examination, including:

ΓÇô
listen to chest for air entry - any crackles, wheeze

ΓÇô
skin for rashes including at pressure points + under nappies/clothing:
	ΓÇ║
petechiae or purpura (do not fade on pressure)
	ΓÇ║
note: if child, rash may not appear until child is rehydrated2

ΓÇô
Hydration assessment - adult, or child

ΓÇô
ENT

ΓÇô
fontanelle in infants - any fullness, bulging

ΓÇô
neck stiffness:
	ΓÇ║
with patient lying down, put hand behind head + gently raise
	ΓÇ║
note: may not complain of neck stiffness, but with passive or active flexion
- cannot touch chin to chest

ΓÇô
+ve BrudzinskiΓÇÖs sign - reflex flexion of the hip + knee when the neck is
passively flexed OR

ΓÇô
+ve KernigΓÇÖs sign - pain along spinal cord ┬▒ resistance to knee extension when
hip and knee are flexed to 90┬░
	ΓÇ║
may indicate bacterial meningitis2

ΓÇó	 Consult the Kiribati Antibiotic Guideline 2022.
ΓÇó	 Careful management of fluid + electrolyte balance is important - discuss with

ΓÇó	 Monitor closely until evacuation:

ΓÇô
vital signs + BGL

ΓÇô
give analgesia + antiemetic as needed

ΓÇô
fluid balance
ΓÇó	 Check timing of further antibiotic doses if evacuation is delayed/prolonged -
contact with MO

Choking (foreign body airway obstruction) -
Adult/Child
Background
ΓÇó	 Abdominal thrusts (Heimlich maneuver) are not recommeded1
ΓÇó	 Consider foreign body aspiration in any toddler / infant with acute onset
respiratory distress2
1.
May present with 1,2
ΓÇó	 Coughing
ΓÇó	 Clutching the neck
ΓÇó	 Extreme anxiety, agitation, gasping sounds
ΓÇó	 Loss of voice, hoarseness, stridor
ΓÇó	 Cyanosis, collapse
2.	 Immediate management 1-3
ΓÇó	 Assess for effective cough
Effective cough (mild airway obstruction)
ΓÇó	 Give reassurance
ΓÇó	 Encourage coughing until foreign body is expelled
ΓÇó	 Allow to position themselves
ΓÇó	 Do not do back blows or chest thrusts while there is an effective cough
ΓÇó	 Continue to check until recovery or deterioration
Ineffective cough and conscious (severe airway obstruction)
ΓÇó	 Call for help
ΓÇó	 Do up to 5 sharp back blows:

ΓÇô
use the heel of your hand in the middle of the back between the shoulder
blades

ΓÇô
if infant - place in a head downwards position ie across your lap
ΓÇó	 If back blows unsuccessful, do up to 5 chest thrusts - like chest compressions for
CPR but sharper and delivered at a slower rate:

ΓÇô
infant - place face up, back across your thigh

ΓÇô
child/adult - sitting or standing position. Support the patientΓÇÖs back when
doing thrusts:4
	ΓÇ║
put your other hand on the patientΓÇÖs back OR on the back of the chair (if
patient sitting) OR
	ΓÇ║
get someone to stand behind patient to support the back OR
	ΓÇ║
stand patient against a firm surface/wall OR
	ΓÇ║
lie patient down
84ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

After each back blow or chest thrust, check to see if obstruction relieved
Aim is to relieve the obstruction rather than give all 5 blows/thrusts
ΓÇó	 If obstruction still not relieved and patient remains responsive:

ΓÇô
continue alternating 5 back blows with 5 chest thrusts
Unconscious or becomes unresponsive1
ΓÇó	 Call for help
ΓÇó	 If solid material is visible in the mouth - remove
ΓÇó	 Start CPR. See BLS
ΓÇó	 Urgently contact MO
3.	 Clinical assessment1
ΓÇó	 Get rapid history as able:

ΓÇô
circumstances leading to choking eg eating, drinking, other
ΓÇó	 Do vital signs
ΓÇó	 Inspect chest for expansion, drawing in of spaces between ribs and clavicles
ΓÇó	 Listen to the chest for air entry and added sounds (crackles or wheeze)
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 In cases of near (severe) choking or unseen positional foreign object:

ΓÇô
consult MO urgently

ΓÇô
give O2 to maintain SpO2 ΓëÑ 94%

ΓÇô
prepare for evacuation

ΓÇô
monitor vital signs

ΓÇô
If the choking episode is minor and the foreign body has been dislodged and
removed:

ΓÇô
if asymptomatic and chest findings normal, the patient can go home after a
period of observation

ΓÇô
if any concerns, contact MO

ΓÇô
If choking as a result of a button battery, see Button battery
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 If discharged, advise to be reviewed the next day:

ΓÇô
consult MO if the patient has any symptoms eg ∩âí HR, ∩âí T or any chest finding
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Consult MO on all occasions of severe choking

Button battery ingestion/insertion -
Adult/Child
Recommend 1,2
ΓÇó	 A suspected or proven battery ingestion is a time critical emergency - urgent
x-ray needed
ΓÇó	 Suspect button battery ingestion in all children &lt; 5 years of age with non-specific
symptoms
Background 1,2
ΓÇó	 Button (disc) batteries cause burns resulting in life-threatening injuries
ΓÇó	 Failure to remove from the oesophagus within 2 hours can lead to perforation of
the
ΓÇó	 oesophagus, fistula (eg oesophageal-aortal/tracheal) and catastrophic
haemorrhage
ΓÇó	 Oral honey may slow the development of a chemical burn for a swallowed button
battery3
1.
May present with 1,2,4
ΓÇó	 Battery missing
ΓÇó	 Seen to have been playing with battery
ΓÇó	 Self reported by child (older)
ΓÇó	 Gagging, gulp, cough or choking episode:

ΓÇô
may be overheard when child swallows battery rather than directly observed
ΓÇó	 Child may deny ingestion
ΓÇó	 Very non-specific symptoms:

ΓÇô
unexplained partial food refusal - may still take soft food/fluids

ΓÇô
drooling or regurgitation

ΓÇô
croup like cough

ΓÇô
chest pain

ΓÇô
upper GI bleeding (melaena/black stools; haematemesis - may mimic
epistaxis):

ΓÇô
may be small bleed initially, then catastrophic bleed

ΓÇô
fever/vomiting/signs of infection without a clear focus

ΓÇô
pain/grunting

ΓÇô
Bloody discharge from ear, nose, vagina, rectum - may have inserted battery
88ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 If inhaled, see Choking
ΓÇó	 If haematemesis or melaena:

ΓÇô
insert IVC x 2

ΓÇô
get rapid history

ΓÇô
urgently consult MO
ΓÇó	 Do vital signs
If ingestion (swallowed) suspected
ΓÇó	 Urgently contact MO:

ΓÇô
if no x-ray facilities, we will need urgent evacuation for x-ray
ΓÇó	 If the x-ray shows battery is located in the hypopharynx (bottom of throat) or
oesophagus:5

ΓÇô
URGENT evacuation for removal via endoscopy required
ΓÇó	 Give oral honey if ingestion suspected &lt; 12 hours and:3,6

ΓÇô
child ΓëÑ 12 months old

ΓÇô
able to swallow

ΓÇô
give 10 mL (2 teaspoons) every 10 minutes, up to 6 doses (or as guided by
MO)
ΓÇó	 keep nil by mouth otherwise
ΓÇó	 If the battery is in the stomach or beyond:2

ΓÇô
management will depend on factors such as age of child, symptoms, size of
battery, time of suspected ingestion

ΓÇô
MO will advise
ΓÇó	 Monitor vital signs
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Ask about - if known:

ΓÇô
timing of ingestion

ΓÇô
size of battery (if ΓëÑ 2 cm, more likely to lodge in oesophagus)

ΓÇô
was ingestion witnessed

ΓÇô
what treatment (if any) has been tried at home

ΓÇô
colour of stools, any blood in vomit, or any other signs/symptoms
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Continue management of suspected swallowed battery in collaboration with MO
ΓÇó	 If insertion suspected eg into vagina, ear, rectum, nose:

ΓÇô
consult MO regarding urgent removal

ΓÇô
x-ray may be required if unexplained nasal/ear/vaginal discharge and unable
to visualise cause1
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ89

5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 As advised by MO. Note: even after removal of a button battery, ongoing damage
can occur7
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Always consult MO

Anaphylaxis - Adult/Child
Recommend1
ΓÇó	 Adrenaline (epinephrine) is first line treatment. Give without delay
ΓÇó	 Anaphylaxis is potentially life-threatening and must be treated as a medical
emergency
ΓÇó	 Check for medic alert jewellery and accessories in emergency situations.2 May
look like normal
ΓÇó	 jewellery or other accessory eg key ring, USB stick, shoe tag, anklet, watch or
tattoo
ΓÇó	 Fluid resuscitation is important if severe anaphylaxis with hypotension
1.
May present with1
ΓÇó	 ANY ONE of the following (onset can range from minutes to hours after
exposure to a substance):

ΓÇô
difficult/noisy breathing

ΓÇô
swelling of tongue

ΓÇô
swelling/tightness in throat

ΓÇô
difficulty talking ┬▒ hoarse voice

ΓÇô
wheeze or persistent cough - usually sudden

ΓÇô
persistent dizziness or collapse

ΓÇô
pale and floppy (young children)

ΓÇô
abdominal pain, vomiting - for insect sting or injected drug (medication)
allergy
ΓÇó	 CONSIDER anaphylaxis in ANY acute onset:

ΓÇô
illness with typical skin features - itchy rash or red/flushing ┬▒ angio-oedema
(swelling) PLUS involvement of respiratory ┬▒ cardiovascular ┬▒ persistent
severe GI symptoms

ΓÇô
OR hypotension or bronchospasm or upper airway obstruction where
anaphylaxis is considered possible, even if typical skin features are not present
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ91

2.	 Immediate management1
Also see flowchart inside back cover
ΓÇó	 Remove allergen if still present - do not delay adrenaline (epinephrine) to do this:

ΓÇô
stop infusion of medicine/blood product

ΓÇô
flick out insect stings; freeze tick with liquid nitrogen or ether containing spray
ΓÇó	 Call for help
ΓÇó	 Lay patient flat. Do NOT allow to stand or walk - can result in fatal hypotension

ΓÇô
if breathing difficult allow to sit - with legs outstretched in front (not in chair):
	ΓÇ║
lay flat again if ∩âóBP or ∩âóLOC or confusion

ΓÇô
do not hold infants upright

ΓÇô
if pregnant, put in left lateral position
ΓÇó	 Give IM adrenaline (epinephrine) into OUTER MID-THIGH WITHOUT DELAY

ΓÇô
repeat adrenaline (epinephrine) every 5 minutes as needed

ΓÇô
if known asthmatic with allergy give adrenaline (epinephrine) first, then
asthma reliever if sudden breathing difficulty (wheeze, persistent cough or
hoarse voice) - even if no skin symptoms
ΓÇó	 Urgently consult MO
92ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

vital signs

for altered LOC and confusion. See GCS/AVPU

+ insert another IVC

3.	 Clinical assessment1
ΓÇó	 Get rapid history - from patient, relatives or friends:

food, medicine, sting/bite, herbal medicines, other exposures in the previous
6ΓÇô8 hours

ΓÇô
known allergies and reaction

hypertension or pulmonary oedema

4.	 Management1
When stable
ΓÇó	 Monitor closely for at least 4 hours after last dose of adrenaline (epinephrine):

relapse/protracted ┬▒ biphasic (two phase) reactions may occur

presents for medical care late in evening
ΓÇó	 If not evacuated, continue to manage as per MO instructions

Fitting - convulsions, seizures ΓÇô Adult/Child
Recommend 1,2
ΓÇó	 Status epilepticus is continuous seizure activity or repeated seizures without full
recovery tonormal LOC between episodes - neurological emergency, aim to stop
seizure urgently1,2
Background
ΓÇó	 Most fits are brief + end within 1ΓÇô3 minutes without needing midazolam2
ΓÇó	 Febrile convulsions occur with rapid ∩âí T in early acute illness in child aged
6 monthsΓÇô6 years1
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Fitting eg:1,3

ΓÇô
sudden body stiffening, will fall if standing

ΓÇô
jerking movements, head arching back

ΓÇô
shallow breathing or may stop temporarily

ΓÇô
dribbling, blood stained if tongue bitten

ΓÇô
┬▒ fever in child
ΓÇó	 Pregnant + fitting, see Preeclampsia, for magnesium sulphate infusion + ongoing
management
ΓÇó	 Post seizure - drowsy, confused, incontinent, agitated, noisy breathing if partial
airway obstruction3
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Fitting:2,3

ΓÇô
protect patientΓÇÖs head + avoid restraining unless essential to avoid injury

ΓÇô
lay down + turn on the side when practical

ΓÇô
note the time

ΓÇô
maintain airway - do not force mouth open or attempt to insert object

ΓÇô
insert IVC if possible

ΓÇô
do BGL - if &lt; 4 adult, &lt; 3 child - treat Hypoglycaemia, concurrently

ΓÇô
contact MO urgently
ΓÇó	 Status epilepticus:1,2

ΓÇô
if fitting > 5 minutes or repeated seizures without full recovery to normal LOC

ΓÇô
give  Midazolam or Diazepam

ΓÇô

Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) - Adult/Child
Hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state (HHS)
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Can be rapidly fatal. Avoid delays to treatment - IV fluids, potassium replacement
+ IV insulin 1,2
ΓÇó	 Be aware - excessive thirst + frequent urination can be a sign of undiagnosed
diabetes
Background
ΓÇó	 DKA - BGL > 11 + acidosis, sudden onset 1,3
ΓÇó	 HHS - BGL > 33.3 + profound dehydration without ketosis - gradual onset,
commonly caused by
ΓÇó	 acute illness 1
1.
May present with4
ΓÇó	 ∩âí BGL
ΓÇó	 Rapid breathing (Kussmaul breathing)
ΓÇó	 Excessive thirst, dehydration
ΓÇó	 Abdominal pain ┬▒ vomiting
ΓÇó	 Fruity sweet smelling breath (acetone - similar to paint thinner or nail polish
remover)
ΓÇó	 Confusion, ∩âóLOC or unresponsive
ΓÇó	 Note: DKA occurs mainly in patients with type 1 diabetes. It is also seen in
patients with type 2
ΓÇó	 diabetes, particularly if taking sodium-glucose co-transporter 2 (SGLT2)
inhibitors, when DKA may
ΓÇó	 occur without hyperglycaemia5

adult 1ooo mL/hour, repeat if systolic BP &lt; 100

ΓÇô
child only give on MO order

ΓÇó	 Start fluid balance

ΓÇó	 Do neurological observations - excessive fluids can cause cerebral oedema eg
headache, ∩âóLOC, agitation/aggression.

Hypoglycaemia - Adult/Child
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Hypoglycaemia is a medical emergency. If left untreated it can cause convulsions,
irreversible
ΓÇó	 brain damage + death1
Background
ΓÇó	 Most common cause in children is ketotic hypoglycaemia (KH), a physiological
condition that isa variant of normal + expected in a fasting state. Most outgrow
KH by mid - late primary school1
ΓÇó	 For known diabetics, hypoglycaemia may be secondary to insulin use
1.
May present with 2,3
ΓÇó	 BGL &lt; 4 adult, &lt; 3 child
ΓÇó	 Sweating, pale skin, shaking, palpitations, feeling anxious
ΓÇó	 Hunger, difficulty concentrating, confusion/inappropriate behaviour
ΓÇó	 Loss of consciousness, seizures
ΓÇó	 Child - drowsy, listless + lethargic1
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Do BGL - rapidly assess + manage concurrently - avoid delays
ALERT if hazardous alcohol use or severely malnourished 3,4
ΓÇó	 Conscious patients:

ΓÇô
Children: a teaspoon of powdered sugar in a few ml of water or 50 ml of fruit
juice, maternal or therapeutic milk or 10 ml/kg of 10% glucose by oral route or
nasogastric tube.

ΓÇô
Adults: 15 to 20 g of sugar (3 or 4 cubes) or sugar water, fruit juice, soda, etc.
Symptoms improve approximately 15 minutes after taking sugar by oral route.
ΓÇó	 Patients with impaired consciousness or prolonged convulsions:

ΓÇô
Children: 2 ml/kg of 10% glucose by slow IV (2 to 3 minutes)

ΓÇô
Adults: 1 ml/kg of 50% glucose by slow IV (3 to 5 minutes)
Neurological symptoms improve a few minutes after the injection.
Check blood glucose after 15 minutes. If it is still low, re-administer glucose by IV
route or sugar by oral route according to the patientΓÇÖs clinical condition.
If there is no clinical improvement, differential diagnoses should be considered:
e.g. serious infection (meningitis, etc.), epilepsy, unintentional alcohol intoxication or
adrenal insufficiency in children.
In all cases, after stabilisation, give a meal or snack rich in complex carbohydrates
and monitor the patients for a few hours.
If patient does not return to full alertness after an episode of severe hypoglycaemia,
monitor blood glucose levels regularly.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ105

Preparation: Adult dilute in 100 mL sodium chloride 0.9%. Child dilute 5 mL

previous ICU/hospital admission(s) for asthma
4.	 Management1
ΓÇó	 Continue to manage severe and life-threatening asthma, or mild asthma that is
not responding, in collaboration with MO
In all cases
ΓÇó	 Repeatedly assess response to treatment + vital signs - in particular fatigue, LOC,
RR, HR, SpO2

ΓÇó	 MO may consider retrieval/hospitalisation if any of:1

ΓÇô
hypoxia at presentation

ΓÇô
abnormal spirometry (if done) 1ΓÇô2 hours after presentation

ΓÇô
respiratory distress, ΓåæWOB unresolved or unable to lie flat without SOB 1ΓÇô2
hours after presentation

ΓÇô
history of ICU admission for asthma; or (if child) hospitalised in prior 12
months

ΓÇô
acute asthma in past 4 weeks, or frequent presentations for acute asthma

ΓÇô
high recent use of salbutamol/other beta2 agonists

ΓÇô
patient cannot be monitored well at home or cannot return easily

ΓÇô
other risk factors eg poor asthma control, chronic lung disease, cardiovascular
disease:
	ΓÇ║
+ if child - confirmed food allergy, incorrect inhaler technique, poor
adherence to preventer/asthma action plan, exposure to tobacco smoke/
clinically relevant allergens
Within 1st hour start corticosteroids - all presentations, even mild

ΓÇô
if oral route not possible, IV hydrocortisone OR IV/IM dexamethasone (child)

1 hour after starting salbutamol - reassess response to treatment
ΓÇó	 Use assessment table in Step 3
ΓÇó	 Do spirometry if available, patient able, and staff trained
ΓÇó	 If adult - check for SOB while lying flat
If still not improving
ΓÇó	 If persisting respiratory distress OR ΓåæWOB OR not able to lie flat without SOB:

ΓÇô
urgently contact MO

ΓÇô
continue salbutamol every 20 minutes

ΓÇô
MO may consider add-on treatments eg IV magnesium sulfate

ΓÇô
retrieval/hospitalisation

Poisoning and overdose ΓÇô Adult/Child
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Manage all suspected poisoning or overdose urgently.
ΓÇó	 Contact the Medical Officer (MO) early.
Background
ΓÇó	 Poisoning and overdose can result from accidental or deliberate ingestion,
inhalation, injection, or dermal exposure to a wide range of substances. Early
recognition and appropriate supportive care are essential. Avoid gastrointestinal
decontamination (e.g. activated charcoal) until toxicology assessment is
completed.
1.
May present with┬╣-Γü┤
ΓÇó	 Confusion, drowsiness, loss of consciousness, fitting
ΓÇó	 Respiratory failure
ΓÇó	 Hyperthermia or hypothermia
ΓÇó	 Nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea
ΓÇó	 Hypotension, hypertension, bradycardia, tachycardia, arrhythmias
ΓÇó	 Conscious and fully orientated
ΓÇó	 History suggestive of deliberate or accidental poisoning or drug taking
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 DRSABCD. Resuscitate if required. See BLS
ΓÇó	 Contact MA urgently
ΓÇó	 Insert IVC ├ù 2 if required
ΓÇó	 If contamination suspected:

ΓÇô
Remove all clothes

ΓÇô
Shower with soap and water

ΓÇô
Use PPE for staff safety
ΓÇó	 If patient is confused, withdrawn, aggressive, or acutely disturbed, prioritise
safety. See Mental health emergency
ΓÇó	 Do not undertake gastrointestinal decontamination until full toxicology
assessment has been completed
3.	 Clinical assessment┬╣-Γü┤
ΓÇó	 Perform a full Toxicology assessment ΓÇô see next table
ΓÇó	 Monitor and record vital signs:

ΓÇô
BP, HR, RR, T

ΓÇô
SpOΓéé

ΓÇô
BGL

ΓÇô
GCS or AVPU scale,

ΓÇô
Note: hyperthermia can be life-threatening
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ119

Toxicology Assessment┬╣
CATEGORY
DETAILS
AGENT
ΓÇó	 Name of product(s), active ingredients, manufacturer
ΓÇó	 Look for container if possible
ΓÇó	 Ask relatives or witnesses
ΓÇó	 Ask if alcohol was taken concurrently (affects toxicity)
ΓÇó	 Ask if paracetamol or other OTC products were taken
ROUTE OF
EXPOSURE
ΓÇó	 Oral, topical, eye, inhaled, injected
DOSE
ΓÇó	 Estimate maximal dose by subtracting remaining tablets/
liquid from full bottle/pack
ΓÇó	 Always consider the worst-case scenario
ΓÇó	 Was the substance diluted (e.g. insecticides)?
ΓÇó	 For frequent substance use:

ΓÇô
frequency

ΓÇô
duration and pattern of use

ΓÇô
time and amount of last use

ΓÇô
average daily consumption
TIME OF
EXPOSURE
ΓÇó	 Exact time
INTENT OF
EXPOSURE
ΓÇó	 Accidental or deliberate
ΓÇó	 If deliberate, See Mental health emergency
ACTION SINCE
EXPOSURE
ΓÇó	 Skin washed, eyes irrigated, self-induced vomiting, drank
milk, etc.
PATIENT
FACTORS
ΓÇó	 Age, weight, gender, comorbidities ΓÇô including mental
health issues
CLINICAL
COURSE
ΓÇó	 What symptoms have occurred since exposure to poison/
medicine
CLINICAL
STATUS
ΓÇó	 BP, HR, RR, T, SpOΓéé, BGL, conscious state
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Consult MA urgently
ΓÇó	 Following stabilisation, provide supportive care and monitoring
ΓÇó	 Use poison-specific treatments where indicated (see tables below)
Toxin Specific Management Considerations
Extra management considerations after:
ΓÇó	 Contact with Poisons Information Centre (PIC) 13 11 26
ΓÇó	 MO consultation
ΓÇó	 Toxicology assessment

Fish poisoning (Ciguatera)
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Educate patients about the risks of eating reef fish from areas known for
ciguatera contamination.
ΓÇó	 Check with local authorities to determine which fish in your area present the
highest risk.
ΓÇó	 Early management includes supportive care and symptomatic treatment for the
neurological effects of poisoning.
ΓÇó	 Advise avoidance of any further consumption of contaminated fish.
Background
Ciguatera fish poisoning is caused by eating reef fish that have accumulated toxins
produced by marine dinoflagellates. These toxins are commonly found in large reef
fish such as barracuda, grouper, and snapper.
The illness may present with gastrointestinal and neurological symptoms within 1 to
24 hours of consumption.
Suspected case: One or more of nausea, vomiting, or diarrhoea, and neurological
signs within 24 hours of eating reef fish.
Confirmed case: Diagnosis is usually clinical but may be confirmed by detection of
ciguatoxin in the consumed fish using approved testing methods.
Incubation period: Less than 1 hour to 24 hours.
Notify and investigate: One suspected case with classic neurological symptoms
should trigger public health notification and investigation.
1.
May present with
SYMPTOM GROUP
SYMPTOMS
NOTES
Gastrointestinal
ΓÇó	 Nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea,
abdominal cramps
ΓÇó	 Occur early and
usually resolve
within 12 hours
Neurological
ΓÇó	 Tingling or numbness of
mouth/lips, tongue, throat,
fingers, extremities
ΓÇó	 Burning sensation on contact
with cold water
ΓÇó	 Itchiness, muscle pain, ataxia,

ΓÇó	 Symptoms usually
develop over 24

Other

ΓÇó	 May persist for
weeks to months
130ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Provide symptomatic treatment:

ΓÇô
Give antiemetics for nausea

ΓÇô
Give fluids for dehydration and shock

ΓÇô
Give paracetamol for pain relief
ΓÇó	 No specific antidote available
ΓÇó	 Treat supportively based on severity of symptoms
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Check for dehydration and neurological symptoms (e.g. tingling, temperature
reversal)
ΓÇó	 Measure vital signs including BP, HR, and respiratory rate
ΓÇó	 Assess for signs of severe poisoning such as breathing difficulty or paralysis
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Encourage frequent small sips of ORS, hot sweetened tea, fresh toddy or
moimoto to maintain hydration
ΓÇó	 If unable to tolerate oral fluids, give IV fluids (refer to diarrhoea/vomiting and
dehydration protocol)
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Review the patient within 24ΓÇô48 hours
ΓÇó	 Monitor for prolonged neurological symptoms ΓÇô may persist for weeks or months
ΓÇó	 Advise to remain well hydrated
ΓÇó	 Avoid physical exertion
ΓÇó	 Avoid eating all fish (including freshwater), alcohol, caffeine, pork, and chicken
during recovery
6.	 Referral / consultation
ΓÇó	 Refer to a medical officer urgently if:

ΓÇô
Difficulty breathing

ΓÇô
Severe or progressive neurological symptoms

ΓÇô
Signs of shock or paralysis

Drowning/submersion - Adult/Child
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Ventilation + oxygenation to reverse hypoxia
ΓÇó	 Active rewarming is not routinely recommended. May lead to rapid overshoot of
core T
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Immersion ┬▒ vomiting, respiratory compromise, apnoea or in cardiac arrest 1,3
ΓÇó	 Suspect spinal injury if history indicates eg dived into shallow water, found near
dumping surf, rocks or after boating accident2
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Rapidly assess + manage concurrently1
ΓÇó	 DRSABCD + BLS, as needed. Note: if hypothermia do prolonged CPR
ΓÇó	 Assess patient on back with head + body at same level:

ΓÇô
not head down - risk of regurgitation + vomiting2

ΓÇô
only roll on side if airway obstructed, reassess condition after rolling2

ΓÇô
c-spine immobilisation, if spinal injury suspected
ΓÇó	 If breathing, place on side + open airway eg head tilt2
ΓÇó	 Do:

ΓÇô
vital signs + GCS

ΓÇô
BGL
ΓÇó	 Consult MO urgently
ΓÇó	 If SpO2 &lt; 95% + respiratory compromise eg ∩âí WOB:1

ΓÇô
give O2 to maintain SpO2 ΓëÑ 95% in adults + children

ΓÇô
if unable to maintain SpO2 with O2 + adequate conscious state (GCS 13ΓÇô15):
	ΓÇ║
Refer to the main hospital. Remove wet clothing, dry + keep warm
with blankets2

witnesses

ΓÇô
keep warm

ΓÇô
vital signs + strict fluid balance

ΓÇô
observe for 4ΓÇô8 hours before considering discharge in collaboration with MO1,3

Acute coronary syndrome (ACS) - Adult
Possible cardiac chest pain, unstable angina,
myocardial infarction
Recommend 1,2
ΓÇó	 Evacuation/treatment of ACS is time critical Use cardiac clinical pathways.
Ensure they are readily available 2
ΓÇó	 https://clinicalexcellence.qld.gov.au/resources/clinical-pathways/cardiac-
clinicalpathways Suspected acute coronary syndrome clinical pathway
ΓÇó	 Acute coronary syndrome clinical pathway
ΓÇó	 Thrombolysis for STEMI clinical pathway
ΓÇó	 Elevated troponin alone (without cardiac symptoms) should not trigger the
urgent treatment of
ΓÇó	 ACS. Troponin may be elevated in many conditions, including sepsis3
ΓÇó	 and PE.1 Urgently consult
ΓÇó	 MO in all cases
Background1
ΓÇó	 ACS includes myocardial infarction (MI) or unstable angina:
ΓÇó	 MI can be ST elevation (STEMI) or non-ST elevation (NSTEMI)
ΓÇó	 patients without ST elevation are initially described as having NSTEACS (non-ST
elevation
ΓÇó	 acute coronary syndrome) until investigated further
ΓÇó	 Reperfusion is restoring the flow of blood to the heart

Acute pulmonary oedema
(Cardiac causes) - Adult
Recommend 1
ΓÇó	 Acute pulmonary oedema (APO) is a medical emergency. It is the rapid
accumulation of fluid in the alveoli from the pulmonary circulation. Fluid impairs
gas exchange and decreases lung compliance leading to difficulty breathing and
hypoxia
Background
ΓÇó	 Causes can be ACS, valve dysfunction. Note: can be non-cardiac causes
eg sepsis, drowning
1.
May present with 1,2
ΓÇó	 Sudden onset of extreme breathlessness. May wake patient up at night
ΓÇó	 Anxiety/agitation
ΓÇó	 Pale/ashen, sweaty
ΓÇó	 Lung crackles
ΓÇó	 Cough (pink frothy sputum in severe cases)
ΓÇó	 Chest pain
ΓÇó	 Cyanosis (late sign), exhaustion, altered LOC
ΓÇó	 Tripod position (sit/standing leaning forward, supporting upper body with hands
on knees/other
ΓÇó	 surface, indicating respiratory distress)
ΓÇó	 Oedema of legs/sacrum and an enlarged liver may co-exist as a sign of right
heart failure
2.	 Immediate management 1,2
ΓÇó	 DRSABCD. Be prepared for rapid deterioration ┬▒ cardiorespiratory arrest
ΓÇó	 Call for help
ΓÇó	 Sit patient as upright as possible - do not lay them down
ΓÇó	 Consult MO urgently + urgent evacuation

ΓÇô
Do vital signs +

ΓÇô
capillary refill time

ΓÇô
LOC. See GCS/AVPU

ΓÇô
continuous cardiac monitoring
ΓÇó	 Give O2 if SpO2 &lt; 94%. Titrate O2 to SpO2
	ΓÇ║
use with caution if COPD. Aim for SpO2 88ΓÇô92%
ΓÇó	 Insert IVC x 2 eg 16 G
ΓÇó	 Take bloods : FBC (Full Blood Count), UECr (Urea, Electrolytes [Na+, K+, Cl-],
Creatinine) & Coagulation Profile
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ149

subling glyceryl trinitrate (GTN) providing sBP > 110. Give 2ΓÇô5 minutely being
guided by clinical response AND

ΓÇô
V furosemide (frusemide)
ΓÇó	 Do ECG - review by MO within 10 minutes
3.	 Clinical assessment 1,2
ΓÇó	 Get rapid history, including:
ΓÇó	 Allergies
ΓÇó	 Medicines - are they taken correctly
ΓÇó	 Past history:

ΓÇô
heart problems eg angina, MI, heart failure, palpitations, RHD, arrhythmias

ΓÇô
hypertension, renal failure, COPD, diabetes

ΓÇô
alcohol/drug use

ΓÇô
recent infection

ΓÇô
currently pregnant2

onset, any chest pain

ΓÇô
other signs/symptoms

ΓÇô
recent ∩âí weight

ΓÇô

ΓÇô
weight (if able)

forward)

4.	 Management 1,2
ΓÇó	 MO may order:

ΓÇô
GTN infusion - titrate to BP and clinical effect:
	ΓÇ║

ΓÇô
if infusion not practical in the rural/remote area, GTN transdermal patch(es)3

ΓÇô
+ other treatment specific to cause (if known)2

airway, LOC, vital signs, SpO2

ΓÇô
cardiac monitoring

ΓÇô
symptoms

ΓÇô
urine output. IDC not routinely recommended. Do not lie patient down in APO

ΓÇó	 Non invasive ventilation ΓÇô Via  Airvo2

Arrhytmias ΓÇô Adult
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Good quality CPR and reducing time to defibrillation are the highest priorities in
resuscitation from sudden cardiac arrest
ΓÇó	 Airway takes precedent over any injury, including possibility of spinal injury -
handle gently with no twisting or bending of spinal column or neck1
ΓÇó	 If pregnant, once CPR in progress put padding such as a towel under the
womanΓÇÖs right hip to tilt the hips about 15ΓÇô30Γü░, but leave her shoulders flat
ΓÇó	 Child/infant - central pulse palpation may be used to asess circulation. If a pulse
cannot be definitely identified within 10 seconds (or HR &lt; 60 with signs of poor
circulation) start CPR
Background
ΓÇó	 Agonal breaths (occasional irregular gasps) are common in early stages of
cardiac arrest
1.
May present with2
ΓÇó	 Fast, slow or irregular pulse
ΓÇó	 ┬▒ symptoms eg:

ΓÇô
palpitations, sensation of ΓÇÿmissed beatsΓÇÖ

ΓÇô
SOB, fatigue, dizziness, light-headedness, syncope/fainting, non-accidental
falls

ΓÇô
chest pain/discomfort, hypotension/shock
2.	 Immediate management2
ΓÇó	 DRSABCD
ΓÇó	 If needed see BLS, and ALS.
ΓÇó	 If chest pain go to Chest pain assessment, and ACS
ΓÇó	 Vital signs
ΓÇó	 ECG3 - to be reviewed by MO promptly - within 10 minutes if symptomatic

ΓÇô
if difficult to interpret, MO may send to cardiologist using ECG Flash if
available. See https://qheps.health.qld.gov.au/caru/networks/cardiac/ecg-flash
ΓÇó	 Attach cardiac monitor
ΓÇó	 Give O2 if needed to maintain SpO2 ΓëÑ 94%
ΓÇó	 Insert IVC x 2
ΓÇó	 Take bloods:
ΓÇó	 FBC, UE, troponin ┬▒ VBG3
156ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

If unstable/deteriorating urgently consult MO2 for urgent evacuation +

ΓÇô
Look for reversible causes eg ACS, AF, hypothermia, hypovolaemic shock,
electrolyte

ΓÇô
abnormalities, hypoxaemia, infection, acidosis, hypothyroidism

ΓÇô
If tachyarrhythmia - MO will advise management
ΓÇó	 If bradyarrhythmia - MO will advise. May include:3

ΓÇô

ΓÇô
medication (depending on cause) eg:3
	ΓÇ║
atropine (for vasovagal bradycardia), or
	ΓÇ║
other medication(s) if drug toxicity
	ΓÇ║
external cardiac pacing if deteriorates/haemodynamically unstable - as per

ΓÇô
onset, associated symptoms

ΓÇô
frequency, timing, duration, severity, longevity, circumstances, triggers,
alleviating factors

ΓÇô

standing, shaving, tight collar, head turning)

ΓÇô
previous episode(s)

ΓÇô
medications

ΓÇô
recent drug/alcohol use

ΓÇô
cardiovascular disease, renal disease

ΓÇô
risk factors for cardiovascular disease eg hypertension, diabetes,
hyperlipidaemia, smoking,

ΓÇô
family history
ΓÇó	 Listen to the chest for air entry + added sounds - crackles or wheeze
ΓÇó	 Any oedema - check legs/ankles + if able sacrum (with patient sitting up and
forward)
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Consult MO/MA for all arrhythmias, who may advise:

ΓÇô
further investigation and management of any underlying cause

ΓÇô
referral to cardiologist

ΓÇô
evacuation/hospitalisation if required

Electrocution/electric shock - Adult/Child
1.
May present with1
ΓÇó	 Electrical exposure
ΓÇó	 Lightning strike
ΓÇó	 Trauma, burns
ΓÇó	 Cardiac arrest
ΓÇó	 Conducted energy weapons eg Taser┬« - negligible risk of injury in healthy person
2.	 Immediate management1
ΓÇó	 Call 194 immediately and provide Situation Report if patient unconscious or still
in contact with electrical source
High voltage from powerlines, train lines, industrial generators - HIGH DANGER
ΓÇó	 Voltage from several 1,000 V to 200,000 V possible
ΓÇó	 High danger for rescuers to get electrocuted - without perceiving they are close
to the source
ΓÇó	 Stay away at a safe distance - at least 1 m per 10,000 V or as directed by
emergency services:

ΓÇô
high voltage can cause instant death if a flash/arcing occurs

ΓÇô
electricity can travel through wet ground or air for many metres
ΓÇó	 Call the electricity provider/railway company. If unknown call ooo to get
support
ΓÇó	 Do not get closer until disconnection of power is confirmed and the line is
grounded on both sides (high voltage electrician, fire and rescue or SES can
provide this, not healthcare providers)
Do NOT approach patient until SAFE
Household electricity
ΓÇó	 Electrical source must be turned off and protected against it being switched
on again:

ΓÇô
ensure person or lock is at the fuse box where electricity has been switched
off OR power plug pulled and loose end taken with the rescuer
ΓÇó	 If not possible, ask for support to remove electrical cables from the patient
using dry timber or rubber (non conducting). This task is risky and should
ideally be performed by an electrician, fire and rescue person or SES - not
healthcare providers
158ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

ΓÇó	 DRSCABCD as per Traumatic injuries
ΓÇó	 If in cardiac arrest see BLS, Do prolonged CPR - high likelihood of recovery
ΓÇó	 Do:

ΓÇô
vital signs + continuous cardiac monitoring

ΓÇô
ECG

ΓÇô
insert IVC
ΓÇó	 If hypotensive - suspect internal injury:

ΓÇô
give fluid bolus of sodium chloride 0.9% or HartmannΓÇÖs 10ΓÇô20 mL/kg

ΓÇô
repeat as advised by MO
ΓÇó	 Urgently consult MO for all electrocutions
3.	 Clinical assessment1
ΓÇó	 Ask patient/witness:

ΓÇô
type of electrical exposure + voltage if known

ΓÇô
other circumstances of injury eg fall, in water

ΓÇô
loss of consciousness or seizures
ΓÇó	 Head to toe assessment as per EFGHIJ in Traumatic injuries
ΓÇó	 Look for entry and exit wound
ΓÇó	 Urinalysis - check for blood
4.	 Management1
ΓÇó	 MO will guide management
ΓÇó	 Evacuation if:

ΓÇô
high voltage exposure

ΓÇô
loss of consciousness, seizures or any other symptoms

ΓÇô
entry and exit wound present - suspect serious deep tissue injury even if looks
minor

ΓÇô
severe pain/tenderness over limb - monitor closely for Compartment
syndrome

ΓÇô
numbness or palsy - indicates critical injury

ΓÇô
visual disturbance - indicates eye injury

ΓÇô
trauma/injury/burns
ΓÇó	 Treat injuries or Burns, as needed
ΓÇó	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain
ΓÇó	 If patient has pacemaker or implantable defibrillator, will need function checking:

ΓÇô
continuous cardiac monitoring required - as soon as available
ΓÇó	 MO may allow home after observation if:

ΓÇô
Low voltage exposure

ΓÇô
asymptomatic

ΓÇô
no loss of consciousness, seizures or cardiac arrhythmia

ΓÇô
ECG, urinalysis normal
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ159

5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 If not evacuated advise to see MO at next clinic
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Consult MO on all occasions
ΓÇó	 Ophthalmology referral as risk of delayed ocular complications1

Acute lower leg ischaemia - Adult
Background
ΓÇó	 Caused by a sudden blockage of blood supply to limb needing urgent evaluation
and management
1.
May present with 1,2
ΓÇó	 Sudden onset intense pain in 1 leg ┬▒

ΓÇô
limb is pale, no pulse, pins and needles/paralysis, cold
2.	 Immediate management1
ΓÇó	 Contact MO urgently + urgent evacuation - a longer transfer time = higher risk of
limb loss
ΓÇó	 Get rapid history, including:

ΓÇô
leg + any other symptoms

ΓÇô
past medical history - allergies, current medicines, including recent
anticoagulants,

ΓÇô
antiplatelets or thrombolytic agents eg warfarin, aspirin, clopidogrel3

ΓÇô
prior thromboembolic events, active bleeding, ∩âí risk of bleeding/bleeding
disorders eg

ΓÇô
haemophilia3

ΓÇó	 + weight
ΓÇó	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain

ΓÇô
pain, sensation, movement, pulses, capillary refill, warmth, colour, swelling

ΓÇó	 As above

infusion

4ΓÇô6 hourly):
ΓÇó	 if APTT not able to be done on-site DO NOT give infusion or only start
immediately prior to
ΓÇó	 evacuation5
ΓÇó	 use local heparin protocol/heparin order form for doses
ΓÇó	 Monitor vital signs + neurovascular observations until evacuated
ΓÇó	 Protect limb. Use cage and heel pad if available - do not elevate1
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 As advised by MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 As above

Migraine2,4
ΓÇó	 If child consult MO/MA

AND metoclopramide - even if not nauseous as improves absorption of

Tension type headache 2,5
ΓÇó	 Usually bilateral, feels like pressure or tightness in head, mild to moderate
intensity (not severe enough to prevent walking or climbing stairs), ┬▒
photophobia, sensitivity to noise, not usually nauseous:

ΓÇô
offer ibuprofen ┬▒ paracetamol. See Acute pain

ΓÇô
consider advice on Lifestyle habits
Lifestyle habits - to help prevent migraines and tension type headaches 4,5
ΓÇó	 Regular sleep schedule
ΓÇó	 Minimise variations in BGL eg eat regular meals, avoid excess sugary food/drinks
ΓÇó	 Stay hydrated eg drink 1.5ΓÇô2 L of water/day
ΓÇó	 Limit caffeinated drinks to 1ΓÇô2 cups/day
ΓÇó	 Regular exercise
ΓÇó	 Use relaxation techniques to manage stress eg mindfulness, yoga, breathing
techniques
ΓÇó	 Avoid known triggers for patient eg alcohol, citrus fruit, chocolate, preserved
meats, perfume
ΓÇó	 If posture problems or neck muscle tightness, refer to a physiotherapist
3.	 Follow up

ΓÇó	 Might need Medical Clinic referral

Dizziness/vertigo - Adult
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Dizziness and vertigo are common symptoms, with many causes. Aim to identify
serious causes
Background1
ΓÇó	 Dizziness common causes - postural hypotension, arrhythmias, syncope,
anaemia, hypoglycaemia, hypercalcaemia, ΓÇ£vitamin B12, medicine side e ect,
anxiety,2 hyperventilation3
ΓÇó	 Vertigo - can be central (brain stem or cerebellum) or peripheral (inner ear)
related
1.
May present with1
ΓÇó	 Dizziness, faintness or feeling like body or surroundings are moving eg spinning
2.	 Immediate management1,2,4,5
ΓÇó	 Check for Red flags:

ΓÇô
if any, get rapid history and contact MO urgently. Suspect Stroke

ΓÇô
MO may consider retrieval for CT/MRI ┬▒ referral to neurologist
Red flags
ΓÇó	 Facial weakness/numbness
ΓÇó	 Arm ┬▒ leg weakness, ataxia
ΓÇó	 Speech di#culty/slurred
ΓÇó	 Visual eld loss, double vision
ΓÇó	 Difficulty swallowing
ΓÇó	 Sensory loss eg in 1 side of body
ΓÇó	 Confusion
ΓÇó	 Headache
ΓÇó	 New sudden unilateral hearing loss with 1st onset of continuous dizziness/vertigo
3.	 Clinical assessment1,2,4,5
ΓÇó	 Get history of dizziness/vertigo, including:2

ΓÇô
timing - episodic or continuous; gradual or sudden onset

ΓÇô
when occurred, are episodes
 in severity or frequency4

ΓÇô
duration of episodes - seconds/minutes/hours/days

ΓÇô
character:
	ΓÇ║
vertigo - spinning, rocking or tilting2
	ΓÇ║
dizziness - o balance, giddiness (light headed), faint (presyncope)

ΓÇô
spontaneous or triggered by eg head movement, sitting, standing, rolling over,
tipping head back, lying down, loud noises, anxiety/stress
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ171

ΓÇó	 Any associated symptoms eg:

ΓÇô
nausea, retching/vomiting, sweating2

ΓÇô
new hearing loss - 1 or both ears, fluctuating/continuous

ΓÇô
tinnitus - noise or ringing in ears OR ear discharge/pain or ΓÇÿfullΓÇÖ sensation of
ear

ΓÇô
vision issues eg bouncing, bobbing or jerking of still objects (oscillopsia)6
ΓÇó	 Get past history - including migraines, M├⌐ni├¿reΓÇÖs disease, current medicines
ΓÇó	 Any recent:

ΓÇô
change of medicine eg for BP

ΓÇô
illness eg URTI

ΓÇô
ear trauma or infection

ΓÇô
head trauma - even minor

ΓÇô
alcohol or drug use, exposure to toxin(s)

ΓÇô
diving or other pressure change (that may cause barotrauma)
ΓÇó	 Do physical examination + assess:

ΓÇô
vital signs + lying and standing BP - does BP drop when standing, how much

ΓÇô
BGL, ECG

ΓÇô
eyes - any nystagmus - quick jerky eye movements (vertical or horizontal),
either spontaneous or only when looking in a speci c direction

ΓÇô
Examine ears

ΓÇô
observe patientΓÇÖs ability to stand and walk. Are they unsteady
If no red flags, consider additional assessments (as below) in consultation with
MO
ΓÇó	 Dix-Hallpike test if:1,2,5,7

ΓÇô
vertigo is episodic +

ΓÇô
is only triggered by speci c head movement +

ΓÇô
lasts only seconds +

ΓÇô
nystagmus not occurring spontaneously +

ΓÇô
no other symptoms
ΓÇó	 HINTS plus exam if:

ΓÇô
vertigo has continued for hours/days + nystagmus occurring spontaneously3-5,8
DIX-HALLPIKE TEST4
SEE VIDEO HTTPS://WWW.YOUTUBE.COM/WATCH?V=LXD-LGQIX-S
OFFER TO SHOW PATIENT VIDEO 1ST, SO THEY KNOW WHAT TO EXPECT.
WARN PATIENT IT CAN PROVOKE VERTIGO
Get patient to sit at the edge of the bed. Turn their head with your hands 30ΓÇô45o
to 1 side
Patient to keep eyes open + focus on a stable point. Then quickly assist patient to
lie supine and hyperextend the neck 30ΓÇô45o below level of bed, while still holding
head. Look for nystagmus
If no nystagmus seen, return to seated position, then test the other side
If patient gets nystagmus and reproduction of positional symptoms, test is +ve for
Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo (BPPV)1 - a common (not harmful) cause of
vertigo due to disturbance of tiny crystals in inner ear1
172ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

HINTS PLUS EXAM2,4,5
SEE VIDEO HTTPS://COLLECTIONS.LIB.UTAH.EDU/ARK:/87278/S6BP04F0
CAN ASSIST IN DETERMINING IF VERTIGO HAS A CENTRAL OR PERIPHERAL
CAUSE. DISCUSS FINDINGS WITH MO
Head
impulse test
ΓÇó	 Sit opposite patient and ask them to stare at your nose
ΓÇó	 Hold the patient's head with both of your hands and turn head
slowly side to side 10ΓÇô20O then back to midpoint. Repeat
but rapidly rotate the head to 1 side and pause. Patient's eyes
should stay on the examiners nose with the rapid head turn
ΓÇó	 Repeat again but this time rapidly rotate to the opposite side
ΓÇó	 If eyes do a series of 'catch-up' movements to re-fixate on
nose, test is +ve
ΓÇó	 Document the direction the head was rotated to when the
'catch-up' movement occurred - eg if catch-up movement was
seen when head was quickly rotated to the patient's le', then
document ΓÇÿ+ve left head impulse testΓÇÖ
Nystagmus
ΓÇó	 Ask patient to follow your finger as you slowly move it right to
left
ΓÇó	 Observe direction of the fast phase of nystagmus - horizontal,
vertical, rotates, unidirectional or direction changing
ΓÇó	 Does nystagmus stop if patient stares at object
Test of skew
ΓÇó	 Ask patient to look straight ahead. Cover 1 of their eyes with
your hand. Then, quickly move your hand to cover the other
eye
ΓÇó	 During this process, observe uncovered eye for any vertical/
diagonal corrective movement (if present, test is +ve)
Plus acute
hearing loss
ΓÇó	 Rub fingers next to both ears and ask if new unilateral hearing
loss. If new and 1st onset of acute continuous vertigo, this is a
+ve for a central sign9
4.	 Management1,5
ΓÇó	 Consider calling vertigo hotline Dial-a-Dizzy
 1300 4 DIZZY, for advice (office
hours)
ΓÇó	 If BPPV likely:

ΓÇô
offer to do Epley manoeuvre (see video for Dix-Hallpike test for instructions).
It may relieve vertigo immediately

ΓÇô
offer advice and exercises to help, as per fact sheet eg www.safercare.vic.gov.
au/ best-practice-improvement/clinical-guidance/emergency/vertigo
ΓÇó	 If already diagnosed with M├⌐ni├¿re's disease and acute attack, offer
prochlorperazine10
ΓÇó	 In all other cases of dizziness or vertigo consult MO:

ΓÇô
+ if nausea/vomiting, o er prochlorperazine or promethazine
