Transient ischaemic attack (TIA) and stroke -
Adult/Child
Recommend
ΓÇó	 TIA + stroke are medical emergencies. Urgent evacuation + timing of treatment is
critical1
ΓÇó	 Advise community about FAST stroke recognition message:2

ΓÇô
Facial weakness, Arm ┬▒ leg weakness, Speech difficulty, Time to act fast
Background
ΓÇó	 A stroke occurs when the arteries to the brain become blocked (ischaemic
stroke/cerebral infarction) or rupture (haemorrhagic stroke), disrupting blood
supply leading to brain damage1
ΓÇó	 TIA - transient episode of neurological dysfunction, most are brief eg &lt; 1 hour +
resolve in 24hours with no brain infarction 1,3
ΓÇó	 See Transient ischaemic attack (TIA)/stroke clinical pathway https://
clinicalexcellence.qld.gov.au/resources/clinical-pathways/tia-stroke-clinical-
pathways
1.
May present with1,3
ΓÇó	 Suspect stroke if abrupt onset of ΓëÑ 1 of:

ΓÇô
asymmetric:
	ΓÇ║
face weakness
	ΓÇ║
arm weakness
	ΓÇ║
leg weakness

ΓÇô
speech disturbance eg slurred speech

ΓÇô
visual field defect/vision loss
ΓÇó	 Note: any abrupt change in neurological function could represent stroke
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 DRSABCD
ΓÇó	 Do BGL - if &lt; 3.5 contact MO urgently + treat as per MO 1,3
ΓÇó	 If persistent symptoms for &lt; 9 hours - patient may be thrombolysis candidate -
avoid delays2
ΓÇó	 Get rapid history, including:3

ΓÇô
symptoms + time of onset. If unknown, the last time the patient was seen to
be well

ΓÇô
are symptoms resolving or persisting

ΓÇô
current medicines eg anticoagulant/antiplatelet medications

ΓÇô
any seizures, loss of consciousness or syncope
ΓÇó	 Suspect TIA if symptoms have resolved. Ask about duration:1
ΓÇó	 &lt;
 10 minutes, 10 minutesΓÇô1 hour or > 1 hour
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ175

ΓÇó	 Contact MO/MA urgently in all cases of suspected stroke/TIA + urgent
evacuation
ΓÇó	 Do vital signs + GCS
ΓÇó	 Only give O2 if SpO2 &lt; 92% 1,4
ΓÇó	 ECG only if does not cause delay. If new atrial fibrillation, send copy of ECG with
patient
ΓÇó	 Continuous cardiac monitoring
ΓÇó	 Insert IVC x 2
ΓÇó	 Take bloods - FBC, UE, LFT, coagulation studies, group and hold for x-match
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Get past history + any TIA/stroke or similar symptoms3
ΓÇó	 Do physical examination + weight (if possible)3
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Ongoing management as per MO
ΓÇó	 Brain imaging eg CT scan equired to determine cause1-3
ΓÇó	 Check ability to swallow before giving oral medications:

ΓÇô
if difficulty swallowing - keep nil by mouth
ΓÇó	 Give paracetamol if fever
ΓÇó	 If ischaemic stroke:1

ΓÇô
cautious ∩âó BP if markedly raised eg > 220/110. Note: most instances resolve
spontaneously

ΓÇô
do not give aspirin until brain imaging excludes intracranial haemorrhage

ΓÇô
monitor BGL - avoid aggressive management/IV fluids with glucose
ΓÇó	 If haemorrhagic stroke:1

ΓÇô
urgent reduction of BP may be necessary to ∩âó haematoma expansion
ΓÇó	 If TIA - management is similar to ischaemic stroke1
ΓÇó	 Monitor closely until evacuation:

ΓÇô
vital signs + GCS
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 As per MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 For medical team to review patient
176ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Delirium - Adult
GENERAL CONSIDERATION:
ΓÇó	 Consider delirium where a patient has one or more of the following risks:
ΓÇó	 age ΓëÑ 65 years
ΓÇó	 history of cognitive impairment or dementia
ΓÇó	 severe medical illness
ΓÇó	 current hip fracture
ΓÇó	 cognitive concerns raised by others
ΓÇó	 Delirium may be confused with dementia
ΓÇó	 Medicine toxicity is a major cause of delirium
1.
May present with2,3
ΓÇó	 Acute change in mental status
ΓÇó	 Difficulty focusing or keeping track, easily distracted
ΓÇó	 Behaviour fluctuates in severity over 24 hour period:

ΓÇô
hyperactive:
	ΓÇ║
disorganised or incoherent thinking, rambling or illogical flow of
conversation
	ΓÇ║
hyperalert, startles easily
	ΓÇ║
restlessness, picking at clothes, tapping fingers or making frequent sudden
changes of position

ΓÇô
hypoactive:
	ΓÇ║
lethargic, drowsy
	ΓÇ║

2.	 Immediate management

emergency

ΓÇó	 Do not leave patient alone
3.	 Clinical assessment 2,4
ΓÇó	 Assess and manage patient in quiet environment

confusion

ΓÇô

ΓÇô

sleepiness, levels of consciousness

ΓÇô
communication or response levels

difficulty meeting requests

ΓÇô
alterations in mood
ΓÇó	 Do full head to toe physical examination

infections eg meningitis, encephalitis, neurosyphilis, UTI, pneumonia

ΓÇô
sodium, potassium and calcium abnormalities. Consider renal or liver disease

ΓÇô
Hypoglycaemia, or hyperglycaemia

ΓÇô
respiratory disorders leading to hypoxia or pulmonary embolism

ΓÇô
myocardial infarction, arrhythmias, heart failure

ΓÇô
seizures, epilepsy and postictal states

ΓÇô
pain or discomfort eg urinary retention, constipation, hip fracture, dental

ΓÇô
withdrawal eg alcohol, benzodiazepines
ΓÇó	 Urgently identify possible medicines toxicity:2,4

ΓÇô
consider polypharmacy, particularly drugs with anticholinergic effects, given

anticholinergics

┬╗
drugs for bladder overactivity eg oxybutynin

┬╗
antipsychotics eg clozapine, olanzapine

┬╗
lithium

┬╗
hypnotics eg benzodiazepines

┬╗
tricyclic antidepressants eg amitriptyline
	ΓÇ║

┬╗
anticholinergics eg benzatropine

┬╗
dopamine agonists eg levodopa

cardiac medications

betablockers eg sotalol and propranolol

digoxin

analgesics

opioids

ΓÇô
SpO2 and BGL to exclude hypoxia or hypoglycaemia as a cause

therapeutic drug monitoring if taking digoxin, lithium, quinidine

ΓÇô

4.	 Management 2,4
ΓÇó	 Manage and treat underlying medical cause as above in consultation with MO
ΓÇó	 For suspected medicines or other substance use, see Poisoning and overdose
ΓÇó	 Contact pharmacist for medication review
ΓÇó	 Offer analgesia. See Acute pain
ΓÇó	 Address hydration, nutrition, ventilation, temperature control, skin care, bladder
and bowel care
ΓÇó	 Provide familiar environmental cues ie clocks, calendars, with involvement from
family
ΓÇó	 Commence regular observations. Observe at all times
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 As per MO or specialist instructions
ΓÇó	 According to identified cause of delirium
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Consult MO and specialist
ΓÇó	 If no biochemical causes or sepsis identified, patient needs Psychiatric referral.

Traumatic injuries ΓÇô Adult/Child
GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS:
ΓÇó	 Early notification for transfer. See Criteria for early notification of trauma for
interfacility transfer
ΓÇó	 (inside front cover)
ΓÇó	 Assume possible spinal injury in all blunt trauma or multiple injuries.
Use caution when moving1
ΓÇó	 Systematic evaluation of all trauma patients (minor and major) 2
ΓÇó	 Common causes of preventable death in trauma: airway obstruction, tension
pneumothorax and haemorrhage
ΓÇó	 Hard/semi-rigid cervical collars are not recommended 3,4
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Trauma
2.	 Immediate management. Primary survey and
resuscitation DRSCABCD 1,6-9
Danger - eg body fluids, toxins, traffic, perpetrators of crime, &lt; 10 m from fallen
powerlines
ΓÇó	 If there is immediate threat to a personΓÇÖs life and rapid movement is needed,
make all efforts to limit spinal movement without delaying treatment.10 If you are
by yourself and patient needs moving out of danger, you may need to drag them
via ankle or arm/shoulder3
Response
Send for help - ideally 1 or more assistants are needed
Catastrophic haemorrhage - control before checking airway 1,2,7,8
ΓÇó	 Do not remove any penetrating/impaled objects eg knife 10
ΓÇó	 Use direct firm pressure - with hands + combine pads and crepe bandages. If not
working, check pad(s) is over bleeding point/using firmer pressure with 1 or 2
pads over smaller area
ΓÇó	 If unable to control:

ΓÇô
use haemostatic dressing ΓÇô clean wound / compress dressing to apply: use clean
dry gause to pack the wound only, cover the wound again with dry gause and use
bandage to compress the dressing. Elevate the limp above the level of the heart
and closely monitor for signs of ongoing bleeding.
	ΓÇ║
If the wound still bleeding, then need to apply a tourniquet

ΓÇô
arterial tourniquet (if limb): 6,10
	ΓÇ║
use a tourniquet rubber or a cloth
	ΓÇ║
apply firmly 5 to 10 cm above the injury/amputation (not over a joint/
wound)
	ΓÇ║
tighten until bleeding stops

┬╗
should elevate the limb above the level of the heart

┬╗
the tourniquet should only applied for only 1 hour to prevent risk of limb
ischaemia

┬╗
Remove the tournique after 1 hour but if need to re-apply re-tighten the
tourniquet again after 20 ΓÇô 30 minutes.

┬╗
Limb should be always elevated when applying a tourniquet and when
re-applying it.
	ΓÇ║
Consult surgeon for urgent referral

General advice
ΓÇó	 Rest quietly for next 48 hours
ΓÇó	 Eat a healthy diet, drink lots of
fluids
ΓÇó	 Avoid activities that make
symptoms worse
ΓÇó	 Use ice packs on swollen or

ΓÇó	 Use paracetamol for headache
(not ibuprofen/aspirin)
ΓÇó	 Do not take sedatives/other

hours and only once you can

ΓÇó	 Return to exercise after a few
days rest
Concussion
ΓÇó	 ΓÇó Symptoms may include:

ΓÇô
mild headaches, which settle with rest/paracetamol

ΓÇô
mild dizziness, feeling tired, difficulty paying attention or remembering
things

ΓÇô
feeling emotional or moody
ΓÇó	 Symptoms usually settle over time. Return to clinic if you are worried
Return to the clinic immediately if
ΓÇó	 Severe headache, especially if getting
worse
ΓÇó	 Dizziness or unsteady walking
ΓÇó	 Nausea, vomiting or poor feeding
(child) or frequent vomiting (adult)
ΓÇó	 Unexpected drowsiness or weakness
ΓÇó	 Disorientation or confusion
ΓÇó	 Restlessness (adult), irritability,
continued crying or unusual agitation
(child)
ΓÇó	 Slurred speech or double/blurred
vision
ΓÇó	 Seizure (fit) or spasms of face or limbs
ΓÇó	 Difficult to wake up
ΓÇó	 If you or carer(s) are worried for any
reason

Spinal injuries ΓÇô Adult/Child
Recommend1
ΓÇó	 Focus on minimal handling, maintaining immobilisation and preventing further
damage
Background
ΓÇó	 Hard/semi-rigid cervical collars are not recommended1,2
ΓÇó	 Cervical spine injury is relatively rare in children.2,3 Also see Cervical spine injury
- emergency management in children www.childrens.health.qld.gov.au/qpec-
statewide-guidelines/
1.
May present with1
ΓÇó	 History of trauma, in particular:

ΓÇô
motor vehicle or motor bike accident - occupant, rider or pedestrian

ΓÇô
industrial accident ie workplace

ΓÇô
dive or jump into shallow water or being ΓÇÿdumpedΓÇÖ in the surf

ΓÇô
sporting accident eg rugby, falling from a horse, trampoline2

ΓÇô
fall from greater than standing height eg ladder, roof

ΓÇô
fall in the elderly

ΓÇô
significant blow to head

ΓÇô
severe penetrating wound eg gunshot
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Do primary survey DRSCABCD as per Traumatic injuries, + initiate any lifesaving
measures
ΓÇó	 Maintain cervical spine (c-spine) in-line immobilisation:1
ΓÇó	 Do vital signs + BGL
ΓÇó	 Assess against Criteria for early notification of trauma for interfacility transfer
Cervical spine (c-spine) in-line immobilisation
ΓÇó	 Support head in a neutral position in line with torso - avoid twisting/angular
movements
ΓÇó	 Use soft collar (as prompt to restrict movement) + sand bags to support neck if
unconscious1,2
ΓÇó	 Padding may help - adult: 2 cm under head child &lt; 8: use thoracic elevation
device (TED)1-3
ΓÇó	 If conscious, ask them to stay still1
ΓÇó	 Restrict spinal movement until patient can undergo appropriate examination ┬▒
imaging4
ΓÇó	 Lay flat on firm supportive surface
200ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

C-spine sand-bag support over the soft neck colar pad to immobilise
suspected C-spine injury.
3.	 Clinical assessment 1,4
ΓÇó	 Do secondary survey EFGHIJ as per Traumatic injuries.

ΓÇô
note mechanism of injury + check for symptoms of spinal injury eg:1

ΓÇô
tingling, numbness in the limbs - where6 weakness or inability to move the
limbs (paralysis)

ΓÇô
altered/absent sensation - where

ΓÇô
nausea, headache or dizziness

ΓÇô
head or neck in abnormal position

ΓÇô
signs of head injury, altered LOC, shock

ΓÇô
breathing difficulties - c-spine or thoracic injury can cause respiratory failure

ΓÇô
change in muscle tone (flaccid/stiff)

ΓÇô
loss of bladder or bowel control

ΓÇô
priapism (persistent erection in males)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ201

NEXUS CRITERIA TO ASSESS RISK OF C-SPINE INJURY4,5
1
Midline cervical tenderness
ΓÇó	 Neck pain on palpation of the posterior midline neck area, from nuchal
ridge to the 3rd thoracic prominence, or palpation of any cervical spinous
process
2
Altered mental status
ΓÇó	 GCS &lt; 15
ΓÇó	 Disorientation to time, place, person or event
ΓÇó	 Inability to remember 3 objects at 5 minutes
ΓÇó	 Delayed or inappropriate response to external stimuli
3
Focal neurologic deficit
ΓÇó	 eg motor weakness, numbness/loss of sensation, paraesthesia, other eg
priapism4
4
Intoxicated - any of
ΓÇó	 Patient or observer reports recent history of  intoxication/consumption of
intoxicating substances
ΓÇó	 Odour of alcohol, slurred speech, ataxia, lack of coordination, or other
cerebellar findings
ΓÇó	 Behaviour consistent with intoxication
ΓÇó	 Tests of bodily secretions are positive for drugs or alcohol affecting mental
alertness
5
Painful distracting injury
ΓÇó	 Any non spinal related condition causing sufficient pain to distract patient
from a possible cervical spine injury eg:

ΓÇô
long bone fracture, significant visceral injury

ΓÇô
large laceration, crush or degloving injury, large burns

ΓÇô
other injury that could impair the patient's ability to appreciate cervical
spine pain
Patient is very unlikely to have c-spine injury if NONE of the above 5 criteria are
present AND
ΓÇó	 NO neck pain, bruising or deformity of neck + has full range of active neck
movement (45┬░ rotation left and right)2,4 AND
ΓÇó	 NO other high risk factors ie:2,6

ΓÇô
age > 65 (higher incidence of degenerative disease)4.6

ΓÇô
fall + landing head first from a height eg diving, trampolining5

ΓÇô
forced neck hyperflexion (low speed, high force eg rugby scrum collapse)2

ΓÇô
high risk motor vehicle/bicycle accident eg head on collision, rollover,
ejection from vehicle, death in same crash, speed > 88 km/hour2,4

ΓÇô
substantial torso injury (clavicles, abdomen, flanks, back, spine, pelvis), or
thoracic/lumbar spine or face
202ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

ΓÇó	 If ANY NEXUS criteria, urgently consult MO - requires evacuation ┬▒ CT scan 4,5
ΓÇó	 Consult MO for clearance before stopping c-spine precautions, regardless of
NEXUS criteria

ΓÇô
generally, if patient is alert, without neurological deficit, sober, does not have
additional
ΓÇó	 painful injuries, and has no pain along spine or neck, it is unlikely they have a
spinal injury4
ΓÇó	 Offer analgesia ┬▒ antiemetic. See Acute pain, Nausea and vomiting
ΓÇó	 If breathing difficulties - monitor SpO2, capnography (if able) + VBG
ΓÇó	 Continue to manage in collaboration with MO until evacuation, which may
include:

ΓÇô
vital signs + neurological observations

ΓÇô
monitoring respiration

ΓÇô
nil by mouth

ΓÇô
keep warm

ΓÇô
IDC ┬▒ oro/nasogastric tube5
ΓÇó	 If hypotensive consider neurogenic shock if hypovolaemia ruled out. Note:
haemorrhage is most common cause of shock in trauma5
ΓÇó	 If paraplegia or a level of sensory loss on the chest or abdomen, presume spinal
instability:4

ΓÇô
do not try to reduce an obvious deformity6
Moving patient with suspected spinal injury1,3
ΓÇó	 Provide in-line immobilisation of the c-spine during transfer and movement of
the patient (manually support)
ΓÇó	 Use scoop stretcher or log roll onto spinal board/vacuum mattress
ΓÇó	 Do NOT leave on rigid spinal board - high risk of pressure ulcers/necrosis
ΓÇó	 Use head blocks/sandbags to maintain cervical alignment - DO NOT strap as
significant aspiration risk
ΓÇó	 If extended immobilisation, straighten bedding and remove debris from under
patient
How to log roll1.4 Min. of 3, preferably 5, people needed
ΓÇó	 1 person takes the lead. They are positioned at the patient's head to provide
manual in-line stabilisation (with 2 hands) to the head and neck. This person
gives instructions to the rest of the team
ΓÇó	 3 people (if available) perform the roll:

ΓÇô
position along the patient's body opposite to the direction that the patient's
head is facing

ΓÇô
1 at shoulders/chest; 1 at hips; and 1 in control of the legs

ΓÇô
roll slowly maintaining spinal alignment, avoiding flexion, rotation, lateral
bending, sagging of chest or abdomen. Keeping the patient's nose in line
with the umbilicus at all times
ΓÇó	 5th person examines back, inserts/removes spinal board etc
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ203

5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 As advised by MO
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Require surgical team to review

204ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Abdominal injuries - Adult/Child
General consideration
ΓÇó	 Missed abdominal injuries are a major cause of avoidable death in trauma
patients1
ΓÇó	 The absence of abdominal pain does not rule out the presence of significant
abdominal injury2
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Abdominal trauma

ΓÇô
Blunt abdominal injury
	ΓÇ║
Blunt abdominal trauma is a leading cause of morbidity and mortality
among all age groups. Identification of serious intra-abdominal pathology
is often challenging; many injuries may not manifest during the initial
assessment and treatment period.

ΓÇô
Penetrating abdominal injury
	ΓÇ║
Penetrating abdominal trauma typically involves the violation of the
abdominal cavity by a gunshot wound or stab wound.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ205

Blunt Abdominal Injury:
ΓÇó	 History taking and identifying the mechanism of abdominal injury is crucial in
establishing the severity of the abdominal injury.
ΓÇó	 Two major life-threatening conditions result from blunt abdominal injury

I.
Internal bleeding from ruptured major vessels or organs

II.
Leakage from visceral content from perforated viscous ΓÇô peritoneal
contamination.
Signs and symptoms
The initial clinical assessment of patients with blunt abdominal trauma is often
difficult and notably inaccurate. The most reliable signs and symptoms in alert
patients are as follows:
ΓÇó	 Pain
ΓÇó	 Tenderness
ΓÇó	 Gastrointestinal hemorrhage
ΓÇó	 Hypovolemia
ΓÇó	 Evidence of peritoneal irritation (board like abdomen/rigid and positive rebound
tenderness)
If the patient had the above symptoms ΓÇô warrant admission for close monitoring
and to reassess regularly
Management:
ΓÇó	 Admit
ΓÇó	 Do primary survey DRSCABCD as per ATLS
ΓÇó	 If hypotensive and tachycardia ΓÇô insert 2 large IV cannulas
ΓÇó	 Resuscitate with IV fluid crytalloid fuids (give Boluses of fluid if hypotensive)
ΓÇó	 Insert IDC to monitor and assess the patients response to IVF resuscitation

ΓÇô
To maintain the volume of urine-output > 30 mls/hour (Normal 1-2 mls/kg/
hour)
ΓÇó	 Start IV antibiotic ΓÇô triple antibiotic covering gram negative, gram postive and
anaerobic bacteria :

ΓÇô
Ampiciliine 1gm every 6 hours (Ampicilline 50mg/kg in paediatric age group)

ΓÇô
Gentamycin 240mg daily (Gentamycin 5mg/kg daily)

ΓÇô
Flagyl 500mg every 8 hourly
ΓÇó	 Need regular pain relieve

ΓÇô
Panadol / Diclofenac
ΓÇó	 Need NGT if patient start developing abdominal distension
ΓÇó	 Discuss with MA/MO for further treatment and urgent referral
206ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Pregnant woman with abdominal trauma 2,5

ΓÇô
If > 20 weeks pregnant (uterus at level of umbilicus) - baby can compress the
vena cava causing hypotension if lying flat:
	ΓÇ║

Fractures, dislocations and sprains
Fractures:
General concideration:
ΓÇó	 Fractures can occur in normal bones or in diseased bones.
ΓÇó	 Injuries or high velocity force cause fractures in healthy bones.
ΓÇó	 Pathological bone fractures occur without any injury or from very minor injuries.
ΓÇó	 Pathological fractures occur because the bone is weak and can break easily
ΓÇó	 Weak bones can be caused by malignancy or severe osteoporosis.
ΓÇó	 Any type of fractures can cause very serious complications
2 main types of fractures
1.
Simple fracture or close fracture

ΓÇô
Fracture without any open wound on the overlying skin

ΓÇô
No open wound but theres obvious bone deformity
2.
Compound fracture or open fracture

ΓÇô
A fracture with an open wound

ΓÇô
There is an open wound with an expose bone.

ΓÇô
The wound that result from a fracture bone that pierce the overlying soft
tissue and the skin creating an open wound that exposes the bone

of slab

firmly over slab

Open (compound) fractures - Adult/Child
Defined as a fractures with a cut through the skin. The overlying skin is wounded
and the broken bone may be exposed. Compound fractures are known as open
fractures. These fractures can become infected easily and need thorough cleansing
and debriding as soon as possible after the injury.
GENERAL CONSIDERATION:
1.
Always treat as a trauma patients : Primary survey / secondary survey and
admit
2.
Consult MA/Orthopedic surgeon ΓÇô for urgent referral and treatment opinion
3.
Open fracture treatment = exposed fracture bone to reduce + thorough wound
wash + IV antibiotic (Reduce the risk of osteomyelitis) + TT injection  +  splint
and elevate

ΓÇô
thoroughly irrigate with sodium chloride 0.9%

ΓÇô
give antibiotics + requires urgent reduction

ΓÇó	 Insert IVC

ΓÇô
ask if contaminated environment eg agricultural, sewage, fresh or salt water1

How to use a pelvic binder or a sheet if no binder :
A range of pelvic binders are available which aim to reduce and stabilise pelvic
fractures. Currently there is insufficient evidence to support one device over the
other. If no device is available, use a bed sheet to wrap around the pelvis. Pelvic
binders also help with:
ΓÇó	 Prevention of re-injury from pelvic motion
ΓÇó	 Decreasing pelvic volume
ΓÇó	 Decreasing pain
ΓÇó	 Controling bleeding

Oral facial fractures
Purpose:
ΓÇó	 To assist Medical Assistants and Nurses in the primary care setting to recognize,
assess, provide initial management, and appropriately refer patients presenting
with oral facial fractures.
Background
ΓÇó	 Facial fractures are increasingly common presentations in primary healthcare.
Prompt recognition and appropriate management are critical to prevent
permanent functional, cosmetic, or vision-threatening complications.
General Principles
ΓÇó	 Follow Advanced Trauma Life Support (ATLS) protocols for all trauma cases.
ΓÇó	 Conduct a full visual assessment in any facial trauma.
ΓÇó	 Identify red flags requiring immediate referral.
ΓÇó	 Provide early pain management and wound care as appropriate.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Nasal Fractures: Often resulting from interpersonal violence, nasal fractures
are the most common facial fractures, accounting for up to 58.6% of all facial
fractures. Symptoms include nasal deformity, epistaxis, and periorbital bruising. ΓÇï
www.racgp.org.au/afp/2012/april/maxillofacial-trauma/?utm_source=chatgpt.
com
ΓÇó	 Mandibular Fractures: These may present with pain, swelling, malocclusion, and
limited mouth opening. They can result from blunt or penetrating trauma and
may destabilize the airway. www.entnet.org/wp-content/uploads/files/Trauma-
Chapter-5.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com
ΓÇó	 Zygomatic Fractures: Symptoms include periorbital swelling, infraorbital nerve
paraesthesia, and restricted eye movement.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Orbital Fractures: Signs include diplopia, reduced visual acuity, and orbital rim
tenderness.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Frontal Bone Fractures: Present with forehead deformity, possible CSF
rhinorrhoea, and periorbital bruising.ΓÇï
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ221

2.	 Immediate Management
ΓÇó	 Airway Management: Ensure airway patency, especially in cases of mandibular
fractures that may compromise the airway. https://link.springer.com/
chapter/10.1007/978-981-15-1346-6_48?utm_source=chatgpt.com
ΓÇó	 Hemostasis: Control bleeding using direct pressure, nasal packing, or hemostatic
agents as appropriate.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Analgesia: Administer appropriate pain relief, considering the severity of the
injury.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Antibiotics: Prophylactic antibiotics may be indicated in cases of open fractures
or significant soft tissue involvement. https://tsaco.bmj.com/content/9/1/
e001304?utm_source=chatgpt.com
ΓÇó	 Imaging: Obtain appropriate imaging studies, such as X-rays or CT scans, to
assess the extent of the fractures.ΓÇï

ΓÇô
Vital Symptoms/Signs Not to Miss
These require IMMEDIATE REFERRAL:
SYMPTOM/SIGN
CONDITION
ACTION
Proptosis, chemosis,
ophthalmoplegia, vision loss
Retrobulbar
haemorrhage
Immediate emergency
referral
Sudden visual loss or
decreased pupil reflex
Traumatic optic
neuropathy
Immediate emergency
referral
Diplopia, restricted upward
gaze, nausea/vomiting
(especially in young patients)
White eye blowout
fracture
Immediate emergency
referral
Mobile maxilla, malocclusion
Maxillary/midface
fracture
Immediate referral
3.	 Clinical Assessment
ΓÇó	 History: Obtain details of the mechanism of injury, time of injury, and any
associated symptoms.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Examination: Perform a thorough head and neck examination, assessing for
deformities, swelling, tenderness, and neurological deficits.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Functional Assessment: Evaluate the patientΓÇÖs ability to open and close the
mouth, chew, and speak.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Neurological Examination: Assess cranial nerve function, particularly the
trigeminal nerve, for sensory deficits.ΓÇï
222ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Conservative Management: Indicated for non-displaced fractures without
significant functional impairment. Includes analgesia, soft diet, and follow-
up.ΓÇï www.rch.org.au/trauma-service/manual/maxillofacial-injury/?utm_
source=chatgpt.com
ΓÇó	 Surgical Intervention: Required for displaced fractures, fractures with airway
compromise, or those causing significant functional impairment.ΓÇï www.slideshare.
net/slideshow/maxillofacial-trauma-19339123/19339123
ΓÇó	 Referral: Refer to oral and maxillofacial specialists for definitive management.
https://metronorth.health.qld.gov.au/specialist_service/refer-your-patient/oral-
maxillo-facial/facial-fractures-lacerations?utm_source=chatgpt.com
5.	 Follow-Up
ΓÇó	 Monitoring: Regular follow-up to assess healing, manage complications, and
address any functional issues.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Rehabilitation: May include physiotherapy for jaw mobility and speech therapy if
necessary.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Psychological Support: Provide support for patients dealing with the
psychological impact of facial trauma.ΓÇï
6.	 Referral / Consultation
ΓÇó	 Urgent Referral: For patients with airway compromise, significant bleeding, or
complex fractures.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Routine Referral: For non-displaced fractures without functional impairment.ΓÇï
ΓÇó	 Specialist Consultation: Consult with oral and maxillofacial surgeons for definitive
management. ΓÇïwww.westernhealth.org.au/HealthProfessionals/Referrals/
Documents/ASC%20Access%20and%20Referral%20Guideline_Oral%20
Maxillofacial%20Surgery.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com

Dog Bite ΓÇô all ages (Kiribati Original Protocol)
GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS:
ΓÇó	 Dog bites can cause serious injury and infection, including tetanus, sepsis, and
rabies (if rabies is endemic).
ΓÇó	 Early assessment and wound care are essential to reduce complications.
ΓÇó	 Tetanus toxoid (TT) should be given based on immunisation history and wound
risk.
ΓÇó	 In rabies-endemic areas, rabies post-exposure prophylaxis may be needed.
ΓÇó	 Bitten by a dog (domestic or wild), may or may not break the skin
1.
May present with 1,3
ΓÇó	 Skin puncture, laceration or crush injury from dog bite
ΓÇó	 Bleeding, swelling, redness, or signs of infection (pain, pus, fever)
ΓÇó	 History of animal attack or close contact with saliva through broken skin
ΓÇó	 Anxiety or distress, especially in children
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Apply basic first aid: control bleeding and clean the wound with soap and
running water for at least 15 minutes
ΓÇó	 Remove any foreign material if visible
ΓÇó	 Cover with a clean dressing
ΓÇó	 Reassure the patient and caregiver
ΓÇó	 Urgently assess TT status (see table on following pages)
3.	 Clinical assessment 1,2,3
ΓÇó	 Obtain history:

ΓÇô
Time and location of the bite

ΓÇô
Whether dog is domestic or stray, vaccinated, or known to the patient

ΓÇô
Provoked or unprovoked attack

ΓÇô
TT and rabies immunisation history
	ΓÇ║
Assess wound:

ΓÇô
Site, depth, contamination, and evidence of infection

ΓÇô
High-risk sites: face, hands, feet, genitals
	ΓÇ║
Do full vital signs
	ΓÇ║
Consider mental health impact (particularly in children)

230ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Consider the Dunbar Scale to assess the levl of bite severity and whether the
patient needs review by the surgical team: An assessment of the severity of biting
problems based on an objective evaluation of wound pathology
ΓÇó	 Level 1. Obnoxious or aggressive behavior but no skin-contact by teeth.
ΓÇó	 Level 2. Skin-contact by teeth but no skin-puncture. However, may be skin nicks
(less than one tenth of an inch deep) and slight bleeding caused by forward or
lateral movement of teeth against skin, but no vertical punctures.
ΓÇó	 Level 3. One to four punctures from a single bite with no puncture deeper than
half the length of the dogΓÇÖs canine teeth. Maybe lacerations in a single direction,
caused by victim pulling hand away, owner pulling dog away, or gravity (little
dog jumps, bites and drops to floor).
ΓÇó	 Level 4. One to four punctures from a single bite with at least one puncture
deeper than half the length of the dogΓÇÖs canine teeth. May also have deep
bruising around the wound (dog held on for N seconds and bore down) or
lacerations in both directions (dog held on and shook its head from side to side).
ΓÇó	 Level 5. Multiple-bite incident with at least two Level 4 bites or multiple-attack
incident with at least one Level 4 bite in each.
ΓÇó	 Level 6. Victim dead.
4.	 Management
Antibiotics
ΓÇó	 All moderate or severe bites, or if patient is at high risk of infection:
ΓÇó	 Amoxicillin + Clavulanate

ΓÇô
Child: 20ΓÇô40 mg/kg/dose (based on amoxicillin component) PO bd

ΓÇô
Adult: 875 mg/125 mg PO bd

ΓÇô
Duration: 5 days, extend to 7 days if signs of infection
ΓÇó	 If penicillin-allergic: doxycycline + metronidazole (consult MO)
Wound care
ΓÇó	 Clean thoroughly with saline after initial soap/water wash
ΓÇó	 Do not suture unless wound is large and not grossly contaminated (consult MO)
ΓÇó	 Update tetanus immunisation (see table)
ΓÇó	 In rabies-endemic settings, follow national rabies PEP protocols

Acute abdominal pain
Background
Acute abdomen refers to a sudden onset of severe abdominal pain that may
require urgent medical attention. The cause can be gastrointestinal, urological,
reproductive, respiratory, or related to trauma. A diagram classifying causes of
acute abdominal pain should be inserted here.
In females of reproductive age, always consider:
ΓÇó	 Ectopic pregnancy┬áΓÇô
particularly if pain is accompanied by vaginal bleeding
ΓÇó	 Pelvic inflammatory disease (PID) ΓÇô particularly if &lt;30 years and sexually active,
with new pelvic pain
In males:
ΓÇó	 Testicular torsion must be considered in all males with abdominal pain
ΓÇó	 In male infants with inconsolable crying, examine for testicular or scrotal
pathology
A definitive diagnosis is not always required immediately. However, a detailed and
structured history and examination are critical to guide next steps and identify red
flags.
Recommendations
Always take a comprehensive pain history and perform a full abdominal and genital
examination. Urgently consult or refer patients with red flags such as signs of
peritonitis, sepsis, shock, or suspected ectopic pregnancy or testicular torsion.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Sudden or gradual onset of abdominal pain
ΓÇó	 Pain may be continuous or intermittent
ΓÇó	 Pain may be localised or generalised
ΓÇó	 Pain may radiate to the back or shoulder tip
ΓÇó	 Associated symptoms may include:

ΓÇô
Nausea or vomiting (bile, blood, or faecal-smelling)

ΓÇô
Diarrhoea or constipation

ΓÇô
Fever or shortness of breath

ΓÇô
Dysuria, haematuria

ΓÇô
Vaginal bleeding or discharge

ΓÇô
Poor feeding, refusal to eat (children)

ΓÇô
Recent trauma to abdomen, scrotum, or genital area

ΓÇô
Weight loss or anorexia
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ233

2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Apply DRSABCD approach
ΓÇó	 If unstable: start IV access and fluids
ΓÇó	 Keep nil by mouth
ΓÇó	 Provide pain relief as needed
ΓÇó	 If testicular torsion or ectopic pregnancy suspected: urgent referral
ΓÇó	 Monitor vital signs
ΓÇó	 Do not delay consultation in unstable patients or those with red flags
3.	 Clinical assessment
History of pain
Ask about:
ΓÇó	 Site ΓÇô ask patient to point to location
ΓÇó	 Onset ΓÇô sudden, gradual; what were they doing when it started
ΓÇó	 Character ΓÇô sharp, dull, stabbing, crampy, burning
ΓÇó	 Radiation ΓÇô e.g. to back, shoulder tip
ΓÇó	 Timing ΓÇô first onset, duration, prior episodes
ΓÇó	 Exacerbating/alleviating factors ΓÇô worse with movement? relieved by lying still?
ΓÇó	 Severity ΓÇô scale 1ΓÇô10, impact on sleep or daily activity
Associated symptoms
Ask about:
ΓÇó	 Vomiting ΓÇô bile, blood, green, faecal odour
ΓÇó	 Bowel habits ΓÇô diarrhoea, constipation, black or bloody stools, last motion
ΓÇó	 Urine ΓÇô frequency, burning, urgency, blood
ΓÇó	 Vaginal ΓÇô bleeding, discharge
ΓÇó	 Respiratory symptoms ΓÇô shortness of breath, cough (to consider pneumonia)
ΓÇó	 Weight loss, poor feeding, food refusal
ΓÇó	 Foreign body ingestion or recent trauma (especially in children)
Physical examination
Perform a full abdominal examination:
ΓÇó	 Look:

ΓÇô
Shape and symmetry (distended, sunken)

ΓÇô
Surgical scars, bruises, hernias, distended veins

ΓÇô
Signs of non-accidental injury
ΓÇó	 Palpate:

ΓÇô
Start away from site of pain; light then deep palpation in 9 areas

ΓÇô
Assess for rebound tenderness (pain when pressure is released)

ΓÇô
Localised tenderness, distension, guarding (involuntary tightening)

ΓÇô
Pain out of proportion to findings

ΓÇô
Palpate kidneys with one hand under flank and one above

ΓÇô

Management of common surgical abdominal
condition associated with acute abdominal pain
Bowel obstruction (large and small bowel)
Background
Intestinal obstruction is an important cause of acute abdomen and accounts for
approximately 10% of emergency surgical admissions. It occurs when the normal
flow of intestinal contents is interrupted, either partially or completely.
Obstruction can occur in the small or large bowel and may be classified in several
ways:
ΓÇó	 Permanent or intermittent
ΓÇó	 Complete or partial
ΓÇó	 Acute or chronic
ΓÇó	 Intrinsic or extrinsic
ΓÇó	 Mechanical or functional
Mortality varies depending on the cause and associated complications. Mortality
is 100% in untreated strangulated obstructions. Morbidity and mortality are
significantly increased in patients where diagnosis is delayed or management is
inappropriate.
Common causes include:
ΓÇó	 Volvulus
ΓÇó	 Strangulated hernias
ΓÇó	 Faecal impaction (e.g. HirschsprungΓÇÖs disease)
ΓÇó	 Neoplastic causes (e.g. bowel cancer)
Recommendations
All patients with suspected bowel obstruction must be admitted for close
monitoring and stabilisation. Initiate IV fluids, insert a nasogastric tube for
decompression, commence broad-spectrum IV antibiotics, and arrange urgent
referral to the main hospital for definitive investigation and management.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó Abdominal pain
ΓÇó Abdominal distension or bloating
ΓÇó Nausea and vomiting
ΓÇó Constipation or obstipation (no passage of faeces or flatus)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ237

ΓÇó	 Admit patient
ΓÇó	 Monitor vital signs regularly
ΓÇó	 Keep nil by mouth (NBM)
ΓÇó	 Insert nasogastric tube (NGT) for decompression and free drainage
ΓÇó	 Begin IV fluid resuscitation as per weight
ΓÇó	 Start IV broad-spectrum antibiotics (e.g. Ampicillin + Metronidazole)
ΓÇó	 Organise urgent referral to main hospital for surgical review and further
management
3.	 Clinical assessment
General appearance
ΓÇó	 Patient may or may not show signs of distress
ΓÇó	 Blood pressure and pulse may be elevated
Abdominal examination
ΓÇó	 Abdominal distension
ΓÇó	 Visible or obvious bowel peristalsis
ΓÇó	 Tense and tender abdomen
ΓÇó	 Bowel sounds:

ΓÇô
High-pitched or ΓÇ£tinklingΓÇ¥ in early obstruction

ΓÇô
Reduced or absent in late obstruction or paralytic ileus

Acute appendicitis
Background
Acute appendicitis is the most common abdominal surgical emergency in the
world, with a lifetime risk of 8.6% in males and 6.9% in females. It is caused by
inflammation of the appendix and requires prompt recognition and referral.
The classical history involves colicky central abdominal pain that localises to the
right iliac fossa (RIF) within 6ΓÇô12 hours and becomes constant. However, this pattern
is only present in approximately half of patients, and clinical judgement must be
used to support diagnosis.
Recommendations
All suspected cases of acute appendicitis should be admitted immediately.
Keep nil by mouth (NBM), start triple IV antibiotics (Gentamicin, Ampicillin and
Metronidazole), hydrate with IV fluids, provide analgesia, and refer urgently to the
main hospital for surgical management.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Colicky central abdominal pain that shifts to the RIF within 6ΓÇô12 hours
ΓÇó	 Constant pain in the RIF
ΓÇó	 Nausea or vomiting
ΓÇó	 Fever
ΓÇó	 Loss of appetite
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Admit patient
ΓÇó	 Keep nil by mouth (NBM) ΓÇô do not allow food or drink
ΓÇó	 Insert IV line and commence fluids
ΓÇó	 Start IV triple antibiotic therapy
ΓÇó	 Provide pain relief as required
ΓÇó	 Organise urgent referral to the main hospital for surgical assessment and
management

3.	 Clinical assessment
Abdominal examination findings of severe appendicitis:
ΓÇó	 Direct tenderness: Pain on pressing in the RIF
ΓÇó	 Guarding: Voluntary or involuntary tightening of abdominal muscles in the RIF
ΓÇó	 Rigidity: Board-like abdomen due to reflex spasm of abdominal wall
ΓÇó	 Rebound tenderness: Pain when pressing firmly for 30 seconds in the RIF and
releasing suddenly
ΓÇó	 RovsingΓÇÖs sign: Rebound pain in the RIF when pressing and releasing in the LIF
ΓÇó	 Psoas sign: Pain in the RIF when patient lifts flexed thigh against resistance
ΓÇó	 Fever or tachycardia may also be present

Cholecystitis
Background
Cholecystitis is inflammation of the gallbladder, most commonly caused by
gallstones. Gallstone disease is one of the most frequent abdominal conditions
requiring hospital admission in the developed world. In the UK alone, 5.5
million people are estimated to have gallstones, and approximately 50,000
cholecystectomies are performed annually.
Gallstones are found 2ΓÇô3 times more frequently in women than in Obesity is the
strongest independent risk factor for gallstone development in women. This is likely
due to the effect of oestrogen, which increases the movement of cholesterol from
the bloodstream into the liver, contributing to gallstone formation.
Recommendations
If acute cholecystitis is suspected, admit the patient immediately. Resuscitate if
there are signs of sepsis or dehydration. Keep the patient nil by mouth (NBM),
initiate IV fluids and broad-spectrum antibiotics, and provide opiate analgesia. Refer
to the main hospital or surgical team, especially if pain persists or the patient is
unstable.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Constant right upper quadrant (RUQ) abdominal pain (not colicky)
ΓÇó	 Pain lasting 2ΓÇô6 hours, may persist up to 24 hours
ΓÇó	 Nausea and vomiting ΓÇô may occur but are not dominant features
ΓÇó	 MurphyΓÇÖs sign positive (pain on inspiration during RUQ palpation)
ΓÇó	 Chronic cholecystitis may present with vague symptoms such as:

ΓÇô
Intolerance of fatty foods

ΓÇô
Early satiety

ΓÇô
Recurrent similar episodes
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Admit patient
ΓÇó	 Apply DRSABCD and initiate resuscitation if signs of dehydration or sepsis
ΓÇó	 Insert two large bore IV cannulae
ΓÇó	 Insert urinary catheter (IDC) and measure urine output if unstable
ΓÇó	 Keep nil by mouth (NBM)
ΓÇó	 Start IV fluids and broad-spectrum antibiotics
ΓÇó	 Provide opiate analgesia
ΓÇó	 Urgently refer to the surgical team or main hospital if instability or pain persists
244ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Gall stone lodges in infundibulum of gall bladder/cystic duct/common bile duct
Increased intraluminal pressure causes hollow viscus distension and pain, nausea
and vomiting, with contraction of the whole biliary tree
Stone not passed -continued rise in
intraluminal pressure and viscus distension
causes mucosal ischaemia. Inflammatory
mediator release and bacterial infection
cause additional damage to mucosa -
acute cholecystitis

Stone is passed and symptoms
of obstruction are relieved
- biliary colic/ chronic
cholecystitis
Complications of continued gall stone obstruction*/- perforation
Infection with obstruction - gallbladder empyema (obstruction at cystic duct)
and ascending cholangitis (complete obstruction at common bile duct, with
bacterial infection)
Gallstone pancreatitis - gallstone obstruction of pancreatic duct
Chronic cholecystitis - recurrent episodes of pain and non specific symptoms
secondary to the presence of gallstones in the biliary tree
Gallstone Ileus - fistula between the inflamed gallbladder and the adjacent
duodenum or jejunum. Gallstone passes to Gl tract. Intestinal obstruction results.
Continued mucosal damage - gangrene of the gallbladder wall
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ245

ΓÇó	 Check vital signs: temperature, pulse, blood pressure, respiratory rate

ΓÇô
RUQ tenderness

ΓÇô
MurphyΓÇÖs sign positive

ΓÇô
Guarding may be present
ΓÇó	 Look for signs of systemic sepsis (tachycardia, hypotension, low urine output)
ΓÇó	 Assess hydration and urine output

Rectal bleeding (Anorectal disease)
Background
Rectal bleeding may be minor spotting or significant blood loss. It may appear as
blood mixed with stool, blood coating the stool, or blood leaking from the rectum.
The cause may be benign or serious, so a careful history and examination are
essential.
Common benign causes include:
ΓÇó	 Haemorrhoids
ΓÇó	 Anal fissure
ΓÇó	 Anorectal abscesses (including pilonidal disease)
ΓÇó	 Rectal prolapse
Other causes and important differentials include:
ΓÇó	 Sexually transmitted infections
ΓÇó	 Inflammatory bowel disease (IBD)
ΓÇó	 Malignancy (colorectal or anal cancer)
ΓÇó	 Proctalgia fugax
ΓÇó	 Proctitis
ΓÇó	 Anorectal foreign bodies
ΓÇó	 Local trauma (including sexual trauma)
Recommendations
Take a detailed history and examine the perianal region carefully. Rectal bleeding
should never be assumed to be benign without excluding serious pathology. Refer if
signs of systemic illness, large volume bleeding, or failure to respond to conservative
management.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Bright red blood mixed with stool
ΓÇó	 Blood coating stool or leaking from the anus
ΓÇó	 Perianal pain, swelling or itching
ΓÇó	 Difficulty passing stool
ΓÇó	 Fever, anorexia (if abscess)
ΓÇó	 Recent weight loss, change in bowel habit
ΓÇó	 Mucous discharge or tenesmus (urge to pass stool)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ247

2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Assess haemodynamic stability (pulse, BP)
ΓÇó	 If heavy bleeding or signs of anaemia, lie
patient flat and monitor closely
ΓÇó	 Provide analgesia as needed
ΓÇó	 Begin IV fluids if unstable or signs of
dehydration
ΓÇó	 Do not delay rectal examination if serious
pathology suspected
3.	 Clinical assessment
Detailed history should include:
ΓÇó	 Onset, duration, and amount of bleeding
ΓÇó	 Colour of blood ΓÇô bright red vs dark or maroon
ΓÇó	 Relation of blood to stool (mixed in, coating, separate)
ΓÇó	 Straining or hard stools
ΓÇó	 Past history of haemorrhoids
ΓÇó	 Change in bowel habits ΓÇô constipation, diarrhoea, mucous
ΓÇó	 Associated symptoms: rectal pain, itch, abdominal pain, weight loss
ΓÇó	 History of IBD, cancer, recent polypectomy
ΓÇó	 History of rectal or sexual trauma
ΓÇó	 Use of aspirin, NSAIDs, anticoagulants or antiplatelet agents
Examination:
ΓÇó	 Inspect perianal area for:
ΓÇó	 External haemorrhoids
ΓÇó	 Skin tags
ΓÇó	 Anal fissures
ΓÇó	 Haematomas
ΓÇó	 Perform rectal examination where safe
and appropriate to check for masses,
tenderness, or stool colour

Abdominal wall hernias
Background
A hernia is defined as the protrusion of a viscus or its contents through a weakness
in the abdominal wall. These are a common cause of abdominal pain and must be
assessed during abdominal examination.
Hernias can be classified as follows:
1.
Anatomical type:

ΓÇô
Inguinal hernia

ΓÇô
Umbilical hernia
2.
Reducibility:

ΓÇô
Reducible hernia

ΓÇô
Non-reducible (incarcerated or strangulated) hernia
Hernias may present without symptoms or may become painful or obstructed.
Bowel herniation into the hernia sac may lead to obstruction, strangulation, or
ischaemia.
Recommendations
All patients presenting with abdominal pain or swelling should be examined
for hernia. Refer urgently if the hernia is irreducible, painful, or associated with
symptoms of bowel obstruction. Avoid attempts to manually reduce the hernia.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Abnormal swelling, especially in the umbilical or groin area
ΓÇó	 Swelling may reduce when lying flat (if reducible)
ΓÇó	 Pain or tenderness over the swelling
ΓÇó	 Nausea, vomiting, and abdominal distension if bowel obstruction present
ΓÇó	 Incarcerated or strangulated hernias may present with:

ΓÇô
Severe abdominal pain

ΓÇô
Non-reducible tender mass

ΓÇô
Constipation or no passage of flatus

ΓÇô
Fever or signs of sepsis
252ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
If signs of intestinal involvement are present (severe abdominal pain, vomiting,
distension):
ΓÇó	 Admit the patient immediately
ΓÇó	 Keep nil by mouth (NBM)
ΓÇó	 Insert nasogastric tube for decompression and free drainage
ΓÇó	 Start IV fluid resuscitation
ΓÇó	 Start broad-spectrum antibiotics
ΓÇó	 Provide analgesia
ΓÇó	 Arrange urgent referral to the main hospital
ΓÇó	 Surgical consultation must occur within 6 hours to avoid strangulation and
ischaemic bowel

If no signs of intestinal involvement:
ΓÇó	 Provide pain relief
ΓÇó	 Advise rest and avoid lifting or strenuous activity
ΓÇó	 Avoid massage or manual reduction (may risk ischaemia)
ΓÇó	 Refer to surgeon for elective hernia repair
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Inspect for visible abdominal or groin swelling
ΓÇó	 Palpate swelling: assess for tenderness and reducibility
ΓÇó	 Ask the patient to cough or stand to assess impulse
ΓÇó	 Check for signs of bowel obstruction:

ΓÇô
Distension

ΓÇô
Bowel sounds (high-pitched or absent)

ΓÇô
Vomiting or failure to pass stool/gas
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ253

For complicated hernia (with signs of obstruction or strangulation):

Common medical
conditions

Urticaria, allergic rhinitis - Adult
Hives, hay fever
1.
May present with
Hives (urticaria)
ΓÇó	 Itchy rash - blotches or raised red lumps:1

ΓÇô
vary in size from pinhead to dinner plate. When first appear, can look like
mosquito bites

ΓÇô
usually disappear within minutes to hours in one spot, but may come and go
ΓÇó	 ┬▒ angio-oedema - deeper swellings mostly affects face and lips:1,2

ΓÇô
often bigger, last longer and may itch less ┬▒ hurt or burn
ΓÇó	 ┬▒ symptoms of viral infection - most common cause of hives in children1,2
ΓÇó	 Note: if hives occur within 1ΓÇô2 hours of exposure (eg food, medicines, stings) and
disappear within
ΓÇó	 6ΓÇô8 hours, is likely an allergic reaction. Localised hives may be a contact allergy1
Hay fever (allergic rhinitis)
ΓÇó	 Clear rhinorrhoea, sneezing, nasal blockage, nasal itch3,4
ΓÇó	 Darkened circles around eyes ┬▒ watery, red eyes3
ΓÇó	 Itchy throat, frequent need to clear throat3
ΓÇó	 Note: symptoms can be confused with recent URTI
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 If hives, check for ANY signs of anaphylaxis. If any treat urgently as per
Anaphylaxis
Signs of Anaphylaxis - any  ONE of the following:
ΓÇó	 Difficult/noisy breathing
ΓÇó	 Swelling of tongue
ΓÇó	 Swelling/tightness in throat
ΓÇó	 Difficulty talking ┬▒ hoarse voice
ΓÇó	 Wheeze or persistent cough
ΓÇó	 Persistent dizziness or collapse
ΓÇó	 Pale and floppy (young children)
ΓÇó	 Abdominal pain, vomiting
ΓÇó	 Suspect Foreign body in nose, in child with any nasal occlusion, facial swelling ┬▒
pain, or smelly, purulent or blood-stained nasal discharge3,6

3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Get history, including:

ΓÇô
onset/duration of symptoms

ΓÇô
known allergies/triggers eg food, exercise, pollen, dust mites, mould

ΓÇô
previous episodes, treatment, was it effective3

ΓÇô
eczema, asthma6

ΓÇô
if hives:
	ΓÇ║
foods and medications consumed several hours before the reaction6
	ΓÇ║
possible stings or bites6
	ΓÇ║
symptoms of a viral infection eg fever, malais

ΓÇô
if hay fever:
	ΓÇ║
are symptoms there year round, seasonal or come and go
	ΓÇ║
is it troublesome for patient/impacting on sleep, sport, work etc
ΓÇó	 Do physical examination, including:

ΓÇô
vital signs

ΓÇô
if hives - check skin, describe lesions, localised/widespread
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 If angio-oedema (see above), consult MO
ΓÇó	 Advise patient to avoid/minimise exposure allergens (if known)4
ΓÇó	 Give antihistamine eg cetirizine (effective for hives and hay fever)
ΓÇó	 If hives advise:1

ΓÇô
keep cool, wear loose clothing

ΓÇô
avoid aggravating factors eg alcohol, excessive heat, spicy foods7

ΓÇô
do not take aspirin or other NSAIDs - can make symptoms worse

ΓÇô
most hives will resolve within a couple of weeks without treatment
ΓÇó	 If hay fever:7

ΓÇô
if watery, red eyes, also see Allergic conjunctivitis, for eye drops

ΓÇô
advise to see MO at next clinic if:
	ΓÇ║
severe/troublesome for patient, or has pre-existing asthma3
	ΓÇ║
note: hay fever can co-exist with asthma

ΓÇô
if allergic to pollens, advise may be at risk of thunderstorm asthma. Stay
indoors in thunderstorms. Also see https://www.allergy.org.au/patients/
asthma-and-allergy/ thunderstorm-asthma

Upper respiratory tract infection (URTI) - Adult
Common cold, influenza, bronchitis, sore throat
1.
May present with1
Common cold (acute viral rhinosinusitis)
ΓÇó	 Blocked nose
ΓÇó	 Runny nose or posterior nasal drip (can trigger cough)
ΓÇó	 Facial pain/pressure
ΓÇó	 ∩âó sense of smell
ΓÇó	 If fever, usually mild
Influenza
ΓÇó	 As per common cold:

ΓÇô
+ sudden onset of fever, headache, muscle + joint pain, severe malaise, sore
throat2,3
Bronchitis - inflammation of bronchi that usually follows an URTI4,5
ΓÇó	 Cough ┬▒

ΓÇô
purulent or coloured sputum

ΓÇô
SOB, wheeze

ΓÇô
chest discomfort/pain due to frequent coughing

ΓÇô
nasal congestion

ΓÇô
headache, fever
ΓÇó	 If cough not prominent feature, consider likelihood of other URTI eg common

2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Use appropriate PPE

3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Get history, including:1

ΓÇô
onset, duration, severity of symptoms

ΓÇô
fever

ΓÇô
SOB, cough, any sputum - colour

ΓÇô
facial pain, chest discomfort/pain

ΓÇô
asthma, COPD, immunocompromised1,6

ΓÇô
recent international travel7
ΓÇó	 Do physical examination, including:

ΓÇô
vital signs

ΓÇô
check/listen for:
	ΓÇ║
WOB

ΓÇ║
∩âó air entry, wheeze, crackles
	ΓÇ║
ENT - red throat, enlarged tonsils, bulging red eardrums
	ΓÇ║
enlarged head/neck lymph glands
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Most respiratory tract infections are viral + get better without antibiotics8
ΓÇó	 If severe symptoms + patient unwell consult MO
ΓÇó	 Paracetamol or ibuprofen if needed. See Acute pain
ΓÇó	 Advise to treat symptoms eg:

ΓÇô
gargle warm salty water

ΓÇô
suck on an ice cube or lozenges

ΓÇô
have a soothing drink eg honey and lemon

ΓÇô
use saline nasal drops/spray

ΓÇô
wash hands, cover mouth when sneezing/coughing to help stop others
catching it
Common cold likely
ΓÇó	 Symptoms usually clear within 7 days1
ΓÇó	 dvise to return if develops features of bronchitis and/or Bacterial sinusitis
Influenza likely
ΓÇó	 Consider nose and throat swab PCR to confirm (or as per Public Health Unit
advice)
ΓÇó	 Consult MO if at risk of complications (antiviral medicine(s) may be indicated),
including:2
 ΓÇô
ΓëÑ 65 years

ΓÇô
immunocompromised, chronic conditions

ΓÇô
pregnant woman

ΓÇô
obesity

ΓÇô
homeless person

Bronchitis likely4

ΓÇô

ΓÇô

ΓÇô
influenza

ΓÇô
heart failure
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Advise to be reviewed if not improving in a few days, symptoms worsen, new
symptoms, or concerned. Consult MO
ΓÇó	 If cough continues > 3 weeks, advise to see MO at next clinic - may need further
investigations
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Laboratory confirmed influenza is notifiable ∩Ç⌐

Recommend1,2
ΓÇó	 The causes can be difficult to diagnose. Be suspicious of septic arthritis

ΓÇó	 Vital signs
ΓÇó	 Screen for Sepsis

ΓÇó	 Recent joint surgery
ΓÇó	 Recent injury/wound (can be minor)

pain - sudden or gradual onset

other symptoms eg fatigue, skin infection, sore throat3

Background
ΓÇó	 Gout occurs from deposits of urate crystals in the body. Causes joint pain +

1.
May present with1,2
ΓÇó	 Painful, red + swollen joint(s) eg big toe, knee
ΓÇó	 Attack of gout
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment1,2
ΓÇó	 Get history, including:

ΓÇô
previous diagnosis,  past episodes of gout + treatment eg urate lowering

ΓÇô
re-presentation for this attack

Secondary prophylaxis for acute rheumatic
fever (ARF) - Adult/Child
Recommend1
ΓÇó	 Strict regular long-term benzathine benzylpenicillin  (Bicillin LA┬«) prophylaxis
(every 21ΓÇô28 days) is critical to prevent recurrent Group A Strep infections
causing ARF
ΓÇó	 Every day of non-treatment over 28 days puts the person at high risk of
recurrence of ARF
Background1
ΓÇó	 Recurrent ARF can lead to a chronic condition called rheumatic heart disease
(RHD) which involves deformity and dysfunction of the heart valves
ΓÇó	 The RHD Register and Control Program TCH + is available for clinical support and
education ∩Ç⌐ 74028100 Ext 302
ΓÇó	 Recommended resources. See https://www.rhdaustralia.org.au/resources

ΓÇô
The 2020 Australian guideline for prevention, diagnosis  and management of
acute rheumatic fever and rheumatic heart disease (3rd edition)
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Common age group 5-25 years old
ΓÇó	 History of recurrent sore throat
ΓÇó	 recurrent joint pains, may or may not be migratory
ΓÇó	 Fever especially with acute attacks of joint pains
ΓÇó	 Involuntary movements (syndeham chorea)
ΓÇó	 Non-blanching rash which is painless and not itchy. Commonly located on the
trunk and extremeties.
ΓÇó	 Easily fatigued
ΓÇó	 SOB if advanced to RHD
ΓÇó	 +/- Murmur on auscultation
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Pain relief: ibrufen 400mg tds no longer than a weak
ΓÇó	 Paracetamol for fever
ΓÇó	 Bedrest
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ267

3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Check and complete care items on the patient's ARF/RHD injection booklet

ΓÇô
Fill in registration form if newly diagnosed

ΓÇô
Check injection compliance

ΓÇô
Last echo at least every two years, annually for RHD.
ΓÇó	 Ask:

ΓÇô
if any problems after previous injection(s)

ΓÇô
where patient wants injection:1

ΓÇô
ventrogluteal preferred site; lateral thigh is acceptable

ΓÇô
note: upper outer quadrant of the buttock is associated with sciatic nerve
damage and must be used with caution
4.	 Management1
ΓÇó	 The duration of secondary prophylaxis is a specialist decision based on a number
of individual and environmental factors. Prophylaxis can only be ceased by a
specialist MO
ΓÇó	 Injections can be given anytime between 21ΓÇô28 days:

ΓÇô
a specialist may recommend a 21 day regimen (rather than 21ΓÇô28 days) for
patients who have breakthrough ARF despite complete adherence to a 28 day
regimen OR have Moderate RHD .
ΓÇó	 Give benzathine benzylpenicillin (Bicillin LA┬«):

ΓÇô
consider strategies for Managing injection pain

ΓÇô
if allergic to penicillin, specialist will order oral erythromycin

ΓÇô
Mix: 1ml lidnocaine + 3mls water for injection.
ΓÇó	 If patient has bleeding problems after injections or consistently declines Bicillin
LA┬« despite attempts to identify and address any barriers to injections:

ΓÇô
specialist may order oral phenoxymethylpencillin
ΓÇó	 If oral antibiotics given:

ΓÇô
only after discussion with MO

ΓÇô
emphasise consequence of missed doses + encourage to consider returning to
injections

ΓÇô
∩âí monitoring for Group A Strep infections eg Impetigo, Sore throat and
recurrence of ARF

Anemia - Adult/Child
Recommend
Anaemia is a condition where the number of red blood cells or the amount
of haemoglobin is below normal. It is not a diagnosis in itself but a sign of an
underlying cause that must be identified and treated.
Normal Haemoglobin Ranges
ΓÇó	 Adults

ΓÇô
Males: 13ΓÇô18 g/dL

ΓÇô
Females: 12ΓÇô16 g/dL
ΓÇó	 Children

ΓÇô
Birth: 13.5 g/dL

ΓÇô
6 weeks: 9.5 g/dL

ΓÇô
3 months: 10 g/dL

ΓÇô
6 months ΓÇô 1.5 years: 11 g/dL

ΓÇô
1.5 ΓÇô 7 years: 10.5 g/dL

ΓÇô
7 ΓÇô 12 years: 11.5 g/dL
Types of Anaemia
Anaemia is not a diagnosis in itselfΓÇöthere is always an underlying cause which must
be determined before treatment. The main types of anaemia include:
Deficiency Anaemia
Caused by a lack of substances essential for blood production:
ΓÇó	 Iron deficiency┬á(most
common):

ΓÇô
Caused by blood loss (e.g. trauma, ectopic pregnancy, childbirth, excessive
menstruation, bleeding gastric ulcer)

ΓÇô
Medications such as aspirin, ibuprofen, and indomethacin may cause gastric
ulcers and bleeding

ΓÇô
Inadequate dietary intake of iron (common in malnutrition)

ΓÇô
 Chronic helminth infection and colon cancer can also cause chronic bleeding
ΓÇó	 Folic acid deficiency:

ΓÇô
Causes megaloblastic anaemia (red blood cells are larger than normal)

ΓÇô
Common in pregnancy, infancy, liver disease, and malabsorption disorders
ΓÇó	 Vitamin B12 deficiency:

ΓÇô
Results in pernicious anaemia due to impaired absorption

ΓÇô
Causes include gastrectomy, absence of intrinsic factor, or malabsorption (e.g.
non-tropical sprue with villous atrophy)
ΓÇó	 Vitamin C deficiency:

ΓÇô
Leads to scurvy, which can be associated with anaemia
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ271

Haemolytic Anaemia
ΓÇó	 Due to excessive destruction of red blood cells
ΓÇó	 May occur in conditions like sickle cell disease
Anaemia due to Bone Marrow Disorders
ΓÇó	 Aplastic anaemia: failure of the bone marrow to produce red cells
ΓÇó	 Leukaemia: cancer of the blood-forming tissues
ΓÇó	 Drug-induced: some antibiotics (e.g. chloramphenicol), anti-epileptics, and oral
diabetic medications can suppress the bone marrow
ΓÇó	 Exposure to poisons may also impair red cell production
Anaemia of Chronic Disease
ΓÇó	 Seen in chronic conditions such as:

ΓÇô
Renal failure

ΓÇô
Tuberculosis

ΓÇô
Chronic liver disease

ΓÇô
Thyroid disorders

ΓÇô
Rheumatoid arthritis

ΓÇô
Malignancies (cancer)
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Fatigue, pallor, weakness
ΓÇó	 Shortness of breath, palpitations
ΓÇó	 Dizziness, fainting, headache
ΓÇó	 Poor feeding or growth (children)
ΓÇó	 Irritability or behavioural changes
ΓÇó	 Delayed development or school problemsΓÇó Heavy or prolonged menstruation
(women)
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Check haemoglobin using lab or haemoglobinometer if available
ΓÇó	 Assess and triage ΓÇô check vital signs
ΓÇó	 If signs of severe anaemia (extreme pallor, fainting, rapid breathing), call for help
and consult MO urgently
ΓÇó	 Stop contributing medications (e.g. NSAIDs, aspirin)
ΓÇó	 Deworm all patients:

ΓÇô
Albendazole 400 mg stat, repeat after 1 week

ΓÇô
OR Mebendazole 100 mg twice daily for 3 days, repeat after 1 week
272ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 History: recent or chronic bleeding, nutrition, medication, chronic illness
ΓÇó	 Full physical exam:

ΓÇô
Skin, conjunctiva, palms for pallor

ΓÇô
Liver, spleen, lymph nodes

ΓÇô
Rectal/pelvic exam in adults if bleeding suspected

ΓÇô
Growth chart and milestones in children
ΓÇó	 Ask about:

ΓÇô
Menorrhagia, post-partum bleeding

ΓÇô
Abdominal pain or past surgeries

ΓÇô
Diet and malnutrition

ΓÇô
Chronic infections (TB, liver or kidney disease)
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 If Hb low and likely cause is known (e.g. iron deficiency), treat accordingly
ΓÇó	 If due to deficiency of iron, folate or vitamin B12 ΓÇô give supplementation and treat
cause
ΓÇó	 The underlying cause must be identified (e.g. bleeding, malabsorption, chronic
disease)
Iron therapy (first-line)
ΓÇó	 Adults:

ΓÇô
Ferrous sulphate 300 mg orally twice daily after meals

ΓÇô
Once Hb is normal, reduce to 1 tablet daily ├ù 6 months
ΓÇó	 Children:

ΓÇô
Dose as per age and weight ΓÇô typically 3ΓÇô6 mg/kg/day of elemental iron
divided twice daily

ΓÇô
Include folic acid and Vitamin C
ΓÇó	 Pregnancy:

ΓÇô
Ferrous sulphate with folic acid (200 mg iron + 350 mcg folate) ΓÇô 1 tablet daily
throughout pregnancy
Add-on treatments
ΓÇó	 Vitamin C┬áΓÇô 100 mg orally 3
times daily
ΓÇó	 Vitamin A┬áΓÇô 200,000 IU orally
stat in malnourished children
ΓÇó	 Folic acid┬áΓÇô Add if poor diet,
pregnancy, or megaloblastic anaemia suspected
ΓÇó	 Vitamin B12┬áΓÇô Only on doctor
advice, for confirmed B12 deficiency
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ273

Dietary advice
ΓÇó	 Encourage iron-rich foods: liver, meat, fish, leafy greens, legumes, eggs
ΓÇó	 Give small, frequent meals in children
If no improvement
ΓÇó	 Hb should rise ~1g/dL per week
ΓÇó	 If no improvement or relapse after treatment, refer to hospital
ΓÇó	 If patient becomes symptomatic or has unclear cause, refer to MO
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Weekly review for 1 month
ΓÇó	 Monitor clinical improvement and weight gain (children)
ΓÇó	 Reinforce adherence to iron and diet
ΓÇó	 Continue therapy for 3ΓÇô6 months to rebuild iron stores
6.	 Referral/Consultation
ΓÇó	 If severe anaemia, no improvement after 2ΓÇô3 weeks, or cause unclear
ΓÇó	 If symptoms worsen (e.g. fainting, breathlessness)
ΓÇó	 Suspected bone marrow disorders, leukaemia, or chronic disease
ΓÇó	 Children with delayed development or failure to thrive despite treatment
274ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Oedema ΓÇô Adult/Child
Background
Oedema is the accumulation of fluid in the tissues, usually seen as swelling. It may
be generalised or localised. Oedema can be a sign of underlying systemic disease
and must be evaluated carefully. Bilateral oedema often suggests systemic causes
such as cardiac, renal, or hepatic disease, while unilateral oedema may point to local
causes such as infection or thrombosis.
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Consider serious causes such as heart failure, nephrotic syndrome, renal failure,
liver disease, or malnutrition.
ΓÇó	 In pregnant women, assess for pre-eclampsia.
ΓÇó	 Swelling of one leg may be due to local causes including infection, injury, or vein
thrombosis.
ΓÇó	 Refer significant oedema for review at central hospital.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Swelling of both legs, face, abdomen, or eyelids
ΓÇó	 Shortness of breath, fatigue, distended neck veins (suggesting heart failure)
ΓÇó	 Jaundice or ascites (suggesting liver disease)
ΓÇó	 Pallor, tiredness, dizziness (suggesting anaemia)
ΓÇó	 Swelling of arms and legs with skin changes in children (suggesting Kwashiorkor)
ΓÇó	 Ankle swelling in pregnancy (physiological or pre-eclampsia)
ΓÇó	 Swelling of only one leg ΓÇô may be due to cellulitis, deep vein thrombosis (DVT),
or injury
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Check vital signs and oxygen saturation
ΓÇó	 Triage and prioritise if patient is breathless, confused, or acutely unwell
ΓÇó	 In pregnant women with oedema + raised BP or proteinuria, refer urgently for
suspected pre-eclampsia
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ275

3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Full history including:

ΓÇô
Onset, location, progression of swelling

ΓÇô
Symptoms of heart, kidney or liver disease

ΓÇô
Medication history

ΓÇô
Dietary status

ΓÇô
Pregnancy status
ΓÇó	 Physical examination:

ΓÇô
Assess site and extent of oedema

ΓÇô
Look for signs of heart failure (e.g. crepitations, elevated JVP)

ΓÇô
Examine liver and abdomen for signs of liver disease or ascites

ΓÇô
Check BP and urine (for protein) in pregnant women

ΓÇô
Look for local signs of infection or thrombosis in one leg
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Manage according to likely cause:

ΓÇô
Heart failure: consider diuretics if advised by MO

ΓÇô
Nephrotic syndrome or renal failure: consult MO; may require fluid/salt
restriction

ΓÇô
Liver disease: supportive care; refer

ΓÇô
Kwashiorkor: nutritional rehabilitation

ΓÇô
Pregnancy: assess for pre-eclampsia

ΓÇô
Infection/DVT in one leg: initiate antibiotics if cellulitis; refer if thrombosis
suspected
ΓÇó	 Advise rest with leg elevation where appropriate
ΓÇó	 Monitor urine output and vital signs
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Monitor oedema and underlying cause
ΓÇó	 Adjust treatment based on clinical course
ΓÇó	 Reassess nutritional status in children or malnourished adults
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Refer to central hospital if:

ΓÇô
Generalised oedema with signs of systemic illness

ΓÇô
No improvement

ΓÇô
Pregnant woman with high BP or proteinuria

ΓÇô
Unexplained or worsening one-leg oedema
276ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Generalised weakness ΓÇô Adult/Child
Background
Generalised weakness is a common complaint and may result from physical illness,
poor nutrition, infection, mental health disorders, or chronic disease. It is important
to distinguish between true muscular weakness and fatigue. Chronic or progressive
symptoms must be evaluated thoroughly to identify the underlying cause.
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Assess for systemic diseases such as anaemia, diabetes, heart failure, infection, or
neurological disorders.
ΓÇó	 Rule out psychiatric causes such as depression or grief.
ΓÇó	 Review nutritional status and medication use.
ΓÇó	 Chronic or unexplained weakness should be referred to a medical officer.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Tiredness, reduced strength, inability to perform usual tasks
ΓÇó	 Difficulty walking, climbing stairs, combing hair
ΓÇó	 Associated symptoms: fever, palpitations, tremors, polyuria, night breathlessness
ΓÇó	 History of chronic illness or recent infection
ΓÇó	 Poor appetite or weight loss
ΓÇó	 Emotional distress, recent bereavement, anxiety
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Take a full history:

ΓÇô
Onset, duration, pattern (general or local)

ΓÇô
Triggers or relieving factors

ΓÇô
Impact on work/daily life

ΓÇô
Recent illnesses, sleep, diet, stress

ΓÇô
Medication or substance use
ΓÇó	 Physical exam including:

ΓÇô
Vitals and general appearance

ΓÇô
Neurological assessment

ΓÇô
Musculoskeletal exam

ΓÇô
Signs of anaemia, infection, or thyroid disease
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ277

4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Address reversible causes:

ΓÇô
Poor sleep or nutrition: improve rest and diet

ΓÇô
Overwork: advise rest and gradual return to activity

ΓÇô
Mild infection: symptomatic treatment
ΓÇó	 Treat specific conditions if identified:

ΓÇô
Anaemia: treat per anaemia protocol

ΓÇô
Diabetes or hypothyroidism: refer to MO

ΓÇô
Cardiac or respiratory issues: assess and refer as needed

ΓÇô
Mental health issues: provide support, consider MO review
ΓÇó	 Reassure if no serious cause found and symptoms are mild
ΓÇó	 Encourage gradual increase in activity and good sleep hygiene
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Reassess within 1ΓÇô2 weeks or sooner if deterioration
ΓÇó	 Monitor weight, activity tolerance, mood and energy levels
ΓÇó	 Repeat full assessment if new symptoms emerge
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Refer to MO if:

ΓÇô
Weakness persists or worsens without clear cause

ΓÇô
Associated with serious signs (fever, weight loss, breathlessness, neurological
symptoms)

ΓÇô
Suspected depression or anxiety requiring more support
278ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Fever ΓÇô Adult
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Fever in adults may have a wide range of causes. A careful clinical history and
examination can help narrow the differential diagnosis.
ΓÇó	 If fever persists for >3 weeks without diagnosis, and evaluation has failed to
identify a cause, the condition may be classified as ΓÇ£fever of unknown originΓÇ¥
(FUO).
ΓÇó	 Refer to medical officer if patient is clinically unwell, deteriorating, or the cause
of fever remains unclear.
Background
ΓÇó	 Fever is a common symptom in adults and may reflect infections, malignancies,
autoimmune conditions, or other systemic disorders. Persistent or unexplained
fever, particularly when accompanied by weight loss, night sweats, or
localising symptoms, warrants systematic evaluation. Clinical features may help
distinguish between infectious and non-infectious causes, such as malignancy or
autoimmune disease.
1.
May present with (see table on following pages)
ΓÇó	 Fever (subjective or documented ΓëÑ38┬░C)
ΓÇó	 Chills, night sweats, weight loss, anorexia
ΓÇó	 Localised pain (headache, abdominal pain, back pain, sore throat)
ΓÇó	 Fatigue, generalised weakness
ΓÇó	 Cough, hemoptysis
ΓÇó	 Rash, myalgias, joint pain
ΓÇó	 Urinary symptoms
ΓÇó	 Neurological symptoms (confusion, headache, visual changes)
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Assess and stabilise airway, breathing, circulation (ABC) if acutely unwell
ΓÇó	 Monitor vital signs: temperature, heart rate, respiratory rate, blood pressure,
oxygen saturation
ΓÇó	 Commence IV fluids if hypotensive or dehydrated
ΓÇó	 Administer antipyretics (e.g. paracetamol 1g orally every 6 hours if needed)
ΓÇó	 Isolate patient if suspicion of a contagious infection (e.g. tuberculosis, typhoid,
viral hemorrhagic fever)
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ279

3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Obtain detailed history:

ΓÇô
Onset and duration of fever

ΓÇô
Associated symptoms (see above)

ΓÇô
Recent travel, exposures (e.g. TB contacts, animals, unsafe food or water)

ΓÇô
Immunocompromised status (HIV, diabetes, steroid use)

ΓÇô
Vaccination history

ΓÇô
Medication history

ΓÇô
Menstrual and sexual history in women
ΓÇó	 Perform physical examination:

ΓÇô
Look for lymphadenopathy, jaundice, rashes, hepatosplenomegaly

ΓÇô
Cardiac exam: new murmurs (suggestive of endocarditis)

ΓÇô
Respiratory exam: crackles, reduced air entry (TB or pneumonia)

ΓÇô
Neurological exam: meningism, confusion

ΓÇô
Abdominal exam: tenderness, guarding, rebound, ascites

ΓÇô
Genital and rectal exam as appropriate
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Treat empirically only if a likely diagnosis is made or patient is severely ill
ΓÇó	 Common syndromes and actions:

ΓÇô
Suspected malaria: Refer and commence antimalarials if rapid test or thick
smear is positive

ΓÇô
Suspected pneumonia: Consider antibiotics (e.g. amoxicillin or doxycycline)

ΓÇô
Suspected TB: Refer for sputum testing and evaluation

ΓÇô
Suspected UTI: Treat with appropriate antibiotics (e.g. nitrofurantoin or
trimethoprim)

ΓÇô
Suspected typhoid: Ceftriaxone IV or azithromycin orally

ΓÇô
Suspected endocarditis: Refer urgently for medical review and blood cultures
ΓÇó	 Provide supportive care: fluids, nutrition, rest
ΓÇó	 Monitor progress daily
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Review in 2ΓÇô3 days or sooner if symptoms worsen
ΓÇó	 If no improvement after 5ΓÇô7 days of empirical treatment, consult medical officer
ΓÇó	 Investigate further if fever continues beyond 2 weeks
6.	 Referral/Consultation
Refer to a doctor or hospital if:
ΓÇó	 Fever >7 days with no clear cause
ΓÇó	 Severe or rapidly worsening symptoms
ΓÇó	 Suspected endocarditis, meningitis, or TB
ΓÇó	 Known malignancy or immunocompromised patient
ΓÇó	 Need for further laboratory investigations or imaging

Non-Communicable Disease (NCD)
Assessment and management
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Assess all adult patients for common NCDs: obesity, hypertension, and diabetes.
ΓÇó	 Use risk-based approach for prevention and treatment.
ΓÇó	 Counsel patients on lifestyle changes and adherence to medications.
ΓÇó	 Refer to hospital or physician when urgent criteria are met.
Background
ΓÇó	 Non-communicable diseases (NCDs) are a major public health concern in
Kiribati. The most prevalent NCDs include cardiovascular diseases, diabetes,
and associated metabolic conditions such as obesity and dyslipidemia.
Effective management requires a comprehensive approach including lifestyle
interventions, medication adherence, regular screening, and timely referral.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 General fatigue or weakness
ΓÇó	 Excess weight or central obesity
ΓÇó	 Headaches, dizziness
ΓÇó	 Blurred vision or visual changes
ΓÇó	 Excessive thirst, hunger, urination
ΓÇó	 Sores on feet or infections that heal slowly
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Check vital signs: BP, HR, weight, height (calculate BMI)
ΓÇó	 Measure random or fasting blood glucose
ΓÇó	 Ask about lifestyle, symptoms, family history
ΓÇó	 If symptoms suggest urgent referral (e.g. BP >200/120 or blood glucose >30
mmol/L), refer immediately
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 BMI:

ΓÇô
Overweight: 25.0ΓÇô29.9

ΓÇô
Obesity: ΓëÑ30
284ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

How to measure BMI and screen for obesity
MEASURE BODY MASS INDEX (BMI):
ΓÇó	 Using the adult height board, take the clientΓÇÖs height in centimetres.
ΓÇó	 Using the beam balance (weighing scale), take the clientΓÇÖs weight in kilograms.
ΓÇó	 Use the BMI chart or the formula below to compute for the BMI
BMI = weight (kg) ├╖ height (m)2     or
         = weight (kg) ├╖ height (cm) ├╖ height (cm) x 10,000
CLASSIFY
If the clientΓÇÖs BMI is equal to or above 30, the client is obese and is AT RISK.

FBS ΓëÑ6.1 mmol/L or RBS ΓëÑ11.1 mmol/L = raised

ΓÇô
Confirm DM if FBS >7.0 mmol/L or PPBS >11.1 mmol/L twice

4.	 Obesity
ΓÇó	 Counsel on dietary changes, portion control, physical activity
ΓÇó	 Monitor BMI and waist circumference
5.	 Management of Hypertension
ΓÇó	 Start treatment within 4 weeks of diagnosis
ΓÇó	 &lt;65 yrs: HCTZ 12.5ΓÇô25 mg/day or Enalapril 5ΓÇô20 mg/day
ΓÇó ΓëÑ65 yrs: Amlodipine 2.5ΓÇô10 mg/day or HCTZ
ΓÇó	 Use combinations as needed (e.g. Enalapril + HCTZ)
ΓÇó	 Avoid Atenolol in asthma; avoid HCTZ in gout

6.	 Management of Diabetes
ΓÇó	 Metformin 500 mg bd with meals (max 1 g tid)
ΓÇó	 Add Glibenclamide or Glipizide if uncontrolled
ΓÇó	 If still uncontrolled, add insulin (Isophane 10U at night)
ΓÇó	 Monitor fasting BGL daily, titrate as needed
Supportive Management
ΓÇó	 Statins (Simvastatin/Atorvastatin) for Type 2 DM ΓëÑ40 yrs or TC ΓëÑ8 mmol/L
ΓÇó	 Aspirin 75ΓÇô100 mg if CVD risk ΓëÑ20% (no contraindications)
ΓÇó	 Inspect feet every visit; perform microfilament test every 6 months
ΓÇó	 Eye exam yearly; refer for vision changes
ΓÇó	 Check urine protein yearly

ΓÇó	 Consider relaxing target HbA1c
level if side effects of achieving
this put the patient at high risk of
hypoglycaemia consequences (e.g.
drivers, people operating machinery),
if advanced complications or other
comorbidities are present and/or life
expectancy is reduced.

MANAGEMENT OF HYPOGLYCAEMIA (EMERGENCY)
(PG. 48 OF PEN GUIDELINE.)
ΓÇó	 Hypoglycaemia is blood glucose level of 4mmol/L or less
ΓÇó	 The signs and symptoms of hypoglycaemia are:

ΓÇô
Cold sweat

ΓÇô
Tremor

ΓÇô
Tingling in the lips or mouth

ΓÇô
Hunger

ΓÇô
Palpitations

ΓÇô
Pounding headache

ΓÇô
Feeling faint or hungry

ΓÇô
Light headedness

ΓÇô
Irritability

ΓÇô
Confusion

ΓÇô
Decreased or loss of consciousness
ΓÇó	 If the patient is unconscious do not administer anything orally.  Seek medical
attention immediately.
ΓÇó	 If the patient experiences symptoms of hypoglycaemia or a blood glucose level
of 4mmol/L or less and is conscious:
ΓÇó	 Administer quick-acting sugar immediately.   Examples of quick-acting sugar
include:

ΓÇô
2 teaspoons of sugar

ΓÇô
2 teaspoons of honey or jam

ΓÇô
1 glass of fruit juice

ΓÇô
 ┬╜ can of soft drink

ΓÇô
6 jelly beans or soft lollies

ΓÇô
1 ripe banana
ΓÇó	 Recheck blood glucose level in 15 minutes, if available.
ΓÇó	 If blood glucose level is Γëñ4mmol/L or the patient is not feeling better, give
quick-acting sugar again or give long acting carbohydrate or let the patient
have meal if it is due. Example of long acting carbohydrates include:

ΓÇô
1 piece of fruit

ΓÇô
Sandwich

ΓÇô
Biscuits
ΓÇó	 Let the patient rest.
ΓÇó	 Identify the cause of hypoglycaemia, which may include:

ΓÇô
Missing a meal, delayed meal or no carbohydrate in previous meal

ΓÇô
Physical activity or exercise

ΓÇô
Too much oral hypoglycaemic or insulin

ΓÇô
Too much intake of alcohol or intake of alcohol on an empty stomach
ΓÇó	 Advise the patient to carry quick-acting sugar with them at all times.
*Improving Diabetes Care, A Training Program for the Health Workers in Nauru.
World Diabetes Foundation Project, 2007.
290ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

MANAGEMENT OF DIABETIC KETOACIDOSIS (EMERGENCY)
(PG. 47 OF PEN GUIDELINE.)
ΓÇó	 Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) is characterized by ketosis, hyperglycaemia and
acidosis. DKA occurs predominantly in Type 1 diabetic patients but can occur
in Type 2 diabetes.
ΓÇó	 The common precipitating factors of DKA are omission of insulin, infection
or sepsis, drugs such as corticosteroids and recent trauma or acute coronary
event.
ΓÇó	 Symptoms include:

ΓÇô
Vomiting

ΓÇô
Abdominal pain

ΓÇô
KussmaulΓÇÖs breathing (initially rapid and shallow but as acidosis worsens,
breathing becomes deep and laboured)

ΓÇô
Mental confusion progressing to coma

ΓÇô
Dehydration

ΓÇô
Ketotic breath
ΓÇó	 Patients also notice rapid weight loss which can be a marker for dehydration. It
is necessary to test urine for moderate to large ketone bodies.
ΓÇó	 Management should be undertaken urgently, and the most important
intervention is
FLUID REPLACEMENT
ΓÇó	 Administer intravenous normal saline ΓÇô first litre for 30 minutes, second later
for one hour, third litre for 2 hours and fourth later for 4 hours.
ΓÇó	 Transfer patient to the hospital once condition is stabilized.
ΓÇó	 In case that an IV access cannot be established, give a short-acting insulin e.g.
Actrapid IM at 8 units/hour. Insulin therapy can be started subsequently.
*RMI National Diabetes Guidelines 2016
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ291

THE RECOMMENDED GUIDELINES ON ANTI-PLATELET DRUG
(PG.43 OF THE PEN GUIDELINE)
All individuals with established CVD or CVD risk ΓëÑ20% should be given
low-dose Aspirin (start at 75mg ΓÇô 100mg) if there are no contraindications
(e.g. hypersensitivity to aspirin, bleeding GI ulcers, haemorrhagic diathesis,
haemorrhoids, thrombocytopenia, ulcerative colitis etc.)

disease.
All other individuals need to be managed according to the cardiovascular risk (10-
year risk of cardiovascular event &lt;5%, 5 to &lt;10%, 10 to &lt;20%, ΓëÑ20%)
