7.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Monthly for BP or glucose control
ΓÇó	 Annually for lipid profile, proteinuria, foot and eye checks
ΓÇó	 Reinforce lifestyle advice at each visit
8.	 Referral/Consultation Urgent referral to hospital if:
ΓÇó	 BP >200/120 mmHg after rest
ΓÇó	 Blood glucose >30 mmol/L or >20 mmol/L with symptoms
ΓÇó	 Diabetic foot ulcers, severe infection, or vision loss
ΓÇó	 New chest pain, stroke/TIA symptoms
Refer to physician if:
ΓÇó	 Persistent BP >140/90 in people &lt;40 yrs
ΓÇó	 Target organ damage or known CVD
ΓÇó	 Poor glucose control despite oral meds
ΓÇó	 Newly diagnosed DM with urine ketones 2+
ΓÇó	 Total cholesterol >8 mmol/L
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ293

Cardiovascular and
respiratory disorders

Congestive cardiac failure - Adult
Recommend
ΓÇó	 CHF is a clinical syndrome that may develop slowly over months or years and is
due to the heart's inability to pump enough blood to meet the bodyΓÇÖs needs.
ΓÇó	 It is vital to identify and manage both the underlying cause and any precipitating
factors.
ΓÇó	 Early diagnosis and appropriate treatment can improve quality of life and reduce
morbidity and mortality.
Background
Congestive Heart Failure (CHF) is a condition caused by structural or functional
abnormalities of the heart leading to impaired cardiac output. It is not a disease
itself, but a syndrome resulting from other cardiac or systemic conditions.
CHF is commonly due to conditions such as ischaemic heart disease, valvular heart
disease, hypertension, hyperthyroidism, or cardiomyopathy. Less common causes
include congenital heart disease, infective endocarditis, myocarditis, cor pulmonale,
and pericardial disease. In many cases, CHF is in a compensated state until
decompensated by precipitating factors.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Shortness of breath on exertion or at rest
ΓÇó	 Orthopnoea or paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnoea
ΓÇó	 Fatigue or weakness
ΓÇó	 Peripheral oedema
ΓÇó	 Elevated jugular venous pressure
ΓÇó	 Hepatomegaly or ascites
ΓÇó	 Weight gain from fluid retention
2.	 Immediate management If severe cardiac failure:
ΓÇó	 Sit patient upright
ΓÇó	 Give oxygen (1ΓÇô3 L/min if available)
ΓÇó	 Administer Frusemide 40 mg IM or IV slowly over 10 minutes
ΓÇó	 Contact medical officer urgently
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ295

3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Take history of cardiac disease, hypertension, medications, salt/fluid intake,
adherence
ΓÇó	 Examine for signs of fluid overload, arrhythmia, murmurs, infection
ΓÇó	 Investigations (if available):

ΓÇô
CBC

ΓÇô
Electrolytes and urea

ΓÇô
ECG

ΓÇô
Chest X-ray (if accessible)
4.	 Management
Non-pharmacological:
ΓÇó	 Salt restriction (no added salt)
ΓÇó	 Weight reduction if overweight
ΓÇó	 Fluid restriction only if dilutional hyponatraemia (limit to 1.5 L/day)
ΓÇó	 Daily weights before breakfast with a target weight loss of 05.-1kg/day. If target
weight loss not achieved it indicates inadequate diuresis and may push up
frusemide dose if BP >90/60.
ΓÇó	 Educate on medication adherence and monitoring symptoms
Pharmacological (mild to moderate heart failure):
ΓÇó	 Frusemide 40ΓÇô80 mg orally daily
ΓÇó	 Enalapril 2.5 mg daily, increasing gradually to a max of 20 mg daily

ΓÇô
Monitor for hypotension, especially in elderly, dehydrated, or hyponatraemic
patients
ΓÇó	 If still symptomatic, add:

ΓÇô
Digoxin 0.0625ΓÇô0.5 mg orally daily (adjust dose based on age and renal
function)
Other medications (if available):
ΓÇó	 Spironolactone, thiazide diuretics
ΓÇó	 Beta-blockers (carvedilol, metoprolol, bisoprolol)
ΓÇó	 Isosorbide dinitrate
ΓÇó	 Hydralazine
5. Precipitating factors
ΓÇó	 Poor medication adherence
ΓÇó	 Excess salt or fluid intake
ΓÇó	 NSAIDs, steroids, antidepressants, verapamil
ΓÇó	 Infections, especially respiratory
ΓÇó	 Anaemia, hyperthyroidism, uncontrolled hypertension
ΓÇó	 Arrhythmias (especially atrial fibrillation)
296ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

ΓÇó	 Acute myocardial infarction
ΓÇó	 Pregnancy
ΓÇó	 Overexertion
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Monitor clinical status, weight, adherence, side effects
ΓÇó	 Adjust medications as needed
ΓÇó	 Reinforce lifestyle modifications
ΓÇó	 Ensure follow-up with medical officer
6.	 Referral / Consultation
ΓÇó	 Always consult a medical officer when managing CHF
ΓÇó	 Refer urgently if:

ΓÇô
No response to treatment

ΓÇô
Worsening symptoms

ΓÇô
Suspected underlying cause needing further investigation
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ297

Pneumonia - Adult
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Pneumonia is a common and potentially serious lung infection requiring timely
recognition and appropriate management.
ΓÇó	 Antibiotic treatment should be initiated early in suspected cases.
ΓÇó	 Patients with moderate or severe pneumonia may require referral or
hospitalisation.
Background
Pneumonia is an acute infection of the lung parenchyma, often presenting with
cough, fever, pleuritic chest pain, and shortness of breath. It is caused by a range of
pathogens, including bacteria, viruses, and fungi, and its severity can vary widely.
Risk factors include chronic illness, advanced age, recent travel, hazardous alcohol
use, and exposure to environmental sources (e.g. muddy soil/water).
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Pleuritic chest pain
ΓÇó	 Fever, rigors
ΓÇó	 Cough with sputum production, shortness of breath
ΓÇó	 Fatigue, muscle aches or generalised body pain
2.	 Immediate management Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 ΓÇó
Take detailed history, including:

ΓÇô
Chest pain, fever, rigors, night sweats

ΓÇô
Cough and sputum characteristics (purulent or coloured)

ΓÇô
Shortness of breath, increased respiratory rate

ΓÇô
Existing chronic illnesses (e.g. diabetes, CKD, CLD), immunocompromised
status

ΓÇô
Hazardous alcohol intake

ΓÇô
Recent travel or exposure to muddy soil or water
ΓÇó	 Physical examination:

ΓÇô
Observe work of breathing

ΓÇô
Listen for decreased air entry, wheeze, crackles (do they clear with cough?)

ΓÇô
Assess for dullness on percussion

ΓÇô
Inspect skin for ulcers, abscesses, non-healing sores
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Contact MO in all cases for further advice

ΓÇô
May recommend antibiotics, sputum for MCS ┬▒ PCR, IV fluids, or evacuation
298ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management Evacuation/hospitalisation is needed
if any of the following:
ΓÇó	 RR ΓëÑ 25/min, HR ΓëÑ 100 bpm, sBP &lt; 90 mmHg
ΓÇó	 SpO2 &lt; 92% on room air (or lower than normal in COPD)
ΓÇó	 Acute confusion
ΓÇó	 Comorbidities, social circumstances, or age ΓëÑ 65 years
CORB-65 Severity Score
ΓÇó	 C: acute Confusion
ΓÇó	 O: Oxygen saturation Γëñ 90%
ΓÇó	 R: Respiratory rate ΓëÑ 30/min
ΓÇó	 B: low Blood pressure (systolic &lt; 90 mmHg or diastolic Γëñ 60 mmHg)
ΓÇó	 Score ΓëÑ 2 suggests severe CAP and need for inpatient management
Antibiotic Treatment
ΓÇó	 Outpatient (if mild):

ΓÇô
Oral amoxicillin or daily IM procaine penicillin

ΓÇô
If penicillin allergy: doxycycline orally
ΓÇó	 Inpatient (severe or moderate with CORB-65 ΓëÑ 2):

ΓÇô
Benzylpenicillin 2MU IV every 6 hours

ΓÇô
PLUS doxycycline 100 mg orally twice daily

ΓÇô
Discontinue IV once clinically improving and switch to oral antibiotics to
complete course
Advise:
ΓÇó	 Fever should reduce within a few days
ΓÇó	 Appetite and general wellbeing should improve progressively
ΓÇó	 Other symptoms may take weeks to fully resolve
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Daily review until improvement, or earlier if symptoms worsen
ΓÇó	 Consult MO if no improvement or worsening
ΓÇó	 Continue follow-up at next scheduled clinic visit
6.	 Referral/Consultation
ΓÇó	 Refer if:

ΓÇô
No improvement after 3 days

ΓÇô
Severe pneumonia suspected
References:
ΓÇó	 Adapted from current clinical guidelines on pneumonia assessment and
management.
ΓÇó	 CORB-65 tool adapted from Australian pneumonia study cohort.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ299

Pleural effusion - Adult
Recommend
Pleural effusion is a potentially serious condition that may indicate infection,
heart failure, or malignancy. Evaluation of the underlying cause and referral for
definitive management is essential. If an underlying lung infection is suspected, treat
empirically for pneumonia. Provide oxygen support if SpOΓéé is less than 94%.
Background
Pleural effusion is the collection of fluid in the pleural space surrounding the lungs.
It commonly occurs due to tuberculosis, congestive heart failure, malignancy, or
bacterial infections. Symptoms can be subtle but may include cough, shortness of
breath, and chest discomfort. Diagnosis requires clinical suspicion and confirmation
via chest examination or imaging.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Shortness of breath, especially on exertion
ΓÇó	 Chest heaviness or discomfort
ΓÇó	 Cough, which may be dry or productive
ΓÇó	 Fever or night sweats, especially if due to infection or TB
ΓÇó	 General fatigue
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Assess airway and breathing
ΓÇó	 Support the patient in a comfortable sitting position
ΓÇó	 Administer oxygen if SpOΓéé is less than 94%
ΓÇó	 Take history:
	ΓÇ║
Duration of symptoms
	ΓÇ║
History of TB, heart failure, or cancer
	ΓÇ║
Night sweats, weight loss, recent illness
ΓÇó	 Physical examination:
	ΓÇ║
Reduced chest movement on affected side
	ΓÇ║
Dullness to percussion over fluid
	ΓÇ║
Decreased or absent breath sounds
	ΓÇ║
Reduced vocal fremitus
	ΓÇ║
Possible tracheal deviation in large effusions
ΓÇó	 Check for associated signs:
	ΓÇ║
Lymphadenopathy
	ΓÇ║
Peripheral oedema (suggestive of heart failure)
	ΓÇ║
Signs of systemic disease
300ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Perform full respiratory and cardiovascular exam
ΓÇó	 Assess vital signs
ΓÇó	 If signs of infection are present (e.g. fever, purulent sputum, crackles), treat
empirically for pneumonia while awaiting referral
ΓÇó	 Consider differential diagnoses: pneumonia, TB, cancer, heart failure
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Consult MO in all suspected cases
ΓÇó	 Refer to hospital or doctor for further assessment and possible thoracocentesis
ΓÇó	 Do not attempt aspiration or drainage in PHC setting without training and
supervision
ΓÇó	 Provide oxygen therapy if SpOΓéé &lt; 94%
ΓÇó	 If pneumonia is suspected, start empiric antibiotic treatment as per pneumonia
protocol
ΓÇó	 Supportive care: monitoring and symptom relief
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Review at next clinic visit if already referred
ΓÇó	 Monitor for worsening breathlessness or new symptoms
ΓÇó	 Support adherence to TB or heart failure treatment if initiated
5.	 Referral/Consultation
ΓÇó	 Refer urgently if:

ΓÇô
Moderate to large effusion suspected

ΓÇô
Respiratory distress

ΓÇô
Suspected TB or malignancy

ΓÇô
No clear cause found

ΓÇô
Patient fails to respond to initial antibiotic treatment
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ301

Dyspnoea ΓÇô Adult
Recommend
Dyspnoea is a serious clinical symptom that may be caused by cardiac or
respiratory conditions. Early identification of the underlying cause is important
for appropriate management. Sudden onset dyspnoea, especially in children, may
indicate foreign body aspiration and requires urgent attention.
Background
Dyspnoea is defined as an uncomfortable awareness of breathing or difficulty in
breathing. It can result from disease of the lungs, heart, or nervous system. Common
causes include asthma, pneumonia, congestive heart failure, pulmonary embolism,
anaemia, or pleural effusion. It is essential to distinguish between cardiac and
respiratory origins of breathlessness for appropriate management.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Shortness of breath during activity or at rest
ΓÇó	 Chest tightness (may be mistaken for pain)
ΓÇó	 Waking at night gasping for breath (paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnoea)
ΓÇó	 Needing multiple pillows to sleep (orthopnoea)
ΓÇó	 Wheeze, cough (dry or productive)
ΓÇó	 Fatigue, reduced exercise tolerance
ΓÇó	 Anxiety, rapid breathing, fast heartbeat
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Assess airway, breathing, and circulation
ΓÇó	 Provide oxygen if available (1ΓÇô3 litres per minute)
ΓÇó	 Support patient in a sitting position
ΓÇó	 Ask about and document key symptoms and history:

ΓÇô
Onset, duration, triggers (e.g. exercise, allergens)

ΓÇô
History of heart disease or respiratory illness

ΓÇô
Smoking or occupational exposures

ΓÇô
Presence of cough, sputum (colour, amount)

ΓÇô
Orthopnoea or paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnoea
ΓÇó	 Do full physical examination:

ΓÇô
Vital signs: RR, SpOΓéé, HR, BP, temperature

ΓÇô
Chest exam: wheeze, crackles, reduced breath sounds, dullness to percussion

ΓÇô
Cardiac exam: murmur, elevated JVP, pedal oedema

ΓÇô
General appearance and work of breathing
ΓÇó	 Consult MO urgently if:

ΓÇô
Acute severe distress

ΓÇô
Hypoxia or cyanosis
	ΓÇ║
Suspected cardiac failure or pulmonary embolism
	ΓÇ║
No clear diagnosis after assessment
302ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Distinguish between likely cardiac and respiratory causes:

ΓÇô
Cardiac: history of heart failure, dry cough, white frothy sputum, orthopnoea,
peripheral oedema, crepitations, improved with diuretics

ΓÇô
Respiratory: history of asthma or chronic lung disease, purulent sputum,
wheeze, improved with bronchodilators

ΓÇô
PE: sudden onset, tachypnoea, tachycardia, immobility history
ΓÇó	 Identify red flags:

ΓÇô
Confusion, cyanosis, very low SpOΓéé, inability to speak full sentences
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Start initial treatment based on suspected cause (e.g. salbutamol for asthma,
frusemide for heart failure)
ΓÇó	 Provide oxygen if desaturation is present
ΓÇó	 Consult MO for further management or referral
ΓÇó	 Prepare for evacuation if severe symptoms or uncertain diagnosis
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Monitor vital signs regularly
ΓÇó	 Reassess respiratory status every 2ΓÇô4 hours
ΓÇó	 Review in clinic once stable to confirm underlying diagnosis and ongoing
treatment
6.	 Referral/Consultation
ΓÇó	 Refer to medical officer for all moderate to severe cases or if diagnosis unclear
ΓÇó	 Evacuate if respiratory distress does not improve with initial treatment or if
hypoxia persists
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ303

Ear, Nose and Throat
(ENT)

Ear assessment - Adult/Child
Recommendations
ΓÇó	 Check every childΓÇÖs ears at each clinic visit
ΓÇó	 Encourage families to bring their child to the clinic if they notice any ear
symptoms or have concerns about hearing or speech development
ΓÇó	 If hearing loss is suspected, liaise with the school or daycare to support the
child with classroom strategies such as sound field amplification or seating
arrangements
Background
ΓÇó	 Otitis media (OM) is inflammation or infection of the middle ear. In children,
OM may develop within the first months of life and often becomes recurrent or
chronic, potentially causing hearing loss
ΓÇó	 Hearing loss can significantly affect speech and language development and
educational outcomes
Resources
ΓÇó	 Care for KidsΓÇÖ Ears ΓÇô Australian Government resources for families and carers
Website: www.careforkidsears.health.gov.au/internet/cfke/publishing.nsf/
Content/Home
ΓÇó	 Otitis Media Guidelines and App ΓÇô Clinical guidance and mobile app for
diagnosis and management
Website: https://otitismediaguidelines.com/#/start-main
Preventing Ear Infections
ΓÇó	 Practise regular handwashing, especially after nose blowing or coughing
ΓÇó	 Keep hands and face free of nasal discharge
ΓÇó	 Ensure children are up to date with pneumococcal and influenza vaccinations
ΓÇó	 Teach and model ΓÇÿBlow, Breathe, CoughΓÇÖ technique
ΓÇó	 Breastfeed if possible; if bottle feeding, use an upright feeding position (avoid
feeding while lying flat)
ΓÇó	 Avoid the use of dummies for children over 6 months of age
ΓÇó	 Keep sick children away from babies
ΓÇó	 Avoid exposing children to tobacco smoke

May present with1

ΓÇô
trauma/blow to ear eg slap, diving, object poked in3

ΓÇô
Any other symptoms eg:
	ΓÇ║
runny nose, sore throat, cough

Get past history:

Examine ears 1,4,6

is ear pushed out/forwards

pull pinna to straighten canal: adult - up, out and back; child - down and back

if pus/discharge - gently clean with Tissue spears first

if impacted wax prevents examination consider syringing with warm water (if

ΓÇô
note: do NOT irrigate if peforation likely, diabetes, immunocompromised or on

swelling, redness, lump(s), flakes/scales

ΓÇô

ΓÇô
colour - pearly grey, yellow/amber, pink/red, white (pus),white chalky patches
(scarring)

ΓÇô
translucency - shiny/translucent (normal), opaque, dull/cloudy, fluid/bubbles

ΓÇô

ΓÇô
handle of malleus is prominent if retracted, or not seen well if bulging6

to ΓÇÿlargeΓÇÖ size):

ΓÇô
a severely retracted eardrum, retraction pocket or healed hole might be

ΓÇô
grommet
Eardrum Red Flags
If any of the following signs are observed, further assessment is required:

ΓÇó	 Hearing Test:
ΓÇ£The AudiometerΓÇ¥ is the machine measures the sound levels that people can hear
in units called decibels in hearing level (dB HL). For example, people with normal
hearing may be able to hear a sound at a level of 0 decibel (0 dB HL). A person
with hearing loss may only be able to hear a sound at a level of 50 decibels
which is in the range for moderate hearing impairment. This test available now at
the central hospital (TCH-Nawerewere).
ΓÇó	 WeberΓÇÖs and RinneΓÇÖs tests:
These tests assess air conduction (AC) and bone conduction (BC) and are used
to help delineate whether hearing loss is sensorineural (SNHL)or conductive
(CHL) in origin
Check nose, throat, chest + other systems as needed.

Nose bleed/epistaxis - adult/child
Background
ΓÇó	 95% of all nose bleeds are anterior and are usually managed by simple
compression of the mose.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Nose bleed
2.	 Immediate management1
ALERT suspect Button battery insertion or ingestion in child with blood around
nostrils. If lodged in the eosophagus, it can burn a hole through to the aorta causing
catastrophic haemorrhage.
ΓÇó	 DRSABCD
ΓÇó	 If active bleeding + SHOCKED2 (major obvious epistaxis in process)
(cool, ΓåæHR , CRT > 2 seconds):

ΓÇô
Admit

ΓÇô
urgently consult MA/NP + urgent evacuation

ΓÇô
insert Anterior nasal pack (use cotton or ribbon gauge) soaked with
adrenaline 0.5mg (5mls of 1:10,000).

ΓÇô
Insert 2 large IV cannula

ΓÇô
start IVF resuscitation with sodium chloride 0.9% 10ΓÇô20 mL/kg

ΓÇô
If not controlled,
	ΓÇ║
Then apply Posterior nasal pack,  (take out anterior pack first)
	ΓÇ║
Patient positioning sits upright, nose succion.
	ΓÇ║
Insert a lubrication Foley cateter (10-14 Fr) gently passing along the floor of
the nose until t appear at the back of the oropharyngx.
	ΓÇ║
Inflate with a small amount of saline (7-10mls)
	ΓÇ║
Gently pulled forward until the inflated balloon seats firmly against the
posterior nasal opening then insert anterior packing (Cotton/ribbon gauge)
to fill up the front part of the nasal cavity.
	ΓÇ║
Then clamp the catheter infront of the nostril entrance to maintain tension
and prevent dislodgement.

ΓÇô
Antibiotic: Augumentin or Septrin  (when applying posterior nasal packing).
ΓÇó	 If active bleeding + NOT shocked:2

ΓÇô
apply continuous pressure on the lower third of nose with thumb and
forefinger for 5ΓÇô10 minutes

ΓÇô
sit upright, lean forward

ΓÇô
breathe through mouth + spit blood out rather than swallowing

ΓÇô
if not stopped after 10ΓÇô15 minutes:1,2
	ΓÇ║
reapply pressure and reassess (incorrect pressure most common cause of
continued bleeding) if still not stop
	ΓÇ║
insert the anterior nasal pack with adrenaline and continue applying
pressure

ΓÇô
if still bleeding:

ΓÇô
Then apply posterior nasal pack

ΓÇô
continue to manage as per ΓÇÿif active bleeding + shockedΓÇÖ above

ΓÇô
urgently consult MA/NP + urgent evacuation

onset, duration and severity of bleeding

red flags for tumour e.g, nasal blockage, facial pain/swelling, headaches,
deep toothache, teenage male1

nose or facial trauma, nose picking, CPAP use

ΓÇô

┬▒ antibiotics

Nasal pack discharge advice1
ΓÇó	 May have symptoms of a cold while in place eg blocked nose, Γåô smell, facial
pressure, headaches, nasal drainage, tearing from eyes
ΓÇó	 Try not to blow nose. Sneeze with open mouth
ΓÇó	 To avoid Γåæ blood flow to the nose and risk of further bleeding - avoid straining,
lifting > 5kg, strenuous exercise, bending over+ sleep with head slightly elevated
ΓÇó	 Avoid aspirin and ibuprofen (may Γåæ bleeding). Take paracetamol if needed for pain
ΓÇó	 Keep pack moist by spraying with sodium chloride 0.9%

May present with1,2

blow to ear e.g, slap, fall from water skis, diving

ALERT button batteries require urgent removal to prevent necrosis of surrounding

3.	 Clinical assessment 1,2
ΓÇó	 Get history and examine ears/nose
ΓÇó	 Identify exact location, shape and composition of FB
ΓÇó	 If possible, get assistant to help, especially if child (in addition to caregiver)
4.	 Management 1,2
ΓÇó	 Foreign body in the Ear (external auditory canal.)

ΓÇô
Examen ear canal with otoscope or any available light source

ΓÇô
If thereΓÇÖs a foreign body

ΓÇô
Do ear Flushing (you can try flushing at maximum of 4-5)

ΓÇô
If the FB successfully removed then check the other ear.

ΓÇô
If the FB not successfully remove then consult the MA/NP
Note: if the INSECT make sure to kill it first by using some drops of oil or Lignocaine,
then can flush.

REMOVING FB FROM NOSE2
SMALL, SMOOTH
OR SPHERICAL
OBJECTS
ΓÇó	 Self blow/exhale - if child is old enough to cooperate eg
> 3 years, encourage to blow nose while occluding the
nostril opposite to the FB
ΓÇó	 Parents' kiss - ask parent/caregiver to seal the child's
mouth with their mouth, while occluding the unaffected
nostril. Then give a short sharp puff of air
ΓÇó	 Suction - only try if FB visible and not 'lodged'. Use micro
suction tube. Form a solid seal between tube and object.
Be careful not to push FB up nose
ΓÇó	 Magnet device - (if available) for metal object - insert
gently until clicks onto object. Be careful not to push FB
up nose
SOFT,
IRREGULAR,
SMALL OBJECT
ΓÇó	 Forceps - only use if FB visible and looks easy to grasp.
Avoid repeated grasping attempts as may push FB in
further

ΓÇó	 Consult MO/NP
REMOVING FB FROM EAR1
SOFT,
IRREGULAR,
SMALL OBJECT
ΓÇó	 Forceps - only use if FB visible and looks easy to grasp.

further
INSECT OR SMALL
INORGANIC
OBJECT

SMOOTH/
SPHERICAL

How to do Ear Flushing:
Method
ΓÇó	 The patient must sit with the ear
facing the light and you must be
able to see the entrance to the
ear canal clearly.
ΓÇó	 Fill the syringe with warm water
and squirt it hard a few times
back into the water container to
check that it works well and the
tip does not come off.
ΓÇó	 Gently pull the pinna backward
away from the head.
ΓÇó	 Place the tip of the syringe just inside the canal and squirt the water into the
ear canal.
ΓÇó	 Water should be directed around the foreign body. The water will then be behind
the foreign body and should push it out.
ΓÇó	 Catch the water that comes out in a bowl. Check to see what has come out of
the ear canal.
ΓÇó	 Examine the ear canal with an otoscope after every five syringes.
ΓÇó	 Once the foreign body has come out of the ear canal you should be able to see
the ear drum.
ΓÇó	 Check both ears.
NOTE
ΓÇó	 Remember: the water used to syringe the ear MUST be the same as body
temperature ΓÇö 37┬░C, warm but comfortable on the hand.

ΓÇô
o	 If you use cold or hot water for ear flushing, the patient may feel dizzy,
nausea or vomiting.

ΓÇô
o	 If the patient complains of dizziness, stop syringing and make the patient
comfortable with support.
ΓÇó	 DO NOT direct the water straight onto the foreign body.

ΓÇô
o	 This will push the foreign body further into the ear canal.
ΓÇó	 DO NOT SYRINGE if the ear is dry and if you know or suspect there is a
perforation in the ear drum. Refer these patients.

ΓÇô
o	 Syringing a dry perforated ear may lead to suppurative otitis media.
ΓÇó	 If you see any trauma to the ear canal after removal of the foreign body:

ΓÇô
o	 Fill the ear canal with eardrops.

ΓÇô
o	 Give the patient eardrops to take home and use four times each day.

ΓÇô
o	 Check the ear again after two days.
Attention
ΓÇó	 Do not syringe ear if there is pain in the ear or recent trauma.
ΓÇó	 Always look in the ear before syringing. If any pain ΓÇö stop and look again.
ΓÇó	 If CSOM ΓÇö syringe with diluted povidone-iodine 10%.
316ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Ear FB removed - advise to be reviewed:1

ΓÇô
if any symptoms arise ie hearing loss, discharge, marked pain, facial nerve
paralysis or dizziness/feeling off balance. Consult MA/NP ┬▒ ENT referral
ΓÇó	 Nose FB removed - advise to be reviewed:2

ΓÇô
if any symptoms arise ie fever, purulent discharge, nosebleed, facial pain or
swelling. Consult MO/NP ┬▒ ENT referral
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 If FB not successfully removed
ΓÇó	 As above.

Epiglottitis - Adult/Child
 Background1
ΓÇó	 A life-threatening condition caused by infection of the epiglottis + surrounding
area
ΓÇó	 Since the Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib) vaccine, epiglottitis is rare
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 High fever, looks sick
ΓÇó	 Hyperextension of neck, may not move neck
ΓÇó	 Drooling saliva, difficulty swallowing
ΓÇó	 Stridor, muffled voice
ΓÇó	 Restless, unsettled
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Secure airway

ΓÇô
Anticipate any airway obstruction but avoid distressing a child as may
exacerbate symptoms:

ΓÇô
Avoid examining the oral cavity and throat

ΓÇô
allow the child to settle in the position most comfortable.
ΓÇó	 The patient should be kept in an upright position as supine positioning can
aggravate airway obstruction
ΓÇó	 Start urgent IV antibiotic chloramphenicol 25mg/kg up 1g IV 6hrly

ΓÇô
Oral antibiotic if canΓÇÖt assess to iv: amoxicillin clavulanate 500+125 mg (child
15 mg/kg amoxicillin component up to 500 mg) orally, 8-hourly.
ΓÇó	 Corticosteroid:

ΓÇô
Dexamethasone 10 mg IV, as a single dose; repeat at 24 hours if required (not
recommended for children)
ΓÇó	 IV Fluid to maintain hydration, especially if the patient is unable to swallow.
3.	 Clinical assessment1
ΓÇó	 Assess for any upper airway obstruction (stridor, muffle voice, difficulty
breathing)
ΓÇó	 Also looking for drooling, and dysphagia.
ΓÇó	 Sore throat which is progressive rapidly.
ΓÇó	 High grade fever, sick looking.
318ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Continue with Oxygen support, antibiotic and IVF Maintenance.
ΓÇó	 Urgent refer if shows sign of airway obstruction.
5.	 Referral/Consultation
ΓÇó	 Consult every case diagnose with epiglottitis
ΓÇó	 Refer if a patient shows airway compromise.

Mastoiditis - Adult/Child
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Refer all suspected mastoiditis cases urgently.
ΓÇó	 Treat with IV antibiotics if available. If IV access is not possible, give oral
antibiotics while arranging urgent referral.
ΓÇó	 Consult MO for further assessment and referral.
Background
ΓÇó	 Mastoiditis occurs when an ear infection spreads to the mastoid bone (behind
the ear), causing swelling, redness, and a possible collection of pus under the
skin behind the pinna (outer ear).
ΓÇó	 This condition can become serious and requires urgent treatment to prevent
complications.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Pain behind the ear
ΓÇó	 Fever
ΓÇó	 Possible discharge from the ear
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Refer urgently to a health centre for prompt care
ΓÇó	 Administer IV antibiotics if available
ΓÇó	 If IV access is not possible, give oral antibiotics
ΓÇó	 Consult MO urgently
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 History of ear discharge
ΓÇó	 Swelling and redness behind the pinna
ΓÇó	 Pain on pressing the bone behind the pinna (post-auricular pain)
ΓÇó	 Fluctuant swelling (pus under the skin may feel like a fluid-filled sac)
ΓÇó	 Fever
ΓÇó	 Otoscopy may show pus in the ear canal
320ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Admit and start IV antibiotics:

ΓÇô
Cloxacillin 25ΓÇô50 mg/kg IV every 6 hours

ΓÇô
Metronidazole (Flagyl) 500 mg IV every 8 hours

ΓÇô
Gentamicin 5 mg/kg IV once daily for 5 days
ΓÇó	 Ear drops: ciprofloxacin or chloramphenicol 4ΓÇô5 drops twice daily for 7 days
ΓÇó	 Give pain relief
ΓÇó	 Educate on ear hygiene
5.	 Referral / consultation
ΓÇó	 Refer for surgical review (mastoidectomy) where indicated

nose, throat, chest

Adult/Child

ΓÇó	 Wash hands thoroughly before and after the procedure

ΓÇó	 Remove and discard. Repeat with a fresh spear until the ear is dry

Sore throat ΓÇô (Pharyngitis, tonsillitis) -
Adult/Child
Recommendation
ΓÇó	 Treat sore throats with antibiotics promptly in people living in rural + remote
settings and in others who are at high risk of Acute rheumatic Fever and Acute
post streptococcal glomerulonephritis.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Sore throat ┬▒ fever
ΓÇó	 Difficulty swallowing, not eating/drinking as much, croaky voice
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Look for symptoms of airway obstruction/compromise or deep neck space
infection eg peritonsillar abscess (quinsy), including:
ΓÇó	 muffled voice, stridor, trismus (unable to open mouth)
ΓÇó	 drooling, neck swelling/twisting, posturing ie sitting forward, neck extension
ΓÇó	 severe neck pain, respiratory distress
ΓÇó	 If any of above, contact MA, admit to start iv antibiotic, secure airway and for
urgent retrieval
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 Get history, including:

ΓÇô
cough, fever, rash

ΓÇô
feeding - normal or reduced intake, difficulty swallowing

ΓÇô
number of times passed urine/wet nappies in last 24 hours

ΓÇô
other symptoms eg diarrhoea, vomiting, headache, malaise, runny nose, red
eyes, hoarse voice, joint pains

ΓÇô
past history - ARF or RHD, immunocompromised, immunisation status
ΓÇó	 Do physical examination, including:

ΓÇô
chest - listen for crackles/wheeze, air entry

ΓÇô
throat/mouth:

ΓÇô
redness, swelling/pus on tonsils

ΓÇô
ulcers/vesicles, strawberry tongue

ΓÇô
greyish or green membrane - may indicate diphtheria

ΓÇô
ears

ΓÇô
palpate neck for enlarged/tender lymph nodes

ΓÇô
look for rash + signs of APSGN, and ARF
ΓÇó	 A runny nose or a hoarse voice is suggestive of a viral infection and does not
require antibiotic therapy.
ΓÇó	 Sore throat in the absence of a runny nose or a hoarse voice is suggestive of a
bacterial infection may need antibiotics treatment.

Sore Throat: Is the Patient at High Risk of ARF (Acute Rheumatic Fever)?
Consider patient at high risk of ARF
if ANY of the following apply:
ΓÇó	 Living in an ARF-endemic area
ΓÇó	 M─üori or Pacific Islander person
ΓÇó	 Existing RHD, or history of ARF/
RHD and aged &lt; 40 years
Important Notes
ΓÇó	 If on secondary prophylaxis for ARF and needs more antibiotics, confirm last
dose.
ΓÇó	 If &lt; 7 days since last dose, do not give extra dose.
ΓÇó	 The next secondary dose is due in 28 days unless otherwise reset.
Symptomatic Treatment
ΓÇó	 Use paracetamol or ibuprofen for relief of pain and fever
ΓÇó	 Throat lozenges may be helpful for adolescents and adults
ΓÇó	 Encourage adequate fluid intake ΓÇô prefer water, avoid sugary drinks
ΓÇó	 Advise to rest and avoid exposure to smoke
Also Consider High Risk If:
ΓÇó	 Family or household has recent
ARF/RHD Overcrowding (ΓëÑ
2 people/bedroom) or low
socioeconomic status
ΓÇó	 Migrant/refugee from a low- or
middle-income country
ΓÇó	 Give 1 dose of IM benzathine
benzylpenicillin (Bicillin L-A┬«)
ΓÇó	 If IM not possible, give oral
phenoxymethylpenicillin and
explain the importance of
completing the full course
ΓÇó	 Mild allergy (e.g. rash): Give
cefalexin
ΓÇó	 Severe allergy (e.g. anaphylaxis):
Give azithromycin
ΓÇó	 Do a throat swab (MCS) first
ΓÇó	 Give antibiotics immediately
Antibiotic Selection
Do NOT give antibiotics routinely
ΓÇó	 If symptoms suggest Strep A sore
throat/tonsillitis:

ΓÇô

PoCT)

ΓÇô
Review next day or earlier if
concerned (e.g. new symptoms,
rigors, dehydration)

ΓÇô
If not improving in 3ΓÇô7 days and
swab confirms Strep A, consider
phenoxymethylpenicillin

Also commonly known as acute rhinosinusitis is a symptomatic inflammation of the

acute onset confusion

ΓÇô
swelling or cellulitis around eyes, forehead or face

ΓÇô

fever, cough, nasal discharge - colour

ΓÇô
immunocompromised

any facial swelling + tenderness with gentle palpation

ΓÇô

Allergic rhinitis - Adult/Child
Is an immunoglobulin E (IgE)-mediated inflammatory nasal condition resulting from
allergen exposure in a sensitised individual, often associated with atopic dermatitis
(eczema) and asthma often begin in childhood and young adult.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Nasal pruritus, sneezing, rhinorrhoea, and nasal congestion.
ΓÇó	 Frequently, there is associated palate, throat, ear, and eye itching as well as eye
redness, puffiness, and watery discharge.
2.	 Clinical assessment1
ΓÇó	 Get history, including:
Identifying triggers, noting the onset and duration of symptoms, and assessing
for seasonal variations. It's also important to ask about other allergic conditions
(asthma, eczema), family history of allergies, and response to previous
treatments.
ΓÇó	 Physical examination of the nose is necessary. Findings may include swelling of
the turbinate and nasal mucosa, the presence of clear nasal mucus and/or a pale
nasal mucosa, and nasal crease (a transverse crease resulting from repetitive
nose rubbing and manipulation).
3.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Give antihistamine e.g. Cetirizine, children ΓëÑ6 months of age: 2.5 to 5 mg orally
once daily when required; children ΓëÑ6 years of age and adults: 5-10 mg orally
once daily when required or Promethazine 10-25mg oral 2-3 times a day.
ΓÇó	 If mild to moderate symptoms: intranasal corticosteroid nasal spray:
beclomethasone nasal spray: (50 micrograms/ spray aqueous) children ΓëÑ6 years
of age and adults: 50-100 micrograms (1-2 sprays) in each nostril twice daily.
ΓÇó	 Allergen avoidance: reducing exposure to relevant allergens such as dander, dust
mite, and pollen
ΓÇó	 Nasal saline irrigation twice a day or can use sterile water 500mls add with 1
teaspoon of salt and ┬╜ teaspoon of baking sodaΓÇªcan use the syringe to flush
each nostril. (Note to keep your solution clean and safe avoid contamination.)
4.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 In 2-4 weeks and find out how well the symptoms are controlled.
5.	 Referral/Consultation
ΓÇó	 If patient symptoms are severe and not respond to drug therapy.

Problems of Thyroid Grand - Adult
Thyroid gland problems encompass range of disorders that affect the thyroid's
ability to produce hormones, leading to either an overactive (hyperthyroidism)
or underactive (hypothyroidism) state. These conditions can manifest in various
symptoms and may result from various causes, including autoimmune diseases,
iodine deficiency, or thyroiditis.

ΓÇô
baby teeth - smaller, lighter in colour

ΓÇô
a child > 5 years may have a mixture of adult and baby teeth, can be hard to tell
ΓÇó	 Do NOT replant a knocked out baby tooth - can damage the developing adult
tooth
ΓÇó	 Replant permanent (adult) tooth within 15 minutes if possible - more likely to heal:

ΓÇô
if done > 1 hour, unlikely to succeed
ΓÇó	 Check tetanus vaccination status and give booster if indicated. See Tetanus
immunisation.
How to replant a knocked out adult tooth1
ΓÇó	 Hold tooth carefully by the top (crown), not the root
ΓÇó	 Check the tooth is intact - sometimes the root may have broken off:

ΓÇô
if suspect it is not intact, consider urgent dental review
ΓÇó	 If dirty, rinse tooth briefly with dairy milk or sodium chloride o.9%:

ΓÇô
do NOT use water. Do not rub or scrub
ΓÇó	 Replace tooth in the socket with firm finger pressure:

ΓÇô
be careful to ensure the tooth is placed the right way around. Take extra care
if multiple teeth lost
ΓÇó	 Use Temporary splint to hold in place (see below)
ΓÇó	 Advise:

ΓÇô
soft diet

ΓÇô
use chlorhexidine gluconate 0.2% mouthwash. See Gum disease
ΓÇó	 ALL re-implanted teeth need urgent review and treatment by dentist:

ΓÇô
arrange evacuation as needed
Temporary splint1
ΓÇó	 Fix the replanted tooth to the adjacent teeth:

ΓÇô
use a small piece of folded aluminium foil or malleable material eg Blu Tack┬«,
orthodontic wax

ΓÇô
put firmly over replanted tooth and teeth either side, so adjacent teeth act as
a splint. Ask the patient to bite down to hold in position
ΓÇó	 Start antibiotics after replanting the tooth:1

ΓÇô
doxycycline if not allergic and 8 years of age OR

ΓÇô
amoxicillin if not allergic for child &lt; 8 years, or if doxycycline allergy in adult:
	ΓÇ║
if allergic to amoxicillin, consult MO/dentist
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ349

If unable to replant a knocked out tooth1
ΓÇó	 Do not let the tooth dry out
ΓÇó	 Store in dairy milk - cool or room temperature. Tooth ligament cells can survive in
milk up to 6 hours
ΓÇó	 If milk not available, store in sodium chloride 0.9% or saliva + plastic wrap - get
patient to spit
ΓÇó	 saliva into wrap first. Ligament cells can survive up to 1 hour if stored this way
ΓÇó	 Do NOT store in water
ΓÇó	 Consult dentist urgently
Displaced tooth1 - tooth is still in the socket, but moved position
ΓÇó	 Do not reposition a baby tooth
ΓÇó	 Reposition adult tooth to original position with !rm !nger pressure
ΓÇó	 Put Temporary splint on tooth
ΓÇó	 Consult dentist urgently - arrange evacuation as needed
Broken tooth/teeth3
ΓÇó	 Keep tooth fragments, as dentist may be able to be re-bond onto the broken
tooth
ΓÇó	 If no pain from broken tooth, treatment is not urgent - refer to the next dental
clinic
ΓÇó	 If pain, dentine or pulp may have been exposed
ΓÇó	 Exposure of dentine:

ΓÇô
intermittent pain when exposed to stimulus - hot, cold or sweet food or drinks

ΓÇô
cover any obvious cavity with orthodontic wax or other inert material eg Blu
Tack┬« or temporary

ΓÇô
filling eg Cavit┬«2- note: will not last very long

ΓÇô
analgesia not normally needed

ΓÇô
advise to see dentist as soon as possible

ΓÇô
avoid stimulus eg hot/cold drinks
ΓÇó	 Exposure of pulp:

ΓÇô
red soft tissue is seen in the area of break or cavity

ΓÇô
severe pain persisting as a dull throbbing ache - even after removal of the
stimulus

ΓÇô
very sensitive to touch, may bleed or have a blood clot over it
ΓÇó	 needs urgent dental treatment:

ΓÇô
within 24 hours to avoid infection/more damage

ΓÇô
arrange evacuation as needed
ΓÇó	 if delay to treatment expected, consult dentist who may advise:

ΓÇô
orthodontic wax or other inert material eg Blu Tack┬«, to cover the broken
tooth to reduce pain

ΓÇô
OR temporary filling eg Cavit┬«

ΓÇô
note: these measures may still not be successful in reducing pain adequately.
Consult dentist

May present with2

if not effective, consider (adult)

Dental caries - Adult/Child
Tooth decay
Background
ΓÇó	 People from rural and remote areas are at high risk of dental caries1
ΓÇó	 Application of fluoride varnish 2ΓÇô4 times a year to primary (baby) + permanent
(adult) teeth is associated with a substantial reduction in the extent of caries
experienced2
1.
May present with2
ΓÇó	 Tooth/teeth sensitive to hot/cold, biting or pressure
ΓÇó	 Dental caries (tooth decay) - hole in tooth, broken down tooth, darkened tooth
ΓÇó	 Risk factors for ' risk of dental caries1 - see the Chronic conditions manual
www.health.qld.gov.au/rrcsu/clinical-manuals/chronic-conditions-manual-ccm
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable
3.	 Clinical assessment3
ΓÇó	 Ask when last dental visit was
ΓÇó	 Assess for risk factors for dental caries
ΓÇó	 Examine teeth for dental caries:

ΓÇô
holes/cavities or structural damage which can be brown or black in
appearance

ΓÇô
tooth/teeth surfaces with a white or frosty appearance may indicate early
stages of decay

ΓÇô
pain or sensitivity

ΓÇô
bad breath or a bad taste in the mouth
356ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management3
ΓÇó	 If toothache, see Toothache.
ΓÇó	 If symptomatic and caries- Dental Extraction by MA
ΓÇó	 Ask about any adverse experience associated with previous fluoride varnish
application
ΓÇó	 Offer to apply fluoride varnish to teeth if:

ΓÇô
there is evidence of dental caries or person is at high risk of dental caries +

ΓÇô
regular brushing with fluoride toothpaste is likely to be ine ective +

ΓÇô
person is > 18 months old +

ΓÇô
there are no contraindications
ΓÇó	 Refer for dentist review
ΓÇó	 If patient has been recalled for re-application of fluoride varnish:

ΓÇô
assess for any changes in risk status

ΓÇô
check if patient has had fluoride varnish applied anywhere else during the
recall period eg by a dentist

ΓÇô
reapply as indicated
ΓÇó	 Reinforce good oral hygiene. See the Chronic conditions manual
www.health.qld.gov.au/rrcsu/clinical-manuals/chronic-conditions-manual-ccm

	ΓÇ║
poor fitting dentures/sharp or broken teeth
	ΓÇ║
use of topical agents in mouth, oral rinses

ΓÇô
pain or pins and needles/tingling sensation(s) in mouth or face

dry mouth - is cause known eg medicine side effect

ΓÇô
fever

ΓÇô
ulcers anywhere else on body

previous ulcer(s)

ΓÇô

	ΓÇ║
any pigmented lesions on the ulcer

if needed, paracetamol or ibuprofen may help. See Acute pain.

Dry Socket - Adult/Child
Background1
ΓÇó	 Dry socket (alveolar osteitis) is when a blood clot disintegrates prematurely after
a tooth/teeth extraction resulting in inflammation of the bone below
1.
May present with1
ΓÇó	 Severe pain ┬▒ bad breath 1-4 days post tooth extraction
2.	 Immediate management Not applicable

ΓÇó	 Ask about:

ΓÇô
when tooth extraction occurred, pain

does not require removal later. Note: do not use if allergic to iodine

if not available contact MO/dentist for further advice
ΓÇó	 Should heal spontaneously within 2-3 weeks
ΓÇó	 If pain persists > 3 weeks, or signs (eg pain, redness) outside of the tooth socket,
consult MO/Dentist
ΓÇó	 dentist for review of diagnosis
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Advise patient to be reviewed daily initially. Redress the socket as needed
ΓÇó	 Consult MO/dentist if not improving
ΓÇó	 Refer for next dentist clinic visit
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Consult MO/dentist as above. Consider telehealth consult with dentist

Oral facial fractures (Kiribati Original Protocol)
Purpose:
To assist Medical Assistants and Nurses in the primary care setting to recognize,
assess, provide initial management, and appropriately refer patients presenting with
oral facial fractures.
Background
Facial fractures are increasingly common presentations in primary healthcare.
Prompt recognition and appropriate management are critical to prevent permanent
functional, cosmetic, or vision-threatening complications.
General Principles
ΓÇó	 Follow Advanced Trauma Life Support (ATLS) protocols for all trauma cases.
ΓÇó	 Conduct a full visual assessment in any facial trauma.
ΓÇó	 Identify red flags requiring immediate referral.
ΓÇó	 Provide early pain management and wound care as appropriate.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Nasal Fractures: Often resulting from interpersonal violence, nasal fractures
are the most common facial fractures, accounting for up to 58.6% of all facial
fractures. Symptoms include nasal deformity, epistaxis, and periorbital bruising.
www.racgp.org.au/afp/2012/april/maxillofacial-trauma/?utm_source=chatgpt.
com
ΓÇó	 Mandibular Fractures: These may present with pain, swelling, malocclusion, and
limited mouth opening. They can result from blunt or penetrating trauma and
may destabilize the airway. www.entnet.org/wp-content/uploads/files/Trauma-
Chapter-5.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com
ΓÇó	 Zygomatic Fractures: Symptoms include periorbital swelling, infraorbital nerve
paraesthesia, and restricted eye movement.
ΓÇó	 Orbital Fractures: Signs include diplopia, reduced visual acuity, and orbital rim
tenderness.
ΓÇó	 Frontal Bone Fractures: Present with forehead deformity, possible CSF
rhinorrhoea, and periorbital bruising.
372ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Airway Management: Ensure airway patency, especially in cases of mandibular
fractures that may compromise the airway. https://link.springer.com/
chapter/10.1007/978-981-15-1346-6_48?utm_source=chatgpt.com
ΓÇó	 Hemostasis: Control bleeding using direct pressure, nasal packing, or hemostatic
agents as appropriate.
ΓÇó	 Analgesia: Administer appropriate pain relief, considering the severity of the
injury.
ΓÇó	 Antibiotics: Prophylactic antibiotics may be indicated in cases of open fractures
or significant soft tissue involvement. https://tsaco.bmj.com/content/9/1/
e001304?utm_source=chatgpt.com
ΓÇó	 Imaging: Obtain appropriate imaging studies, such as X-rays or CT scans, to
assess the extent of the fractures.
Vital Symptoms/Signs Not to Miss
These require IMMEDIATE REFERRAL:
SYMPTOM/SIGN
CONDITION
ACTION
Proptosis, chemosis,
ophthalmoplegia, vision loss
Retrobulbar
haemorrhage
Immediate emergency
referral
Sudden visual loss or
decreased pupil reflex
Traumatic optic
neuropathy
Immediate emergency
referral
Diplopia, restricted upward
gaze, nausea/vomiting
(especially in young patients)
White eye blowout
fracture
Immediate emergency
referral
Mobile maxilla, malocclusion
Maxillary/midface
fracture
Immediate referral
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 History: Obtain details of the mechanism of injury, time of injury, and any
associated symptoms.
ΓÇó	 Examination: Perform a thorough head and neck examination, assessing for
deformities, swelling, tenderness, and neurological deficits.
ΓÇó	 Functional Assessment: Evaluate the patient's ability to open and close the
mouth, chew, and speak.
ΓÇó	 Neurological Examination: Assess cranial nerve function, particularly the
trigeminal nerve, for sensory deficits.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ373

4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Conservative Management: Indicated for non-displaced fractures without
significant functional impairment. Includes analgesia, soft diet, and follow-up.
https://www.rch.org.au/trauma-service/manual/maxillofacial-injury/?utm_
source=chatgpt.com
ΓÇó	 Surgical Intervention: Required for displaced fractures, fractures with
airway compromise, or those causing significant functional impairment.
https://www.slideshare.net/slideshow/maxillofacial-trauma-19339123/19339123
ΓÇó	 Referral: Refer to oral and maxillofacial specialists for definitive management.
https://metronorth.health.qld.gov.au/specialist_service/refer-your-patient/oral-
maxillo-facial/facial-fractures-lacerations?utm_source=chatgpt.com
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Monitoring: Regular follow-up to assess healing, manage complications, and
address any functional issues.
ΓÇó	 Rehabilitation: May include physiotherapy for jaw mobility and speech therapy if
necessary.
ΓÇó	 Psychological Support: Provide support for patients dealing with the
psychological impact of facial trauma.
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Urgent Referral: For patients with airway compromise, significant bleeding, or
complex fractures.
ΓÇó	 Routine Referral: For non-displaced fractures without functional impairment.
ΓÇó	 Specialist Consultation: Consult with oral and maxillofacial surgeons for
definitive management. https://www.westernhealth.org.au/HealthProfessionals/
Referrals/Documents/ASC%20Access%20and%20Referral%20Guideline_Oral%20
Maxillofacial%20Surgery.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com
374ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

Odontogenic infection and spread to neck
spaces (Kiribati Original Protocol)
Background
Odontogenic infections arise from dental or oral pathology and can lead to serious
systemic complications. If left untreated, these infections can spread to adjacent
tissues, including the neck spaces, leading to severe conditions such as LudwigΓÇÖs
Angina. Early detection and prompt management are essential to prevent airway
compromise, sepsis, and other life-threatening consequences.
General Principles
ΓÇó	 Follow standard infection control protocols to prevent cross-contamination and
ensure patient safety.
ΓÇó	 Recognize red flags and symptoms that require urgent referral.
ΓÇó	 Provide early pain management and antibiotics as indicated.
ΓÇó	 Always ensure airway management is prioritized, especially if neck space
involvement is suspected.
ΓÇó	 Conduct a full assessment and monitor for any signs of systemic infection or
airway compromise.
1.
May present with
Odontogenic infections may present with the following symptoms, which could
indicate spread to the neck spaces:
ΓÇó	 Swelling and erythema of the lower face, neck, or jaw.
ΓÇó	 Severe pain in the affected area.
ΓÇó	 Difficulty swallowing (dysphagia) or painful swallowing.
ΓÇó	 Trismus (inability to open the mouth fully).
ΓÇó	 Fever and chills suggesting systemic infection.
ΓÇó	 Difficulty breathing or hoarseness (suggesting airway compromise).
ΓÇó	 Swelling of the tongue or floor of the mouth.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ375

2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Airway Management: Always assess the airway first. In cases of LudwigΓÇÖs Angina,
where airway obstruction is a risk, early intervention to secure the airway is
critical. Consider intubation or emergency surgical intervention if necessary.
ΓÇó	 Analgesia: Administer appropriate pain relief, considering the severity of the
infection and the patientΓÇÖs overall condition. Opioid analgesics may be required
for severe pain.
ΓÇó	 Antibiotics: Broad-spectrum intravenous antibiotics should be administered
early. Common regimens include a combination of penicillin, metronidazole, or
clindamycin to cover both aerobic and anaerobic bacteria.
ΓÇó	 Hydration and Fluid Management: Administer IV fluids as necessary to maintain
hydration and support systemic circulation.
ΓÇó	 Monitoring: Close monitoring of vital signs, including temperature, pulse, and
oxygen saturation, is essential to detect signs of sepsis or airway compromise
early.
Vital Symptoms/Signs Not to Miss
These symptoms require IMMEDIATE REFERRAL to a higher level of care or
emergency intervention:
SYMPTOM/SIGN
CONDITION
ACTION
Difficulty breathing, stridor,
or hoarseness
Airway compromise
Immediate emergency
referral
Severe swelling of the neck,
dysphagia
LudwigΓÇÖs Angina
Immediate referral for
airway management
Fever with worsening
symptoms
Sepsis or systemic
infection
Immediate referral
for antibiotics and
management
Increased pain and swelling
unresponsive to analgesics
Spread of infection to
deep spaces
Immediate referral for
surgical consultation
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 History: Obtain a detailed history of the symptoms, including the onset and
progression of pain, fever, and swelling. Ask about recent dental procedures or
infections.
ΓÇó	 Examination: Perform a thorough examination of the oral cavity, including
palpation of the floor of the mouth, submandibular regions, and neck. Assess for
signs of swelling, erythema, and tenderness.
ΓÇó	 Neurological Examination: Check for signs of infection affecting the cranial
nerves, particularly the glossopharyngeal and vagus nerves, which could signal
involvement of deep neck spaces.
ΓÇó	 Functional Assessment: Evaluate the patient's ability to open the mouth,
swallow, and breathe comfortably. Difficulty opening the mouth or swallowing
could be an indication of severe infection or airway compromise.
376ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 Conservative Management: For mild odontogenic infections, oral antibiotics and
analgesics may be sufficient. Follow up with dental or oral health specialists as
necessary.
ΓÇó	 Surgical Intervention: Surgical drainage may be necessary for deep infections or
when there is abscess formation. In severe cases, ludwig's angina or deep neck
infections may require neck exploration or debridement.
ΓÇó	 Referral:

ΓÇô
Refer patients with signs of airway compromise or severe systemic infection to
the emergency department for immediate intervention.

ΓÇô
Refer to an oral and maxillofacial specialist or ENT surgeon for definitive
management, especially if the infection has spread to deep neck spaces.
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Monitoring: Patients should be closely monitored for any signs of worsening
infection or airway obstruction. If the patient requires hospitalization, frequent
reassessment is necessary.
ΓÇó	 Rehabilitation: In severe cases, follow-up may include physical therapy for jaw
mobility or speech therapy, depending on the extent of the infection and its
impact on the oral and pharyngeal structures.
ΓÇó	 Psychological Support: Provide psychological support for patients dealing with
the stress or anxiety of managing a potentially life-threatening condition.
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Urgent Referral: For patients with airway compromise, signs of sepsis, or those
who require surgical drainage or debridement.
ΓÇó	 Routine Referral: For patients with early-stage odontogenic infections or those
not showing signs of severe systemic involvement.
ΓÇó	 Specialist Consultation: Consult with an oral and maxillofacial surgeon, ENT
specialist, or infectious disease expert as necessary.
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ377

Trigeminal neuralgia ΓÇô Adult/Child
(Kiribati Original Protocol)
Background
Trigeminal neuralgia is a chronic pain condition involving the trigeminal nerve (5th
cranial nerve).
ΓÇó	 It causes sudden, severe, sharp, or electric shock-like pain on one side of the face.
ΓÇó	 Often triggered by light touch, chewing, talking, wind or brushing teeth.
ΓÇó	 May be idiopathic or due to compression, multiple sclerosis (MS), post-herpetic
neuralgia, or rarely a tumour.
ΓÇó	 In resource-limited settings, clinical diagnosis and first-line treatment should be
initiated before considering imaging.
ΓÇó	 See also:

ΓÇô
Better Health Channel ΓÇô Trigeminal Neuralgia www.betterhealth.vic.gov.au/
health/conditionsandtreatments/trigeminal-neuralgia

ΓÇô
https://www.ninds.nih.gov/health-information/disorders/trigeminal-neuralgia
Recommend
ΓÇó	 Always discuss with Medical Officer (MO) if trigeminal neuralgia is suspected.
This condition is rare in children ΓÇô consider alternative diagnoses in young
patients.
ΓÇó	 Early treatment improves quality of life and reduces the risk of persistent pain
and weight loss.
ΓÇó	 If recurrent or resistant to treatment, refer to visiting neurologist or dental
surgeon if available.
1.
May present with
ΓÇó	 Severe, sharp, stabbing or electric-shock pain in the cheek, jaw, teeth, or
forehead.
ΓÇó	 Pain occurs on one side only.
ΓÇó	 Attacks last from a few seconds to 2 minutes, often in clusters.
ΓÇó	 Pain may be triggered by:

ΓÇô
Chewing, swallowing, brushing teeth

ΓÇô
Talking, touching the face, cold wind
ΓÇó	 No fever, swelling, rash or pus
ΓÇó	 No facial numbness or weakness (if present ΓåÆ consider another diagnosis)
ΓÇó	 Atypical or persistent pain, numbness or bilateral symptoms may suggest
another condition (e.g. dental abscess, shingles, sinusitis, MS, tumour).
378ΓÇâ|ΓÇâKiribati Primary Clinical Care Manual

2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Reassure patient and explain condition.
ΓÇó	 Start first-line medication only after MO review.
ΓÇó	 Avoid known triggers (e.g. cold wind, chewing on affected side).
ΓÇó	 Advise to return for review if:

ΓÇô
Symptoms worsen or do not improve in 1ΓÇô2 weeks

ΓÇô
Facial numbness, weakness, or persistent pain occurs
ΓÇó	 Do not attempt dental extraction unless clear cause identified.
ΓÇó	 If available, refer to dental or visiting specialist clinic.
3.	 Clinical assessment
ΓÇó	 History:

ΓÇô
When did pain start? How long does each episode last?

ΓÇô
Which part of the face is affected?

ΓÇô
Any known triggers?

ΓÇô
Pattern: how often, how intense?

ΓÇô
Previous similar episodes?

ΓÇô
Recent dental problems or facial infections?
ΓÇó	 Examination:

ΓÇô
Check vital signs

ΓÇô
Inspect skin for rash or swelling (exclude herpes zoster)

ΓÇô
Check cranial nerves: any weakness, droop, numbness?

ΓÇô
Examine teeth and gums

ΓÇô
Palpate face for tenderness or masses
ΓÇó	 If neurological signs or persistent pain ΓåÆ MO may refer for imaging (if available)
4.	 Management
ΓÇó	 First-line medication (after MO review):

ΓÇô
Carbamazepine
	ΓÇ║
Form: Tablet (100 mg)
	ΓÇ║
Strength: 100 mg
	ΓÇ║
Route: Oral
	ΓÇ║
Dose: Start 100 mg twice daily; increase slowly every 3ΓÇô7 days
	ΓÇ║
Maximum: Usually 400ΓÇô1200 mg/day in 2ΓÇô3 divided doses
	ΓÇ║
Duration: Review after 2 weeks
	ΓÇ║
Contraindications: Hypersensitivity, bone marrow suppression
	ΓÇ║
Safety notes: Monitor for drowsiness, dizziness, rash, low sodium, low white
cell count. Do not stop suddenly.
	ΓÇ║
Blood tests (if available): FBC, electrolytes, LFTs prior and during treatment
Kiribati Primary Clinical Care ManualΓÇâ|ΓÇâ379

ΓÇó	 If carbamazepine is not tolerated: MO may consider, or amitriptyline if available
ΓÇó	 Non-medication care

ΓÇô
Soft diet, avoid cold wind, cover face in wind

ΓÇô
Offer counselling for chronic pain or mental health impact

ΓÇô
Monitor for weight loss or sleep disruption
5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Review within 1ΓÇô2 weeks to assess symptom control and side effects
ΓÇó	 If pain is well controlled, continue same dose
ΓÇó	 If no improvement or poor tolerance, discuss with MO
ΓÇó	 Ongoing review if long-term therapy is needed
ΓÇó	 Encourage return for reassessment if:

ΓÇô
Pain worsens

ΓÇô
New neurological symptoms develop

ΓÇô
Unable to eat or sleep

ΓÇô
Facial numbness or weakness appears
6.	 Referral/consultation
ΓÇó	 Refer to MO or specialist if:

ΓÇô
Diagnosis uncertain or persistent pain

ΓÇô
Numbness or facial weakness present

ΓÇô
Recurrent episodes not responding to treatment
ΓÇó	 If available, refer to:

ΓÇô
Dental services if suspect dental origin

ΓÇô
ENT or neurology specialist (if visiting or via outreach)

ΓÇô
Consider telehealth if imaging or specialist opinion needed
ΓÇó	 If suspected secondary cause (MS, tumour, shingles) ΓåÆ refer to hospital

Jaw dislocation ΓÇô Adult/Child
Background12
Jaw dislocation most commonly involves anterior displacement of the mandible
at the temporomandibular joint (TMJ), typically caused by trauma or excessive
mouth opening (e.g. yawning, dental procedures). It may be unilateral or bilateral.
Prompt recognition and reduction are essential to relieve pain and restore function.
Inappropriate handling can worsen the injury or delay recovery. Dislocations may
recur in individuals with underlying joint laxity or structural abnormalities.
Recommend12
ΓÇó	 Jaw dislocation is typically an anterior displacement of the mandible.
ΓÇó	 Prompt reduction is important to relieve pain, restore function, and prevent
complications.
ΓÇó	 Avoid repeated manipulation ΓÇô refer early if uncertain.
1.
May present with12
ΓÇó	 History of trauma, yawning, laughing, or wide mouth opening
ΓÇó	 Pain in front of ear(s) or jaw
ΓÇó	 Inability to close mouth
ΓÇó	 Jaw appears displaced forward, difficulty speaking
ΓÇó	 Drooling, anxiety, or distress
2.	 Immediate management
ΓÇó	 Reassure patient and support jaw with gentle pressure
ΓÇó	 Avoid forcing jaw movement
ΓÇó	 Place patient in sitting or semi-reclined position
ΓÇó	 Provide analgesia and refer promptly if in distress or reduction not possible
3.	 Clinical assessment123
ΓÇó	 Take history: mechanism of injury, previous dislocations, recent dental procedures
ΓÇó	 Check for:

ΓÇô
Symmetry of jaw

ΓÇô
Palpable depression in preauricular area

ΓÇô
Inability to occlude teeth

ΓÇô
Associated injuries (e.g. head, cervical spine)
ΓÇó	 Assess for signs of fracture or nerve injury (numbness, weakness)
ΓÇó	 Examine ears and oral cavity
ΓÇó	 Assess airway and ability to swallow/speak

5.	 Follow up
ΓÇó	 Avoid wide mouth opening for 2 weeks (e.g. yawning, large bites)
ΓÇó	 Soft diet for several days
ΓÇó	 Educate patient on recurrence risk
ΓÇó	 Monitor for signs of fracture, nerve damage, or joint dysfunction
ΓÇó	 Refer for imaging or specialist if:

ΓÇô
Recurrent dislocations

ΓÇô
Suspected fracture

ΓÇô
Reduction unsuccessful
6.	 Referral/consultation
Refer urgently if:
ΓÇó	 Unable to reduce or unsure of diagnosis
ΓÇó	 Suspected mandibular fracture
ΓÇó	 Associated head injury or airway compromise
ΓÇó	 Dislocation complicated by seizure, trauma, or high-risk comorbidity
MHMS to advise if ongoing instability or access to imaging/specialist is required.

Eye problems

Eye assessment ΓÇô Adult/Child
Understanding eye anatomy is crucial for public health nurses as it enables them
to assess, identify, and address potential eye and vision problems contributing to
overall patient well-being and preventing vision loss.
EYE TIPS1
DO - WHEN INDICATED
DON'T

it

injury is obvious
ΓÇó	 Advise not to drive with eye padded
as depth perception may be altered
ΓÇó	 Give local anaesthetic eye drops to
take home
ΓÇó	 Try to remove protruding foreign
body (FB)
ΓÇó	 Put drops or ointment in an eye that
has suffered an obvious rupture or
penetrating injury
ΓÇó	 Use steroid eye drops or double
pad an eye, unless advised by MO/
ophthalmologist
ΓÇó	 Routinely apply eye pad
1.

ΓÇó	 Red, painful eye(s)
ΓÇó	 Changes to vision, photophobia, blurred vision
ΓÇó	 Discharge, tearing
ΓÇó	 Feeling like something in eye (Foreign body sensation)

Eye examination
ΓÇó	 If a red, swollen, tender eyelid + febrile assume Orbital/periorbital cellulitis.
ΓÇó	 Examine systematically
ΓÇó	 Measure VA
ΓÇó	 Do visual acuity (VA)

ΓÇô
note: 3/3 or 6/6 VA does not exclude a serious condition. ΓåôVA may be long-
standing or due to uncorrected refractive error

ΓÇô
repeat VA using a multiple-pinhole occluder if available. If vision improves it
likely means a VA issue that can be corrected with glasses, and less likely an
acute issue
ΓÇó	 Use ophthalmoscope set to +10D and held at 10 cm from eye or bright pen light 5
ΓÇó	 Check both eyes without putting pressure on the eye or forcing the lids open,
including:4

ΓÇô
eyelashes - any in-turned, crusted discharge

ΓÇô
eyelids - do they fully close, any lacerations, swelling, redness, bruising

ΓÇô
lower eyelid - colour of conjunctiva

ΓÇô
sclera - bleeding, FB

ΓÇô
cornea - clear or hazy, FB, white spot1

ΓÇô
anterior chamber - any blood (hyphaema) or pus (hypopyon)

ΓÇô

	ΓÇ║
shape + reaction to light (do they react equally)
	ΓÇ║
red reflex for any dimming, any cloudiness
	ΓÇ║
any asymmetry in the position of and brightness of the corneal reflex

ΓÇô
eye movements:
	ΓÇ║
ask patient to look up, down, side to side - any pain
	ΓÇ║
any restriction. If restricted upward movement consider orbital fracture in
blunt injury
	ΓÇ║
any double vision. To confirm if it is true double vision (and not just blur/
ghosting), cover eye. If double vision disappears, it is not true double vision
ΓÇó	 If eye swollen closed, gently lift lid to check for injuries + pupil size and reaction
to light

ΓÇó	 Always evert upper eyelid if red eye, sensation of FB, or grittiness:

ΓÇô
check for/exclude retained FB

ΓÇô
+ look for redness or small round bumps - may indicate conjunctivitis
(bacterial, viral, allergic)
ΓÇó	 IF SKILLED ONLY

ΓÇô
Stain with fluorescein to help identify a FB or corneal defect eg abrasion

ΓÇô
Measure intraocular pressure (IOP) if tonometer available and skilled
Visual acuity (VA)
ΓÇó	 Use Snellen or tumbling E chart positioned at the specified distance eg 3 or 6
metres, from the patient in a well lit area:10

ΓÇô
test one eye at a time

ΓÇô
ask patient to cover one eye with their palm and read or point in the direction
of the E's, from the top of the chart left to right

ΓÇô
record VA as 2 numbers. Top number is the distance from the chart, bottom
number is the smallest line of letters/Es that can be read without mistakes eg
3/3, 6/9
ΓÇó	 If cannot read the top letter at 3 or 6 metres:10

ΓÇô
hold up your fingers at varying distances eg 5 metres, 4 metres etc and record
vision as counting fingers (CF) (the maximum distance they can see between
5 and 1 metre eg VA CF 5 m)
ΓÇó	 If cannot see to count fingers, check if they can see hand movements or have
perception of light by shining a torch into the eye at 10 cm5
Fluorescein examination of cornea (eye drops or strips)
ΓÇó	 Avoid if obvious or high likelihood of globe rupture or penetrating injury
ΓÇó	 If red eye(s) use separate strips/tissues for each eye1
ΓÇó	 Fluorescein eye drops - use smallest amount possible, too much will flood the
eye and a defect may be missed.4 Gently dab the closed eye with a tissue to
remove excess
ΓÇó	 Fluorescein strips - gently touch the strip to the inside of the lower eyelid,
pre-moisten with saline if dry eye. Repeat if more dye is needed
ΓÇó	 Ask the patient to blink to spread the dye
ΓÇó	 Dye will pool or 'uptake' (stain) in damaged areas of cornea
ΓÇó	 In normal light uptake will look yellow. With blue light of ophthalmoscope
uptake will be green:11

ΓÇô
multiple pinpoint uptake - may be dry eye

ΓÇô
sharp, well-defined border - may be abrasion(s)

ΓÇô
round with fuzzy edges, branching (dendritic) pattern - may be herpes
simplex virus

ΓÇô
vertical lines on upper cornea - may be retained FB in eyelid

Removal of foreign body2,3
ΓÇó	 Lie patient down, with affected eye closest to you
ΓÇó	 Ask patient to look up, approach from side to Γåô blinking. Hold eyelid open if
needed2
ΓÇó	 First try to irrigate with sodium chloride 0.9% (use a syringe without needle) FB
may wash off2
ΓÇó	 Or, try to dislodge with a moistened cotton tip applicator using ΓÇÿdab' or 'nudging'
motion2
ΓÇó	 If still not dislodged, MO may consider removal from cornea with 25 G needle
(best done with slit lamp)4
ΓÇó	 Consider instilling oxybuprocaine in both eyes to stop blinking to aid removal
ΓÇó	 If unable to remove the FB, or there is a 'rust ring' after removal - consult MO
ΓÇó	 If FB removed from cornea give chloramphenicol as per Corneal abrasion.
ΓÇó	 Advise to wear protective eyewear during risky activities1
